--- Page 1 ---
<meta>This text concludes an academic announcement, following from the previous page's discussion of biblical translations. The catchword "nii" connects to the end of the previous page.</meta>

...nii <note>original: "nii," likely the end of "Germanii" or a similar word from the previous page</note> etc., and the German versions of **Blessed Luther**, **Dietenberger**, and **Piscator**; <note>Johann Dietenberger (1475–1537) was a Catholic translator; Johannes Piscator (1546–1625) was a Reformed theologian.</note> as well as the Dutch, Danish, English, French, Italian, and Spanish versions. Finally, from the commentaries—both those of the Hebrews <note>meaning Jewish commentators</note>, especially those who stick more closely to the literal text, and those of the Christians, particularly the lovers of philology <note>original Greek: "Φιλολογάντων"</note>—we shall studiously select those things which serve to illustrate the literal expression <note>original Greek: "τῷ ῥητῷ"</note> and the literal sense of Holy Scripture. All for the greater glory of *GOD*. Furthermore, we desire that those who are not displeased by these studies and require our assistance be dutifully and kindly requested to continue attending as frequently as they have done until now. We shall labor diligently so that, according to our modest talent received not without God’s help <note>original Greek: "οὐκ ἀθεεὶ"</note>, we may fulfill the requirements of our office; and if perhaps we cannot please in any other way, let us at least please through a welcome brevity, since those Cretans seem honorable to us, of whom Plato says they practiced "depth of thought rather than many words" <note>original Greek: "πολύννοιαν μᾶλλον ἢ πολυλογίαν"</note>. We now humbly venerate the Holy Spirit, that He may deign to breathe the wind of His grace from on high upon our labors, pour His gifts upon us, and mercifully grant "a mouth and wisdom" <note>original Greek: "στόμα ἀτάρ σοφίαν"</note>. With Him giving, "every difficult thing becomes achievable" <note>original Greek: "ἅπαν ἄπορον πόριμον"</note> for us. The lectures, with the good hand of God <note>original Greek: "σὺν ἀγαθῇ θεῦ παλάμᾳ"</note>, will begin next Thursday, as it is called, at one o'clock in the afternoon in the 
->Smaller Auditorium. Published on the second day<-
->of Pentecost in the Year of the Christian Era<-
->1668.<-

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Duke Gustavus Adolphus. The author moves from the list of titles on the previous page to a humble justification for his work, using a "modesty topos"—a common literary device where an author compares their meager skills to great figures of the past to show humility before a powerful patron. The catchword at the bottom of the previous page was "Purum" (Pure), which likely begins the next page's description of the Prince's character.</meta>

# MOST SERENE AND MOST HIGH
## PRINCE,
### MOST MERCIFUL LORD,

Since Saint Jerome <note>Hieronymus (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father and the primary translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible. He was renowned for his immense learning.</note>—of whom it was said that no one knew what he himself did not know—confessed that regarding his work on **OBADIAH** <note>The shortest book in the Old Testament. Jerome wrote two commentaries on it: one in his youth and one in his old age.</note> he had both toyed with the text as a youth and presumed too much as an old man, he did not hesitate to say: *I was an infant, I did not yet know how to write, my hand faltered, my joints trembled; now, even if I have made no other progress, I at least hold that Socratic principle: I know that I know nothing* <note>original: "Scio, quod nescio." A famous philosophical paradox attributed to Socrates, emphasizing that true wisdom is recognizing the limits of one's own knowledge.</note>; What courage, then, should I have when coming forward into **YOUR** August presence with my own notes on **OBADIAH**, where nothing ought to be brought unless it is refined in genius, elaborate in workmanship, and adorned in words? I can promise none of these things for myself, as I am both aware of and lament my own weakness.

But **YOUR** kindness, **MOST MERCIFUL OF PRINCES**, and the innate moderation of your divine character, raise me up as I tremble; for it is your custom neither to scorn the infancy of those speaking, nor to hate ignorance, but rather to encourage hesitation of your own accord. Relying, therefore, upon that kindness, I most humbly lay this work—whatever its worth—prostrate at **YOUR** feet. The most devoted piety <term>pietas: a combination of religious devotion and dutiful loyalty to one's country or superiors</term> of **YOUR** lowliest little servant could no longer contain itself, and preferred to bear the charge of some rashness or imprudence rather than the crime of an ungrateful soul, or to remain silent about those things which must always be remembered. Indeed, the Christian World knows these things, and all those with a sense of right mind admire them in **YOU**: sincere Piety,

<summary>The author humbles himself before the Duke, citing Saint Jerome’s own admissions of inadequacy when interpreting the Book of Obadiah as a justification for his own modest efforts. He praises the Prince’s known kindness and moderate character as the reason he feels bold enough to present his work.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Jerome, Book of Obadiah, Socratic wisdom, humility, dedication, patronage, piety, Gustavus Adolphus of Mecklenburg</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Duke Gustavus Adolphus. The author is moving from a general expression of humility to a direct praise of the Duke’s character and scholarly patronage. He specifically mentions his own appointment to a school three years prior and a multilingual poem he recently composed for the Duke.</meta>

It is better to revere in silence your pure zeal for Religion, your astounding learning, your guiding prudence, your love for your subjects, your fairness toward all, and your unshakable spirit amidst both prosperity and adversity—a truly **PRINCELY SPIRIT**—than to obscure these virtues with ill-fitting words. Allow me now at least to proclaim **YOUR** most merciful affection for the humanities <note>original: "bonas literas." Literally "good letters," referring to the liberal arts, classical literature, and scholarly pursuits.</note>, which, since **YOU YOURSELF** understand to a miraculous degree and even today deign to lower those hands accustomed to Scepters toward them, you are deservedly called the **MOST LEARNED OF PRINCES and the PRINCE OF THE LEARNED**. You extend that same favor toward all scholars, and among them, toward me, the least of all.

For it did not seem enough to **YOUR SERENITY** <term>Serenity: A formal title of address (Latin: *Serenitas*) used for high-ranking royalty, signifying a calm and clear majesty.</term> to have most mercifully summoned me three years ago to the governance of the School <note>original: "Scholæ regimen." This refers to the author's appointment as a rector or headmaster of a gymnasium or academy, a position granted by the Duke.</note>—perhaps having become known to you through the kind report of others—but you also recently wished to reward my devotion, expressed in a multilingual yet modest poem <note>original: "Polyglotto, sed tenui carmine." A "polyglot" work written in multiple languages (likely including Latin, Greek, and Hebrew), which was a common way for scholars to demonstrate their skill to a patron.</note>, with Royal generosity, and at the same time to give me hope for that future which the prayers of myself and my family strive toward.

Furthermore, since I currently possess nothing besides a barren will to obey, except for this present little book, may the simple devotion of it please **YOUR SERENITY**, I beseech you. Though it can produce nothing worthy of **YOUR AUGUST** height, it nevertheless longs to reveal a perpetual pledge of my devotion. The excellence of the subject matter will somehow compensate for what the smallness of its size denies the book. Not everything that is large is always good, but that which is good is, by that very fact, great. Moreover, it seemed impolite <note>original: "incivile." Lacking in courtly manners or social grace.</note> to thrust massive volumes upon **YOUR SERENITY** whenever you might find a spare moment to devote to reading. If, therefore, **YOUR SERENITY** [looks upon] these first [fruits] with a favorable countenance and...

->)( 3<-

<summary>The author praises Duke Gustavus Adolphus as a "Prince of Scholars" and thanks him for his past patronage, including a school appointment and a reward for a multilingual poem. He offers this small book as a humble token of gratitude, arguing that a small, good work is better than a massive, tedious one.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to Duke Gustavus Adolphus of Mecklenburg-Güstrow. Having previously praised the Duke’s intellect and patronage, the author now offers a series of high-flown blessings and formalizes the date and location of the dedication: Wittenberg, August 2, 1670.</meta>

...has so far admitted my modest services; under His auspices, it will be possible to proceed to greater things. Indeed, I shall reach the ultimate harbor of my desires if it should be my lot to always rejoice in the favor and grace of **SUCH A GREAT PRINCE**—than whom even the most careful prayers could not imagine a better—just as much as I admire his divine gifts.

**GOD GAVE YOU! MAY GOD PRESERVE YOU! AUGUST HERO, THE IDEAL OF PRINCES, THE STAR OF THE PEOPLES, THE DELIGHT OF THE HUMAN RACE! MAY GOD PRESERVE YOU!** May the **PRINCE OF PRINCES** <note>original: "PRINCEPS PRINCIPUM." A reference to God as the King of Kings.</note> most kindly grant to **YOUR SERENITY AND YOUR MOST EXALTED FAMILY A PLEASANT LIFE, A SECURE EMPIRE, A SAFE HOME, A BRAVE ARMY, A FAITHFUL SENATE, AN UPRIGHT PEOPLE, A PEACEFUL REGION, A LONG LIFE, HAPPY DESCENDANTS, AND WHATEVER THE WISHES OF THE BEST PRINCE MIGHT BE! DIVINE PRINCE! MAY GOD PRESERVE YOU!**

Written at Wittenberg, in the Year of the Christian Era 1670 <note>original: "CIↃ IↃC LXX." An older form of Roman numerals where CIↃ represents 1,000 and IↃC represents 600.</note>, on the 4th day before the Nones of August <note>August 2nd. The "Nones" was a marker in the Roman calendar falling on the 5th or 7th of the month.</note>, the day which is sacred to **GUSTAVUS** and brings to memory that **GREAT GUSTAVUS ADOLPHUS** <note>Gustavus Adolphus the Great (1594–1632), the King of Sweden and a hero of the Protestant cause in the Thirty Years' War, for whom the Duke was named.</note>, whose name and virtues **YOUR SERENITY** possesses: May **GOD** add years to your virtues and the fortune and successes of the **GREAT GUSTAVUS**, but, after a long series of years, a more peaceful departure to the heavenly dwellings!

**YOUR SERENITY'S**

-> *The most humble and* <-
-> *obedient servant of all,* <-
-> **AUGUSTUS Pfeiffer.** <-

<summary>Augustus Pfeiffer concludes his dedication to Duke Gustavus Adolphus with a rhythmic series of blessings for the Duke's reign and family. He signs off from Wittenberg in 1670, explicitly linking the Duke's virtues to his famous namesake, the Swedish King Gustavus Adolphus.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page marks the transition from the formal dedication to Duke Gustavus Adolphus to the author’s address to the general reader. Having concluded his high-flown praises of his patron, the author now introduces the specific subject of his scholarship: the Book of Obadiah and the commentary of the Jewish scholar Isaac Abarbanel.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut header featuring a repeating pattern of stylized floral motifs arranged in two horizontal rows.</image-desc>

# TO THE BENEVOLENT READER
## GREETINGS.

I present to you Obadiah, a prophet small in size but great in the weight of his mysteries. St. Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the primary translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note> says of him in his Preface to the Epistle to Philemon that he is so wonderful and grand *that he does not know whether he should marvel more at the brevity of the language or the magnitude of the meanings;* Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar and archbishop known for his encyclopedic writings.</note> moreover, in his book on the *Allegories of Holy Scripture*, *calls him among the prophets shorter in the number of words but equal in the grace of his mysteries;* and Hugh of St. Victor <note>A 12th-century Saxon philosopher and mystic.</note> describes him as *simple in speech and manifold in sense, sparse in words, but rich in thoughts;* finally, Blessed Luther says he is *small in body, greatest in meaning, just as what is more precious is usually stored in smaller vessels.* In addition to his interpretation <term>Exegesis; the critical explanation or interpretation of a text</term> (in which you will see that I have practiced *fullness of thought rather than a multitude of words* <note>original: "πολύνοιαν μᾶλλον ἢ πολυλογίαν ἀσκῆσαι." A Greek phrase emphasizing quality of insight over quantity of speech.</note>), you have here the Commentary of Abarbanel <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a renowned Jewish statesman, philosopher, and biblical commentator.</note> (or Abravanel), translated into Latin by me some fifteen years ago. Four years ago, I revised that version, and when I saw that Abarbanel had selected the strongest arguments from the whole of Jewish vain-talk <note>original: "Matæologia Judaica." A polemical term used by Christian scholars of this era to describe Jewish theological discourse they considered useless or erroneous.</note>, I added a Refutation, and finally those things which might serve the purpose of a proper commentary. Now, it has pleased me to present this same modest work to you once more, but in a more corrected form. To this end, I wanted to list here those things which I thought should be changed or added while re-reading either the Interpretation of the Prophecy or the translation of Abarbanel's Commentary, so that you may restore them or turn them to the better as you please. In the Latin translation of Abarbanel, the following occur: P. 12. line 13. it was translated, *through the whole world*, I would prefer, *in the world*. P. 14. last line: for *submission* substitute *modesty*. P. 24. l. 16. for *they wrote* read *they narrated*. ibid. l. 25. for *this Prophe-*

<vocab>Obadiah, Jerome, Isidore, Hugh of St. Victor, Martin Luther, Isaac Abarbanel, Mataeologia, Exegesis, Commentary</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces his work on the Prophet Obadiah, quoting Church Fathers and Luther to emphasize the book's profound depth despite its short length. He also provides a list of corrections for his previous translation of Isaac Abarbanel’s commentary.</summary>
<keywords>Obadiah, biblical commentary, St. Jerome, Isaac Abarbanel, Martin Luther, translation errata, Hebrew scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page serves as the errata for the work, providing corrections to the preceding translation of the Book of Obadiah and the commentary by Abarbanel. The translator notes minor errors in wording, missing citations, and clarifies geographical references before moving on to the scholarly interpretation (exegesis). The catchword "IN EXA-" at the bottom of the previous page anticipates the section titled "In Exegesi" found here.</meta>

...that he **did not act then** substitute: **did not prophesy formally here.** And on line 26, for **that he was a prophet** restore: **that he can be called a prophet.** 

Page 25, line 1: "is turned" is redundant; I would prefer: **and are explained.** 
Ibid, line 7: for **by the addition of the declaration**, write: **by the addition, the declaration.** 

Page 34, last line: you could translate **if thieves should plunder you**, and so in the following passages. However, nothing of the meaning is lost, however you choose to translate it. 

Page 36, line 6: **a small city was made**, and thus more significantly: **it began to become a tiny town, and at length reached that point** etc. 

Page 43, line 13: for **by petition** substitute: **by vows.** 

Page 52, line 15: after "concerning him," these words were omitted: **which the Idumeans did in the desolation of the first temple, of which David, etc.** 

Page 67, line 3: for <note>original: "הפרק"</note> **the chapter** write <note>original: "על הפרק"</note> **on the chapter.** 

Page 74, line 23: **placed them in two boundaries**, I would prefer it thus: **in two regions: one was in Lusina** (or Lucina. It hardly suggests Portugal <note>original: "Lusitaniam"</note>. Let others judge whether he casts an eye back to Sanlúcar de Barrameda, once called the Forum of Lucifer, or indeed to a city called something else today. If the name fit, I would say Seville <note>original: "Hispalin"</note> was indicated). 
Ibid, line 25: translate: **the other in the province of Toledo.** 

Those who know what it is to translate entire Rabbinic books will easily forgive these and other faults; those who do not know are able to judge neither these nor other matters.

### In the Exegesis <term>Exegesis; the critical explanation or interpretation of a text</term>

Page 9, line 5: add: **Also compare Hoornbeek** <note>Johannes Hoornbeek (1617–1666), a Dutch Reformed theologian.</note> **page 541 f. and Paul Slevogt’s** <note>A German philologist and professor at Jena.</note> **special Dissertation.** 

Page 13, line 12: **Abarbanel cites the same on folio 92, column 1, from the tractate Berachot** <note>A section of the Talmud dealing with blessings.</note>. 

Page 16, line 8: **Abarbanel cites the same on folio 223, column 3, from the tractate Megilla** <note>A Talmudic tractate regarding the Festival of Purim.</note>. 
Ibid, line 18: **And Hoornbeek, page 16.** 

Page 18, line 15: **Unless perhaps they mean the same man as Obed.** 
Ibid, line 25: for **five** substitute: **four, or more exactly, three and a half.** 

Page 25, line 26: **Menasseh ben Israel** <note>A famous Portuguese rabbi and author (1604–1657) who lived in Amsterdam.</note> **feels the same.** 

Page 31, line 27: after "although" add: **according to the reports of Alsted, Bunting, and others** <note>Johann Heinrich Alsted and Heinrich Bünting were prominent 17th-century scholars of chronology and geography.</note>; **and in the last line, for Suleiman read Selim.** 

Page 33, line 32: add: **(Time has long since uncovered the deceptions of him, as well as those of the false messiah Sabbatai Zevi.)** <note>Sabbatai Zevi (1626–1676) was a Sephardic ordained rabbi who claimed to be the long-awaited Jewish Messiah, gaining a massive following before eventually converting to Islam under duress.</note>

Page 34, line 17: Or thus: **Of which war the principal object is Bozra, of that war the principal object is not Jerusalem.** 

Page 37, line 18: **According to some.** 

Page 38, line 12: for **O** you could also read **as if.** 

Page 65, line 4: add: **Buxtorf, Appendix on Betrothals and Divorces** <note>Johannes Buxtorf was a celebrated Hebraist; this refers to his legal scholarship on Jewish marriage.</note>. 
Line 22: read **Psalm 136, verses 6 and 7.** 

Page 80, line 29: omitted words: **"And they shall be as if they had not been."** 

The rest we entrust to your fairness.

Farewell.

## TOWARD AN EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL
# ON THE BOOK OF OBADIAH
### PRELIMINARY REMARKS.

To us, it has always seemed the most impudent and, above all others, an intolerable **accusation** <note>original Greek: ἔγκλημα</note> with which the Jews burden Christians: namely, their habit of calling them "Idumeans," calling the Roman Empire the "Idumean Kingdom," and referring to Rome itself as "Bozrah," the capital city of that kingdom. Anyone who desires evidence of this may consult Buxtorf’s *Talmudic Lexicon* (columns 30 and 2227), Helvicus’s *Giessen Disputations* (Volume 4, page 246), or Gerson’s *Talmud* (Part 1, chapter 17, page 104). Indeed, for anyone even moderately well-versed in the writings of the Rabbis—and especially those of Abarbanel—this matter is the best known of all. Nor is there any need for far-fetched conjectures or much divination to investigate the intention of these **Jewish writers** <note>original Latin: "Judæastrorum," a derogatory term using the suffix "-aster" to imply a poor or false imitation</note>, since anyone can easily perceive that **this plot is being hatched against us** <note>original: "hanc cudi fabam," a Latin idiom meaning "this bean is being threshed for us," or "we are the targets of this trouble"</note>. By means of this most foolish invention, they aim at our necks an **entire Iliad of woes** <note>original: "Iliada malorum," a classical reference to a massive amount of suffering</note> and every deadly thing predicted by the ancient Prophets against the Idumeans. Therefore, before we undertake our primary task, we shall first strike down this **primary lie** <note>original Greek: πρῶτον ψεῦδος</note> of the **Jewish claimants** <note>original Latin: "Apellarum," a Roman nickname for Jews</note> and the very flimsy arguments of this accusation, so that as we progress, we may more easily unravel the deceits of Abarbanel. Here, indeed, you may see into how many directions a **spirit of confusion** <note>original: "spiritus vertiginis," a biblical reference to being made "dizzy" or "staggering" in judgment</note> carries away these minor masters of the Jews! Truly, we may rightfully use that well-worn proverb: **"Truth is one, but falsehood is many-formed."** <note>original Greek: Τὸ ἀληθὲς ἓν, τὸ δὲ ψεῦδος πολυειδὲς εἶναι</note> First, Rabbi Nachmanides (on Genesis, folio 42, column 2), Rabbi Bechai (on the Pentateuch, in the section **Vayechi** <note>The Torah portion starting at Genesis 47:28</note>, folio 6, columns 1 and 2), and our author Abarbanel (on Isaiah chapter 34, folio 53, column 4) assert that Janus, the first King of Latium, was himself an Idumean—specifically, a grandson of Esau. From this they conclude that the Latin people are a colony of Idumeans and that the Romans are therefore Idumeans. They recount this from the history of **Josippon** <note>original: "Josepho Gorionide," referring to the *Yosippon*, a 10th-century history of the Jews often attributed to Josephus in the Middle Ages</note>, whom they hold in great...

### PRELIMINARY REMARKS.

...they are persuaded that it happened this way: the Edomite leaders denied passage to Joseph when he was seeking Canaan or about to bury his father there, but they were defeated in battle. One of them, named Zepho, a grandson of Esau (*Genesis 36:15*), was led away by Joseph into Egypt. Later, having slipped away from Egypt in flight, he came to Annius, the King of Carthage. When he was appointed by him as a military commander against the peoples of Italy, he seized a favorable opportunity to defect with his men to the Italians. He was hailed as King by them and obtained the name Janus, that is, *Saturn* (according to Abarbanel). From his own name, he founded Genoa and reigned for 50 years in Campania; see also *Zennorenna* <note>The *Tze'enah Ure'enah*, a highly influential Yiddish homiletic commentary on the Torah, first published in the late 16th century</note>, page 65.

2 **Response:** Although it would detract little from **authentic** <note>original Greek: γνησίως (gnēsiōs)</note> Christians if we allowed the seat of the Antichrist to be made *Bozrah*, the Antichrist to be made an *Idumean* or even *Esau* himself, and his offspring, the *Jesuits*, to be made *Esau-ites*—since they resemble such figures in their character, as the **Blessed Gesner** <note>Salomon Gesner (1559–1605), a Lutheran theologian and professor at Wittenberg</note> demonstrates in *On Obadiah* (page 75, bottom)—yet because the Jews weave too many broad consequences from this, we will concede nothing to them blindly.

We note, therefore:
1. Those things, as **Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Elder (1564–1629), a celebrated Hebraist and professor at Basel</note> also well-advised in his *Appendix to the Kuzari* (399), do not appear in the Basel *Münster* edition of **Joseph Gorionides** <note>Often called "Josippon," this is a 10th-century Hebrew history of the Jews. Early modern scholars frequently confused its author with the 1st-century historian Flavius Josephus</note>. Perhaps they are still to be sought in the Venice edition, which, as **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), one of the greatest scholars of the Renaissance</note> observed in *Refutation of the Three Sects* (chapter 4), is more complete at the beginning and end than the Basel edition.

2. Even if such a thing were found in the Venice edition, Joseph Gorionides is not of such authority among us that we should immediately agree with him. We rightly hold **Flavius Josephus** <note>The famous 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian and author of 'The Jewish War' and 'Antiquities of the Jews'</note> in high esteem whenever he speaks the truth. Yet Joseph Gorionides is not the same person as him. For the father of Flavius was Matthias, and his grandfather was Joseph (*Life of Josephus*, page 1); whereas either the father or certainly the grandfather of Gorionides was Gorion. Furthermore, Flavius Josephus distinguishes himself quite clearly from that other Joseph in *The Jewish War* (book 2, chapter 25, and book 4, chapter 5). Finally, this Gorionides often contradicts Flavius. For example, regarding the reason for the killing of John [the Baptist], compare Flavius Josephus (*Antiquities*, book 18, chapter 7) and Gorionides (book 5, final chapter, from the beginning). For this reason, Scaliger, Isaac Casaubon, Gerhard Johann Vossius, Johannes Drusius, Possevinus (in his entry for *Hegesippus*), Francisco Ribera, Constantin L'Empereur, Anton Hülsius, and others have long since convicted the history of Gorionides extant today of **spuriousness** <note>original Greek: νοθείᾱς (notheias)</note>.

3. And the lie itself, whoever its author is imagined to be, betrays and stabs itself. It is enough to produce a few absurdities as evidence. Abarbanel says that Zepho fled from Egypt in the sixth year after the death of Jacob. He indeed, according to—

### PRELIMINARY REMARKS. 3

**Nachmanides** <note>Moses ben Nachman (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar and commentator</note> and **Bahya** <note>Bahya ben Asher (13th century), a Spanish rabbi known for his Torah commentary</note> [conclude] that he fled only after the death of Joseph—that is, 54 years after the death of Jacob. However, they all cite **Josippon** <note>A 10th-century history of the Jews, often falsely attributed to Josephus (Gorionides)</note>, whom perhaps none of them actually saw when they were writing these things. Furthermore, the timeline itself is contradictory, making it impossible for Zepho to be **Janus** (whom Abarbanel ignorantly confuses with **Saturn**). On this point, **Hülfius** <note>Antonius Hulsius (1615–1685), a Dutch theologian and professor of Hebrew</note> has exposed Abarbanel’s notable deception (*in the place cited, page 135*). 4. Abarbanel himself, in the same work, admits that the Italians were established by **Kittim**, the grandson of Japheth; how, then, can they be derived from Zepho, the grandson of Esau, from the descendants of Shem? He responds that Zepho and his followers "changed" the Italians into Idumeans. What a wondrous metamorphosis! <note>original Greek: μεταμόρφωσις (metamorphōsis)</note> And what if the Italians had changed those few incoming Idumeans into Italians? **Far be it!** <note>original Greek: ἄπεγε (apege)</note> 5. Finally, the entire fable is slaughtered by Abarbanel's own kinsmen. For in the **Yalkut** <note>The *Yalkut Shimoni*, a major medieval collection of Jewish midrashic traditions</note> (*Part 1, page 50, middle of column 1*), the *Beth Israel* from the **Sotah** <note>A tractate of the Talmud</note> (*chapter 1, commentary 46*), and the **Tze'enah Ure'enah** <note>A popular Yiddish commentary for laypeople</note> in the section *Vayechi* <note>original Hebrew: ויחי, the Torah portion covering the end of Genesis</note> (*folio 65, column 3*), it is related that the sons of Ishmael, Esau, and Keturah came to fight Joseph, but when they saw Joseph's crown placed upon Jacob's coffin, they each took the crowns from their own heads and placed them on the same coffin. Therefore, no one fought, no one was captured, and no one escaped in flight to Italy. The **Cadmean brothers** <note>A classical reference to siblings or kin who destroy one another in internal strife</note> are mixing battles among themselves!

II. Abarbanel proposes a similar fabrication from **Josippon** (*at the place cited*): namely, that **Magdiel** <note>One of the chiefs of Edom listed in Genesis 36:43</note>, from the descendants of Esau, was the first founder of the Roman district. Therefore, the Romans are Idumeans.
**Response:** 1. This, whatever it is worth, is asserted without proof. We wish to be given reasons, not mere words. Indeed, one must wonder with what audacity this reckless Rabbi can contradict the **Jerusalem Talmud**, in which (*Tractate Avodah Zarah, chapter 1*) <note>original Hebrew: ע"ז (Abodah Zarah), the tractate concerning 'Foreign Worship'</note> **Remus and Romulus** are quite clearly said to have laid the first foundations of the city of Rome. These are the words: *On the day that Jeroboam erected the two calves, Remus and Romulus came and built two huts at Rome, etc.* But perhaps Magdiel is Romulus? Or perhaps he is not. Certainly, for Abarbanel later on, Magdiel is Rome itself. For a long time now, the Jews have ruined their own credibility with these most trifling fables about the origin of Rome, which you may see, if you wish, in **Buxtorf’s** *Talmudic Lexicon* (*column 2230*). 2. But even if the story being told were true for a moment—even if Magdiel were both Rome herself and the first founder and inhabitant of Rome—would all Romans and Italians therefore be Idumeans? By that logic, would not the Israelites who occupied **Canaan** have been Canaanites, since those were the first founders of their dwellings? *But the Idumeans could have later led colonies into Italy:* Indeed, and they also could **not** have. Even if they had, I ask you, how many times has Italy changed its inhabitants? How many times has it been brought under subjection by other peoples, with the old residents driven out and the colonists ordered to migrate? I could cite the Greeks,

### PRELIMINARY REMARKS.

[They] are persuaded that this occurred: that the Edomite leaders denied passage to **Joseph** when he sought to go to **Canaan** to bury his father there. Instead, they were defeated in battle, and one named **Zepho**—the grandson of **Esau** (*Genesis 36:15*)—was led away into **Egypt** by Joseph. Later, having escaped Egypt by flight, he came to **Annius, King of Carthage**. When he was appointed as the leader of the army against the peoples of **Italy**, he seized a favorable opportunity and defected with his men to the **Italians**. He was hailed as King by them and obtained the name **Janus** (that is, **Saturn**, according to **Abarbanel** <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a statesman and philosopher who argued that Romans were descendants of Edom</note>). He founded **Genoa** after his own name and reigned for 50 years in **Campania**. See also the *Tsenah u-Renah* <note>original: "Zennorenna," a popular Yiddish commentary on the Torah for laypeople</note>, page 65, column 2.

**Response:** Although it would take little away from **genuine** <note>original Greek: γνησίως (gnēsiōs)</note> Christians if we allowed **Bozrah** <note>A major city of Edom, often associated with judgment in prophecy</note> to be identified with the seat of the **Antichrist**, or the **Antichrist** with an **Idumean** (or even **Esau** himself), and the **Jesuits** to be made "**Esauites**" from his offspring—since they resemble such characters in their behavior, as the Blessed **Gesner** <note>Balthasar Gesner (1592–1650), a Lutheran theologian</note> demonstrates—yet because the Jews weave such overly broad consequences from this, we will grant them nothing rashly. 

We note therefore:
1. Those stories (as **Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a famous Hebraist</note> also rightly warns in his additions to the *Cosri*, page 399) do not appear in the **Basel edition** of **Joseph ben Gorion** edited by **Münster**. Perhaps they are still to be sought in the **Venetian edition**, which **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a foundational figure in modern chronology and historical criticism</note> observes is more complete at the beginning and the end than the Basel one.
2. Even if such a thing were found in the Venetian edition, this **Joseph ben Gorion** <note>Commonly known as "Josippon," a 10th-century Hebrew history of the Jews</note> does not have such authority among us that we would immediately agree with him. We rightly hold **Flavius Josephus** <note>The famous 1st-century Roman-Jewish historian</note> in high regard whenever he speaks the truth. However, he is not the same person as this **Joseph ben Gorion**. 
    * For Flavius's father was **Matthias**, and his grandfather was **Joseph** (*Life of Josephus*, page 1). The father or grandfather of the other was **Gorion**. 
    * Furthermore, Flavius Josephus distinguishes himself quite clearly from that other Joseph in *The Jewish War* (Book 2, Chapter 25, and Book 4, Chapter 5). 
    * Finally, this author often contradicts Flavius. For example, regarding the reason for the killing of **John** [the Baptist], compare **Flavius Josephus** (*Antiquities*, Book 18, Chapter 7) with **Gorionides** (Book 5, final chapter, at the beginning). 

For these reasons, the history of Gorionides currently in circulation was long ago proven to be a **forgery** <note>original Greek: νοθείας (notheias)</note> by **Scaliger**, **Isaac Casaubon**, **Gerhard Johann Vossius**, **Johannes Drusius**, **Possevinus**, **Francisco Ribera**, **Constantijn L'Empereur**, **Antonius Hülsius**, and others <note>This list represents the "Who's Who" of 17th-century European biblical and historical scholarship</note>.

3. And the lie itself, whoever its author is imagined to be, betrays and stabs itself. It is enough to produce a few absurdities to prove the point. **Abarbanel** says that **Zepho** fled from Egypt in the sixth year after the death of **Jacob**. He, indeed, according to **Nach-** <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to Nachmanides</note>

# PRELIMINARY REMARKS.

[The story] escaped the notice of **Nachmanides** <note>Moses ben Nachman (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar</note> and **Bahya** <note>Bahya ben Asher (1255–1340), author of a popular commentary on the Torah</note> until after the death of **Joseph**, that is, in the 54th year after the death of **Jacob**. Furthermore, the timeline itself contradicts the possibility that **Zepho** could be **Janus** (whom **Abarbanel** clumsily confuses with **Saturn**), a case where **Hulsius** <note>Antonius Hulsius (1615–1685), a Dutch theologian and Hebraist</note> has exposed **Abarbanel’s** notable deception in the work cited, page 135. 

4. **Abarbanel** himself, in the cited place, admits that the **Italians** were founded by **Kittim**, the grandson of **Japheth**; how then can they be derived from **Zepho**, the grandson of **Esau**, who comes from the descendants of **Shem**? He responds that **Zepho** and his people changed the **Italians** into **Idumeans** <note>Edomites</note>. A strange metamorphosis! <note>original Greek: μεταμόρφωσις (metamorphōsis)</note> And what if the **Italians** had instead changed those few **Idumeans** in their midst into **Italians**? Away with such a thought! <note>original Greek: ἄπεγε (apege)</note> 

5. Finally, the whole fable is cut down by **Abarbanel’s** own fellow countrymen. For in the *Yalkut* <note>The Yalkut Shimoni, a major collection of midrash (Jewish legal and homiletic stories)</note>, Part 1, page 50, column 1, middle; in the *Beth Israel* from the tractate *Sotah*, chapter 1, commentary 46; and in the *Tsenah u-Renah* <note>original: "Zennorenna"</note>, Section *Vayechi* <note>original Hebrew: ויחי</note>, folio 65, column 3, it is narrated that the sons of **Ishmael**, **Esau**, and **Keturah** came to fight against **Joseph**. However, when they saw the crown of **Joseph** placed upon the bier of **Jacob**, each of them took the crowns from their own heads and placed them on that same bier. Therefore, no one fought, no one was captured, and no one escaped by flight to **Italy**. These brothers are like the sons of Cadmus, fighting amongst themselves! <note>A reference to a Greek myth where brothers born from dragon's teeth killed one another; here it suggests Jewish sources are mutually destructive or contradictory.</note>

II. **Abarbanel** proposes a similar fabrication from the **Son of Gorion**: namely, that **Magdiel**, one of the descendants of **Esau**, was the first founder of the **Roman** region. Therefore (he argues), the **Romans** are **Idumeans**.

**Response 1.** This, whatever it is, is asserted without proof. We want reasons given to us, not mere words. Indeed, one wonders with what audacity this thoughtless **Rabbi** can contradict the *Jerusalem Talmud*, in which (in the Tractate *Avodah Zarah* <note>original Hebrew: ע"ז; "Foreign Worship" or "Idolatry"</note>, chapter 1) **Remus** and **Romulus** are quite clearly said to have laid the first foundations of the city of **Rome**. The words are these: *On the day when Jeroboam set up the two calves, Remus and Romulus came and built two huts at Rome, etc.* <note>Referencing the biblical King Jeroboam's idolatry in 1 Kings 12:28.</note> But perhaps **Magdiel** is **Romulus**? Perhaps, however, he is not; certainly, for **Abarbanel**, **Magdiel** later becomes **Rome** itself. For a long time now, the Jews have ruined their own credibility with these most trifling fables about the origin of **Rome** (which you may see, if you wish, in **Buxtorf’s** *Talmudic Lexicon*, column 2230).

2. But even if the fable being told were true for a time: suppose **Magdiel** were both **Rome** itself and the first founder and inhabitant of **Rome**—does it follow that all **Romans** and **Italians** will be **Idumeans**? Would not the **Israelites** occupying **Canaan** have been **Canaanites** by that logic, since they were the first founders of their own settlements? But the **Idumeans** *could* have led colonies into **Italy** afterward—but they also *could not* have. Even if they had: how many times, I ask, has **Italy** changed its inhabitants and been subdued by other peoples, with the old ones driven out and the settlers ordered to migrate? We could cite the **Greeks**,

III. Nevertheless, **Abarbanel** <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a statesman and theologian</note>, in the place cited, according to the sharpness of his own mind, proposes another conjecture <note>original Greek: στοχασμὸν (stochasmon)</note> and wildly guesses that **Lotan** (from Genesis 36:20) was the Latin King of Italy. 

**Response:** 1. This is nothing but an uncertain allusion. 2. Moreover, the insufficiently cautious Rabbi did not notice that **Lotan** was not from the **Idumeans** <note>Edomites</note>, but from the **Horites**, who were driven out by the Idumeans, according to Deuteronomy 2:12.

IV. **Arias Montanus** <note>Benito Arias Montano (1527–1598), a Spanish scholar</note>, following the **Son of Gorion** <note>referring to the Josippon, a medieval history of the Jews</note> as cited by **Christoph de Castro** (Commentary on Obadiah, p. 278) and **Cornelius a Lapide** (ibid., p. 352), reports that **Aeneas**, the King of Latium, was an Idumean, and that he fled into Egypt, then into Libya, and from there into Italy—a story that **Abarbanel** had previously attributed to **Janus** or **Saturn** based on the same Son of Gorion. Certainly, it is a wonder if the "Son of Gorion," being torn into so many different versions, could pull anyone over to his side.

V. But there is no end to this storytelling yet. Even **Julius Caesar**, when it pleases the Rabbis, was an Idumean. Therefore, all Romans are Idumeans, and the Roman Empire is an Idumean kingdom, since its first Emperor was such. **Rabbi David Kimchi** <note>Known as Radak (1160–1235), a prominent biblical commentator</note> asserts this premise in clear words in his commentary on Joel 3:19: <gloss>And King Caesar was an Idumean, and likewise all the kings after him who were in Rome.</gloss> 

**Response:** 1. The premise is false. Julius Caesar was born of the very famous Roman *Julia* family, as **Johann Glandorp** <note>A 16th-century German historian</note> shows in his *Roman Onomasticon*, page 425. 2. And the conclusion does not follow.

> If the Romans are Idumeans, rightly says **Francisco de Ribera** (Commentary on Obadiah, no. 5, p. 318), it would be because Julius Caesar reigned among them as an Idumean, and they themselves should be called Idumeans because they had **Herod** as King, whom it is established was an Idumean by lineage. And elsewhere [the Jews] say they have no king but Caesar.

And what then? Were the Idumeans themselves actually Jews when **David**, the King of the Israelites, ruled over them? (2 Samuel 8:14). Compare **B. Gesner** on Obadiah, page 82.

VI. Indeed, even **Titus Vespasian** <note>The Roman General and Emperor who destroyed the Temple in Jerusalem</note> was an Idumean, if we believe the Jews. For in the Talmudic tractate *Gittin* (Chapter 5), he is called: <gloss>The wicked one, son of the wicked one, grandson of Esau the wicked.</gloss> See also *Beth Jacob*, folio 31, 4, and Kimchi on Obadiah 11. Therefore, [they argue] the Roman Empire is Idumean. 

**Response:** **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a leading scholar of the Renaissance</note>, in his notes on the *Chronicle of Eusebius* (no. 2152), says:

> The Jews, both ancient and modern, believe that Titus was an Edomite, and whenever anything is mentioned by the Prophets regarding the Edomites, they often interpret it as referring to Titus.

But I would be doing nothing less than playing with trifles if I were to linger over these fables, just like the Jews who invent them. We could likewise mock this fabrication, yet these "Apellae" <note>A Roman slang term for Jews, often used derisively in literature to mean "the circumcised"</note> shall not lie with impunity. 1. Again, the premise is most false. Titus Vespasian was born from the Roman Flavian family...

# PRELIMINARY REMARKS.

...was born; his father was Titus Flavius Vespasianus, his grandfather Titus Flavius Sabinus, and his great-grandfather Titus Flavius Petronius. Compare *Suetonius, Life of the Caesars, chapter 10.2.* And how shameful is this lack of logical sequence <note>original Greek: ἀνακολουθία (anakolouthia), meaning a failure to follow logically</note>: "A certain Roman Emperor was an Idumean. Therefore, the Roman Empire is Idumean. Therefore, Christians are Idumeans?" By this logic, Septimius Severus was an African. Are we therefore Africans? Likewise, Maximinus was a Thracian, Philip an Arab, Decius a Pannonian, and Flavius Claudius and Diocletian were Dalmatians. Shall all Christians then become Thracians, Arabs, Dalmatians, and what else might they not become? 

Let this Jewish fable pass away then, along with that other one found in the trifles of the *Talmud (Gittin, folio 56.2)*, the *Pirke Eliezer (chapter 49)*, and *Bereshit Rabbah (Section 10)*: the story that a fly entered through the nostrils into the brain of the Emperor Titus and most miserably tortured him for seven years, and indeed killed him; and that it finally grew to the size of a dove. **Rabbi Eliezer** reports that he himself saw this fly, weighed at two pounds, in Rome. Who would learn the origin of Titus from those who lie so shamelessly about his death?

VII. The Jews argue that we are Idumeans not only because of origin and governance, but also because of religion, though they do not use a single consistent reason. First, they assert that the Idumeans were the first to embrace the Christian faith; hence, they claim Christians are not wrongly called Idumeans. The former point is asserted by **Rabbi Nachmanides** <note>Moses ben Nahman (1194–1270), a prominent Spanish Sephardic rabbi and scholar</note>, whose opinion **Abarbanel** approves with his own reckoning, adding: *“Without doubt Nachmanides did not invent this himself, but received it so from others. Perhaps he found it in certain annals of those times.”* 

And **Rabbi Aben Ezra** <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a prolific scholar of the Golden Age of Spain</note> writes on *Genesis 27:40*: *“There were men, not very many, who believed in a certain man whom they treated with divine worship. When Rome received that faith under Constantine—who reformed the whole religion and placed the image of that man on his banner—there were none in the world who received that new religion except for a few from the Idumeans. Therefore the Roman Empire is called the Empire of Edom. Likewise, at this time Egyptians, Sabaeans, and Elamites are called Ishmaelites, although few among them are truly of the seed of Ishmael.”*

**Response:** 1. It is false that the Idumeans were the first to embrace the Christian religion. "The law went out from Zion, and the word of the Lord from Jerusalem" (*Isaiah 2:3, John 4:23*). Therefore, the Jews were the first who received the new law of the true Messiah—let them burst with envy at our words <note>original Latin: "nostris rumpantur ut ilia verbis," a classical idiom for bursting with jealousy</note>. And if, as Abarbanel says, the Apostles of Jesus first led the Idumeans astray, certainly the Apostles (who were Jews) had already embraced the Christian religion before the Idumeans. Furthermore, it is manifest from *Mark 3:8* that afterwards, among others, even Idumeans adhered to Christ. Now, if we are to be named after the people who first received the faith

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Jewish historical claims. On the previous page, he dismissed the idea that the Roman Emperor Titus was of Idumean (Edomite) descent. # PRELIMINARY REMARKS.

...our own, we would be Jews, not Idumeans. 2. But even if we grant that the first Christians were Idumeans, it does not follow that all Christians can be called Idumeans because of their religion. The followers of a sect take their name from the first author of that religion, not from his first disciples. Thus, in the past, the Sadducees were named after Zadok, and the Hillelites and Shammaites were named after the leaders of their schools, Hillel and Shammai. Therefore, by the logic of faith, the Jews should call us Christians after the leader of our religion, not Idumeans after his obscure disciples. 3. Nor does **Aben-Esra** <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a celebrated Spanish Jewish scholar and biblical commentator.</note> help his own cause when he says that many are called Ishmaelites (by the Jews, that is) who are not actually so. We must ask: by what right? They call us Idumeans as well, though we are not. Consequently, not every Jewish etymology should be taken as Gospel truth by us.

VIII. Nor are we moved by the fiction of **Rabbi Nachmanides**, **Rabbi Nissim**, **Rabbi Kimchi**, and **Joseph Albo** in his <term>Ikkarim (Principles)</term> <note>A famous work of Jewish philosophy by Joseph Albo, written in the 15th century.</note>, Discourse 4, Chapter 42. All of these men lived at least 1,100 years after the birth of Christ. They claim that the Italians received their faith through a certain Idumean <term>Sacrificulum (Priest)</term> <note>original Hebrew: *komer* (כומר), a term often used in a derogatory sense by Jewish writers for non-Jewish or Christian priests.</note>. 

I respond: 1. **Abarbanel** <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a statesman and philosopher who argued that Rome was the "successor" to Edom.</note> himself admits that they do not provide clear reasons for this assertion. Our shoulders are not so strong that we can carry so many Jewish lies. 2. Furthermore, the logic is flawed. We could likewise conclude: **Rabbi Akiba**—from whom the Jews received a large portion of their traditions—was a convert, a Canaanite by birth, and specifically from the descendants of Sisera, as recorded in the <term>Juchasin (Book of Genealogies)</term> page 41 and the <term>Zemach David (Sprout of David)</term> page 86. Does it follow then that the Jews are Canaanites, and that the punishments predicted for Canaanites in Holy Scripture apply to them? If they say that Akiba ceased to be a Canaanite when he became a Jewish convert, we shall say that the "Idumean Priest" ceased to be an Idumean when he became a Christian.

IX. Furthermore, **Abarbanel** argues we are Idumeans based on a similarity of character. He notes it is common for the Prophets to name one people after another if their behaviors are similar, just as Isaiah (Chapter 1, Verse 10) calls his contemporaries "rulers of Sodom" and "people of Gomorrah." He lists five main points by which Christians can best be compared to **Esau** <note>Esau, the brother of Jacob, is traditionally identified in Jewish thought as the ancestor of Edom/Idumea, which in turn became a symbolic name for Rome and later Christendom.</note>:

1. Just as Esau had the same father as Jacob, he says we acknowledge and worship the same one GOD as the Jews.
2. Just as Esau treated his father, so we worship GOD deceitfully and fraudulently.
3. Just as Esau married foreigners, so the Romans mixed themselves with the most diverse nations.
4. Just as Esau was deprived of his birthright and possessed a blessing only in a physical sense, so we enjoy only temporal (earthly) benefits.
5. Just as Esau hated Jacob, so we hate the Jews.

I respond: 1. In this entire comparison, no strength of argument is detected. Thus far, **Abarbanel** <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a prominent Jewish statesman and philosopher who frequently critiqued Christian interpretations of prophecy.</note> has said nothing against us that all Jews do not also attribute to the **Muhammedans** <note>Muslims.</note>, whom they nevertheless maintain are not Idumeans but **Ishmaelites**. 2. We do not deny that the Prophets sometimes derive the name of a people from the character of another; however, that naming is transitory and temporary, expiring if the sinners repent. See **Jeremiah 18:7** and **Ezekiel 18:22**. Unless the Jews concede this, they themselves will forever be **Sodomites and Gomorrhæans**, because they are explicitly called so by **Isaiah** in chapter 1, verse 10. 3. The Rabbi objects to such crimes against **genuine** <note>original Greek: *γνησίως* (gnēsiōs), meaning "sincerely" or "legitimately."</note> Christians in vain. Hypocrites will expect no defense of themselves from us. Indeed, since in the Sacred Scriptures the name of "Idumeans" sometimes refers to any impious people—a view held by **Eusebius** in his *Preparation for the Gospel*, book 5, chapter 24—we say there is no reason why pseudo-Christians might not also be called Idumeans in this sense. This does not affect us: *whether they be impious Trojans or Rutulians, they are held with no distinction.* <note>original Latin: *impii Troës Rutilive fuant, nullo discrimine habentur.* This is an adaptation of Virgil’s *Aeneid* (10.108), where Jupiter declares he will make no distinction between the warring factions.</note> However, let the Jews also consider their own morals and those of their Rabbis (which **Gerson** treated in his work on the **Talmud**, part 2, chapter 17), and let them stop slandering us, lest they hear of their own misdeeds. 4. But let us touch briefly on the individual points. **α.** Abarbanel concedes that Jews and Christians know and grasp the same GOD. This is true not by way of formal concept, but objective concept. Yet, in his commentary on **Isaiah**, page 712, he clearly charges us with **Idolatry**. We, for our part, though we gladly absolve the Jews from the gross idolatry of the heathens, nevertheless prove that they are not free from every species of idolatry; this is shown by the **Great Man, Dr. Calovius** <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a leading Lutheran orthodox theologian and the author’s teacher.</note>, our venerable teacher, in his *System of Theology*, volume 1, chapter 2, question 10, page 189, and in his *Additional Remarks on the Syncretism of Calixtus*. **β.** True Christians worship GOD **without hypocrisy** <note>original Greek: *ἀνυποκρίτως* (anhypokritos), meaning "unfeignedly."</note> and are not to be judged by the character of hypocrites. That the Jews of this time adhere to GOD with true faith—which is Abarbanel’s vain boast—is denied by everyone in the whole world. **γ.** And the delusions of the Romans, especially those not yet imbued with the Christian religion, must not be charged against all Christians. Meanwhile, now that the **Messiah** has been revealed, we are permitted with impunity to marry wives from outside our family and kinship, all else being equal. Let the Jews, however, look into themselves for a moment and examine their own **Genealogy**; they will certainly find an irreparable confusion. For today, after the genealogical records were burned in the destruction of **Jerusalem** and so many subsequent dispersions through the climates of the world, not one of them can prove himself a true **ancestor** <note>original Greek: *πρόγονον* (progonon), though here used in the sense of a "descendant" or "scion" of a lineage.</note> of Jacob or of his own lineage...

**Abarbanel**, in his *First Response*, discusses the **Transmigration of Souls** <note>original Greek: *Metempsychosis*. This is the belief that a soul is reborn into a new body after death.</note> which, according to most, was first devised by the **Pythagoreans** and **Platonists** and from there nurtured by the **Pharisees** and **Kabbalists**. It is nothing but a most absurd fiction, which is exploded by the **Jesuit commentators of Coimbra** <note>original: *Conimbricenses*, referring to the influential 16th-century Aristotelian commentaries from the University of Coimbra.</note> in their *second book on the Soul, chapter 1, question 7, article 1*, and by the learned **Dr. Dannhauer** in his *Psychological Collections, Dissertation 1, controversy 5*. And I do not know what the Jews will answer to the following doubt: Since, according to the Kabbalists, the soul is numerically one and informed the bodies of **Adam** and **David**, and is destined to inform the body of the **Messiah**, how can these three eventually rise again in the resurrection such that no one lacks his own soul? Let them break their teeth on this problem. <note>original Latin: *Frangant hic genuinos suos*, a metaphorical way of saying "let them struggle with this difficult point."</note> Compare **L’Empereur** on *Isaiah 53, page 231*. If such fabrications are considered arguments, we might as well assert that the soul of **Balaam’s donkey** migrated into **Abarbanel**—just as **Empedocles**, who was also accustomed to thinking such trifles were significant, used to say that he himself was once a fish.

And what does that wretched anagram of the name **Jesus** <note>original Hebrew: ישו (*Yeshu*)</note> into "vanity" or "lie" <note>original Hebrew: שוי (*shav*), though the author notes it is spelled incorrectly without the letter *Yod*.</note> mean? In this way, anything can be made out of anything. For example, by doubling the letter *Bet* in the name **Abarbanel** <note>original: אברבנאל</note>, I could produce: *A son who brings shame upon his father* <note>original Hebrew/Aramaic: בר נבל אבא (*Bar naval aba*), citing the Targum on Micah 7:6</note> or *The foolish Master has come* <note>original Hebrew: בא רבא נבל (*Ba raba naval*)</note>, and what not? 

Furthermore, if living in the wilderness makes one an **Edomite** <note>original Latin: *Idumæum*. Abarbanel argued that Jesus's followers or origins were linked to Edom, a traditional enemy of Israel.</note>, then tell me, **Don Abarbanel**, were not your ancestors—who wandered for 40 years in the desert—Edomites? Was not **Elijah** (*1 Kings 19:4*) actually **Esau**? But it is also claimed that our **Savior** was contentious? I hear the same old song that was sung against all the **Prophets**. In truth, the Jews themselves are the greatest "Edomites," since they are described everywhere as litigious and quarrelsome (*Exodus 17:2, 2 Samuel 19:9, Isaiah 41:12, Jeremiah 15:10*). Our Savior was most gentle (*Isaiah 42:1, 4*). That he occasionally rebuked the Pharisees will surprise no one who knows that to maintain peace with the wicked is to declare war on the godly. 

**XI.** **Jerome**, in his *fifth book on Isaiah*, reports that some of the Hebrews read "Rome" <note>original Hebrew: רומה (*Roma*)</note> instead of "Dumah" <note>original Hebrew: דומה (*Duma*)</note> in *Isaiah 21:11*, applying that prophecy to the **Roman Empire**. Truly, that was a malicious perversion of the text, regarding which see **Cornelius à Lapide** and **Francisco Ribera** on this passage, note 3. 

**XII.** Thus, we are not moved by the "witnesses from the house"—the **Talmudists** and **Targumists**—brought forward by Abarbanel. We are well aware that the Jews' hatred for the Romans began when the Romans' rule over them began. This hatred then boiled over into mere slanders; for *out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks* (*Matthew 12:34*). <note>original Greek: ἐκ τοῦ περισσεύματος τῆς καρδίας τὸ στόμα λαλεῖ</note>. This hatred was later transferred to all **Christians**, who were held in the same low regard. Hence that "criminal charge" regarding their **Edomite origin**, which could however be more rightfully thrown back at the authors themselves. For the Edomites, under **John Hyrcanus**, merged into one people with the Jews, and from that time began to be counted among the Jews—

# ABARBANEL.

The words of the Prophet Obadiah contain only one single coherent prophecy from beginning to end: therefore, I did not wish to establish a formal partition, but I have chosen to propose six questions here:

**THE FIRST QUESTION** is this: *Whether the present prophecy, which Obadiah prophesied concerning Idumaea, has already been fulfilled by **Nebuchadnezzar**? For after he had subjugated the land of Israel and desolated Jerusalem, he also subjected the kingdoms of the neighboring nations to himself—namely the Egyptians, Ammonites, Moabites, Idumaeans, and others, as is recorded in Jeremiah from chapters 46 to 50. Or, was it fulfilled only later in the time of King **Hyrcanus** <note>John Hyrcanus I (ruled 134–104 BCE), a Hasmonean leader who famously forced the Edomites to convert to Judaism.</note> of the descendants of the **Hasmoneans**, who, invading Idumaea, struck down its cities, namely Reviva and Marissa, and crushed the pride of the Idumaeans, making them his tributaries and binding them to the rite of circumcision, as all these things are reported by Josippon in his History book 4, chapter 4, and by Flavius Josephus in Antiquities book 13, chapter 17? Or finally, is this prophecy not yet fulfilled, but remains to be fulfilled in the future?*

**THE SECOND QUESTION:** *What is the intended meaning of **Edom** and **Mount Esau**, about which Obadiah prophesies in the present prophecy? Should we understand the Idumaea that borders the land of Israel, past which the Israelites traveled,*

<header>ABARBANEL’S EXAMINATION OF OBADIAH.</header>

*...before they came into the land of Sihon and Og <note>Sihon and Og were two Amorite kings defeated by the Israelites during the Exodus, as described in Numbers 21.</note>, as is recorded in the Law <note>The Torah or Pentateuch.</note> in Numbers 20:14 and following; which David also subdued, placing a garrisoned soldier in it, as in 2 Samuel 8:14; and which Nebuchadnezzar desolated, as did the aforementioned Hyrcanus? <note>John Hyrcanus (134–104 BCE), a Hasmonean leader who famously forced the Edomites to convert to Judaism.</note> Or should we truly understand Rome and the other lands of the Christians by "Edom" and "Mount Seir"—those whom our Sages of blessed memory <note>original: "Sapientes nostri p. m." (piae memoriae). A traditional Jewish honorific for deceased rabbis.</note> were accustomed to call the Idumaean <note>A Greek/Latin name for the Edomite people.</note> nation, and Rome, the city of Edom?*

THE THIRD QUESTION: *How does it happen that this prophecy of Obadiah regarding Idumaea occurs in almost the same words without any change in Jeremiah chapter 49, verse 14 and following? This is especially puzzling since our Sages of blessed memory have long ago warned in the tractate Sanhedrin, folio 89, side 1 <note>A section of the Babylonian Talmud dealing with legal and prophetic matters.</note>: "Two Prophets never prophesy with the same style or tenor." And it is certainly worth our while to know which of these two preceded the other in prophesying: was it Obadiah before Jeremiah, or was it the latter before the former? And what is the necessity or use for two Prophets to occupy themselves with one and the same matter in their vaticination? <note>A formal word for prophecy or predicting the future.</note> Even if God, the Blessed One, wished to reveal His secrets to Obadiah, why did He not indicate something else to him other than what was said about Idumaea?*

THE FOURTH QUESTION *is about those words: "Thus says the Lord* GOD *to Edom: We have heard a rumor from the Lord." For what he says: "We have heard a rumor from the Lord," can in no way be understood as being spoken by God the Blessed One Himself, as if this speech and these words were His own. For how could* God*, the Blessed One, say of Himself: "We have heard," in the plural? And how could He have heard it "from the Lord"? It would be as if He were outside of Himself. If, however, these are the words of the Prophet speaking about himself, why does he say: "Thus says the Lord"?*

THE FIFTH QUESTION *is about what he says: "And there shall not be any remaining of the house of Esau, for the Lord has spoken it." It is as if he were saying that no one from among them would be left as a survivor or escape, but that absolutely everyone would die in that war. Yet Amos contradicts this at the end of his prophecy in chapter 9, verse 12 <note>The text cites chapter 11, which appears to be a misprint or different numbering for Amos 9:12.</note>, saying: "That they may possess the remnant of Edom." For here he teaches that there will be certain remnants who survive, over whom the Israelites shall rule. Whence, then, does such a contradiction occur in their words, since the words of both are of the Living God and all were delivered by the one Divine Shepherd,* GOD?

THE SIXTH QUESTION *is about those words: "And the captivity of this host, who are among the Canaanites even unto Zarpath" <note>Usually identified as Sarepta in modern-day Lebanon.</note>: For what business is...*

<sig>C 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Canaan-</meta>

<summary>Abarbanel raises several critical questions regarding the Book of Obadiah, including why its text mirrors Jeremiah's, the grammatical oddity of God speaking about "hearing a rumor from the Lord," and a perceived contradiction with the Prophet Amos regarding whether any Edomites will survive.</summary>

<header>ABARBANEL’S EXAMINATION</header>

Canaanites in this verse and (what you might wonder at more) what do they have to do with Zarpath? <note>Zarpath is the Hebrew name for the Phoenician city of Zarephath, but in medieval Jewish literature, it became the standard term for France.</note> Rabbi David Kimchi <note>Known as the Radak (1160–1235), a prominent medieval rabbi and grammarian.</note> explains "Canaanites" as referring to the Germans inhabiting Germany and Slavonia; but I do not know where he obtained this prophecy, that these lands are inhabited by Canaanites, unless perhaps he looked to the words of Rabbi Abraham Ibn Ezra, who heard from the reports of great men that Germany is inhabited by Canaanites who fled from their lands before the Israelites. Truly, the matter is not so. For the Canaanites crossed over into Africa, as the Sages of pious memory <note>original: "p. m." (piae memoriae)</note> relate. And look, I shall explain the text while solving all these questions at once.

THE GENERAL THEME *of this prophecy is that it shows the future desolation of Edom, and explains that this nation, which is called Edom, was at the beginning a small and meager thing, but afterward its empire extended through the whole world. Likewise, that many and evil afflictions had indeed happened to this people before, yet they could never be totally destroyed; but at the time of the final gathering of the Israelite exiles, it is to be desolated entirely and cut off by a universal and total devastation, because it acted wickedly toward the Israelites by desolating their Temple—both the first and the second—as well as through its decrees and persecutions while the Israelites were under its rule; finally, that the returning Israelites will be the rulers and will gain power in its land and in all the Southern cities, and so the kingdom shall be the Lord's—as will be explained more fully in the text itself.*

**a.** In the Basel edition of Josippon <note>original: "Gorionidis." A history of the Jews from the Second Temple period, often attributed to a "Joseph ben Gorion."</note> in the place cited, that city is called בירת Debir, and by Flavius Josephus <note>A 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian.</note> in the place cited, Adora. **b.** Our Abarbanel himself carefully collected the places in his commentary on Isaiah 34, at the end of section 53, verse 4. **c.** Or: *Who else would it be besides the Lord Himself.* **d.** A phrasing taken from Ecclesiastes 12:11. Elsewhere, GOD is called the Shepherd, Genesis 48:15, Psalm 23:1, Psalm 80:2, etc. **e.** Mercerus <note>Jean Mercier (d. 1570), a famous French Hebraist and scholar of the Bible.</note> renders the words of Kimchi thus: *In the Tradition <note>original: "Cabala"</note> it is handed down that the Germans are Canaanites: that when the Canaanite turned his back to Joshua (as we have written in the book of Joshua), they betook themselves to Germany and Slavonia, which they call the land of Ashkenaz <note>The Hebrew term for German-speaking lands.</note>; and today they are still called Canaanites.* **f.** This is sarcasm from one Rabbi against another. **g.** Mercerus translated the words of Ibn Ezra thus: *We have received from the mouths of great men that those who inhabit Germany are Canaanites, who fled when the Israelites were entering the land; thus, that Zarpath is France.*

->AD OBADIAM.<-
<note>ON OBADIAH.</note>

# EXAMINATION.

Two things especially seem noteworthy to us in these words of Abarbanel <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508) was a prominent Jewish statesman, philosopher, and biblical commentator.</note>. The first concerns the division of the present prophecy; the second concerns its summary and subject matter. Regarding the first, Abarbanel believes the subject of the prophecy admits no partition, even though it can be very conveniently divided into two parts. The first part, from *verse 1 to 17*, contains legal threats regarding the destruction to be sent upon the Edomites <note>The "Idumaeans" in the Latin text refers to the Edomites, the descendants of Esau.</note>; the second part, from *verse 17 to the end*, contains Gospel promises about the spiritual liberation to be provided to the Israelites through the Messiah. This is certain for Christians from *Acts 3:24* and *chapter 10:43*; nor can the Jews deny it, because of a passage in the *Tractate Shabbat, chapter 6*: *All the prophets only prophesied for the days of the Messiah.* <note>original Hebrew: "כל הנביאים לא נתנבאו אלא לימות משיח"</note> Next, he adapts the subject of the prophecy to his own hypotheses and preconceived opinions; once these are reduced to nothing, this fabricated subject of the prophecy collapses of its own accord. We say more correctly: the prophet here reproaches the Edomites for their *malicious joy* <note>original Greek: ἐπιχαιρεκακίαν (epichaerikakia); the act of taking pleasure in the misfortune of others.</note>, their insolence and violence against their kinsmen, the Jews, when they were being deported to Babylon; and he announces to these same Edomites the divine punishment of retaliation <note>The law of "talio" or "lex talionis" – an eye for an eye.</note>. To the Jews, however, he promises a certain liberation—both physical and, most importantly, a spiritual one to more effectively uplift their spirits, to be most abundantly provided through the arrival of the Messiah, awaited for so many centuries. Furthermore, we will not touch upon the six small questions proposed just yet, but we will weigh the author's responses in the following sections.

# ABARBANEL.

*THE VISION OF OBADIAH, etc., up to the words:* BEHOLD, I HAVE MADE YOU SMALL AMONG THE NATIONS, *etc.* *We do not have the means of knowing ancient things, which our own eyes have not seen, except from the report of others—provided they are truthful, so that it is advisable to rely on their report. And therefore, things received by tradition are among the types of reasoning used by the Philosopher* <note>Likely referring to Aristotle, often called "The Philosopher" in medieval scholarship.</note>. *Now, Scripture does not actually explain who this prophet was, nor in which century he prophesied. But our Sages, who received their teachings from every generation and even from the Prophets themselves, do they not teach you and say that this Obadiah is the one mentioned in the First Book of Kings, chapter 18, verse 3—that he was the master of the palace under Ahab? And they say he was an Edomite convert, and from...*

### EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL
<meta>catchword: besides</meta>

...to the descendants of Eliphaz, the friend of Job. Thus they say in the Tanchuma <note>The Midrash Tanchuma is a collection of ancient rabbinic homilies on the Torah.</note>: *The Vision of Obadiah* <note>original Hebrew: חזון עובדיה</note>. The Holy One, blessed be He, said to Eliphaz: "You rebuked my servant Job through a vision, as it is said: *In thoughts from the visions of the night, etc. Job 4:13.* I will raise up from you a son as a prophet, who shall take vengeance on the house of your father through a vision, which is called: *The Vision of Obadiah.*" And at the end of the fourth chapter of the tractate Sanhedrin <note>A section of the Talmud dealing with legal and historical matters.</note>, Rabbi Isaac said: The Holy One, Blessed be He, said: "Let Obadiah come, who lived between two wicked people—Ahab and Jezebel—and yet learned nothing of their evil deeds, and let him take vengeance on the wicked Esau, who lived between two righteous people—Isaac and Rebecca—yet learned nothing of their good deeds." Ephraim the Opponent, a disciple of Rabbi Meir, said: Obadiah was an Edomite <term>Proselyte</term> <note>A person who has converted to Judaism.</note>, which accords with the common saying: *The axe returns to the same forest from which it was taken.* <note>A Talmudic proverb suggesting that the tool for a forest's destruction comes from the forest itself; here, an Edomite convert is the one to prophesy Edom's downfall.</note> When Rabbi Dimi arrived, he added: *Your own hand has been wounded by yourself.* Furthermore, I have already written in the first inquiry of my Preface to the Minor Prophets why his book was placed after Hosea, Joel, and Amos, even though he preceded them in time.

But look! Rabbi Abraham Aben Esra <note>Also known as Ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a celebrated Spanish Jewish scholar and biblical commentator.</note>, in his excessive wisdom, writes that it cannot be said that this Obadiah is the same one described in the Book of Kings during the time of Ahab, because while it is written there that *Obadiah was a man who feared the Lord,* the Scripture does not call him a "Prophet," an excellence which is otherwise very distinguished. Truly, this objection of his is most vain. For perhaps, while Obadiah was in the court of Ahab, <insert>the prophecy was not yet inspired in him</insert>, by which he might have associated himself with those wicked ones, Ahab and Jezebel. Meanwhile, the Scripture testifies that he was a man who feared the Lord, protected the prophets, and delivered them from death. Certainly, he could easily have published this small prophecy later.

But Scripture has long since taught the truth of this matter when it says: *And a certain woman of the wives of the sons of the prophets cried out to Elisha, saying: "Your servant, my husband, is dead; and you yourself know that your servant feared the Lord greatly." 2 Kings 4:1.* And this was most certainly Obadiah, for he was famous for his piety and fear of **GOD**, which is why the Scripture calls him so three times. Since the Scripture further testifies that he was among the "sons of the prophets," why should we strongly deny that he produced a small prophecy of this kind? And as for his wife not calling him a "Prophet," she does this out of humility, or because he had not prophesied any other...

ON OBADIAH. 15

...besides this small prophecy. k. It is enough for us that Scripture calls him "one who fears the Lord," since Scripture furthermore testifies that he was a "son of the prophets." <note>A common biblical term for a member of a prophetic guild or a disciple of a prophet.</note> And perhaps for this very reason the wife <note>Referring to the widow of the "son of the prophets" in 2 Kings 4:1.</note> said: *And you yourself know,* for without doubt the matter of Obadiah and the prophecy which he prophesied was known to Elisha. And if the matter were not so, men of integrity of blessed memory would not have affirmed it, nor would the disciple of Rabbi Meir—who was himself an Edomite stranger—testify that Obadiah was likewise an Edomite <term>Proselyte</term> <note>A convert to Judaism.</note>; nor did this become known to them except from the tradition of the ancestors.

a. Or: *They are of the same certainty as philosophical arguments.*

b. It is his epithet <note>An honorary title</note>. For the ancient doctors of the Hebrews mostly had their honorary epithets, just like the Scholastic theologians. Hence Cocceius did not express these well in *Selections from the Sanhedrin, p. 243.*

c. Cocceius, in the cited place, translates: *From itself and into itself the forest [is cut], if it be fitted with an axe,* and a little further down: *If it clothes itself in iron.* Buxtorf renders it more conveniently: *From the forest itself the axe comes, and to the forest it returns,* in his *Talmudic Lexicon, chapter 3.* <note>This refers to the handle of the axe being made from the wood of the forest it destroys.</note>

d. Cocceius, in the cited place, reads *yarad* <note>original Hebrew: ירד</note> and translates: *The thigh contracts rot from itself.* But the sense is meager. Our reading is more suitable. The Amsterdam edition of Benveniste also reads *yarad*, but Abarbanel reads *yarar* <note>original Hebrew: ירר</note>, either from another edition or by conjecture.

e. Abarbanel again wittily criticizes his kinsman Ibn Ezra.

f. Mercer translates the words of *Ibn Ezra*: *We are ignorant of his age, nor can we say he is the one mentioned in the book of Kings in the times of Ahab, because it is written there that he was one who feared the Lord; but if he had been a Prophet in his own right, why would he be called "one who feared the Lord" and not likewise a "Prophet," since the dignity of a Prophet is far more excellent?*

g. Literally: *The prophecy did not rest upon him.*

h. It is a Rabbinic tradition that the <term>Shekhinah</term> <note>The Divine Presence</note> leaves a person when they associate themselves with the wicked: *En Israel, p. 151, column 3.*

i. Literally: *Of the Heavens.* However, the Rabbis also call <term>GOD</term> <note>original Latin: DEUM</note> "Heaven." *Buxtorf's Talmudic Lexicon, column 2440.*

k. We read: *Nistappaknu* <note>A Hebrew/Aramaic term meaning "we are satisfied" or "it is sufficient for us."</note>.

Here Abarbanel treats the birth and age of Obadiah, and quite boldly, along with his fellow countrymen Rashi and Kimchi (though Kimchi himself has some doubts), he declares him to have been a contemporary <note>original Greek: σύγχρονον</note> of Ahab and an Edomite by nation.
