<meta>This page continues the critique of Isaac Abarbanel’s commentary on the Book of Obadiah. Having previously discussed the prophet's identity, the author now examines arguments regarding Obadiah's birthplace and the historical era in which he lived, contrasting Rabbinic tradition with Christian scholarship.</meta>

### EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

Even if everything here is not fully explored due to Obadiah’s own silence, we still cannot grant Abarbanel either of his points. Regarding Obadiah’s homeland, Dorotheus, Bishop of Tyre, in his *Lives and Deaths of the Prophets*, and Epiphanius, in his *Life of Obadiah*, both assert that he was a Hebrew and specifically a Sichemite <note>A resident of Shechem, an ancient city in central Israel.</note>. Although this cannot be proven with absolute certainty, we rightly hold the Jews to their own rule: *Whenever the name of a prophet is given but his city is not, he is from Jerusalem.* Concerning this, see Vorstius’s notes on Maimonides' *Foundations of the Law*, page 148. 

And from where does Abarbanel prove that Obadiah was an Edomite? He relies on a paradoxical tradition <note>original: "ϖαράδοξον"</note> based on a principle we find highly suspicious. For although we do not reject all traditions, we do not rashly approve just any of them. We know that every man is by nature a liar, and that the Rabbis, above all others, have cast off all shame in fabricating stories. Drusius speaks correctly on this matter in his commentary on *Obadiah*, page 502: "As is usual," he says, "one may grant or withhold belief at will. For even if it is accepted, no absurdity follows from it, and the whole matter depends on the authority of those who recorded it in writing—men whom we know for the audacity they have always shown in inventing fables." 

Yet Abarbanel insists that the Talmudic writers received their traditions directly from the Prophets. But this is a very great assumption <note>original: "μέγα λίαν αἴτημα"</note>, and it is more easily claimed than proven. We are well aware that just as the Roman Catholics boast of a continuous succession of Popes, the Jews brandish their "perpetual Kabbalistic chain" before us like a Medusa’s head <note>A metaphor for a distraction or a terrifying argument intended to paralyze an opponent; reference to Vorstius on *Zemach David*, page 213.</note>; but we have long since grown calloused to such things. 

Furthermore, regarding the age in which Obadiah lived, Jerome, in his *Introduction to the Twelve Prophets*, cites a certain Hebrew rule: *In those books where the time is not stated in the title, the author prophesied under the same kings as the preceding prophet.* According to this rule, Obadiah would have prophesied under Uzziah and Jeroboam, the same era in which Amos prophesied. However, Mercerus <note>Jean Mercier, a 16th-century Hebraist.</note> testifies that he has read this in none of the Hebrew sources available to us. 

Certainly, the Talmudic writers and later Rabbis—with the exception of Ibn Ezra—ignore this rule. They believe our Obadiah is the same man who lived in the court of King Ahab (around the year 3034 from creation, according to David Ganz) and who is said to have fed a hundred prophets in *1 Kings 18:13*. For this reason, they surmise that he was gifted with the Prophetic Spirit by the Lord. This same opinion is seriously defended by Lyranus and Christophorus à Castro in his *Introduction*, Book 4, Chapter 3. Jerome and many others seem to have cited this same view, either following the opinion of the Hebrews or as a paradox <note>original: "ϖαραδόξως"</note>. They likewise claim our Obadiah was the third of King Ahaziah’s "captains of fifty," whom Elijah spared after the first two... <note>Refers to the narrative in 2 Kings 1, where Elijah calls down fire on two captains but spares the third who humbles himself.</note>

<summary>The author disputes Abarbanel's claim that Obadiah was an Edomite convert, arguing instead for his Judean origins and debating whether he is the same Obadiah mentioned in the historical books of Kings.</summary>
<keywords>Obadiah, Abarbanel, Jerome, Shechem, Jerusalem, Talmudic tradition, Kabbalistic chain, Biblical chronology, Ahab, Elijah</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Isaac Abarbanel’s commentary on the Book of Obadiah. Having previously discussed the prophet's identity, the author now examines arguments regarding Obadiah's birthplace and the historical era in which he lived, contrasting Rabbinic tradition with Christian scholarship.</meta>

### ON OBADIAH

He is recorded as having been spared from the fire that consumed others from heaven <note>original: "igne cœleſti abſumtis peperciſſe legitur," referring to the story of Elijah calling down fire on the soldiers in 2 Kings 1:14.</note>. This is reported by Dorotheus, Epiphanius, and John of Jerusalem, and from there by Sixtus of Siena <note>A 16th-century Jewish convert to Christianity and a renowned biblical scholar.</note> in his *Library*, Book 1, page 18. Mariana states that this is also the opinion of certain Hebrew scholars. However, Salianus refutes this same view in his *Annals* for the year 3255, number 11, volume 4, page 362. Furthermore, Josephus, in his *Antiquities of the Jews*, Book 9, chapter 2, attributes to Ahab’s palace governor that widow who paid off her debt with oil divinely provided through Elisha [2 Kings 4:1]; Abarbanel <note>Isaac Abarbanel, a leading 15th-century Jewish statesman and commentator.</note> favors this same view.

Nor are there lacking those who would have our Prophet be the same as Prince Obadiah [2 Chronicles 17:7], as Cornelius à Lapide <note>A highly influential Jesuit biblical commentator.</note> testifies. But in truth, one Obadiah is hardly sufficient to sustain all these roles. For is it credible that a Palace Governor, a Commander of Fifty, and a Prince would have bequeathed to his widow nothing but a small jar of oil? Who would easily believe that a palace governor or a general of the royal army was at the same time a Prophet? But perhaps he received the gift of prophecy after showing piety toward the Prophets? Lyranus <note>Nicholas of Lyra, a famous medieval Franciscan commentator.</note> draws on the passage in Matthew 10:41 for this point. Yet such a thing is elicited from that passage only with significant <unclear>συβλότητι</unclear> <note>original Greek: "subtlety" or "cleverness" — the author suggests the interpretation is a bit of a stretch.</note>. For the Savior does not say that he who receives a prophet shall *become* a prophet, but rather that, even if he is not a prophet, he shall receive the same gratuitous reward as a prophet.

Nor is the reasoning of Abraham ibn Ezra <note>A celebrated medieval Spanish Rabbi and scholar.</note> as vain as it seems to Abarbanel, or even to Mercer and Paraeus on this passage. If the Holy Spirit had intended to describe our Obadiah in the Book of Kings by his office and way of life, He surely would have called him a "Prophet" rather than a "Palace Governor." But perhaps he had published no prophecy during the time of Ahab? He had certainly published one by the time the author of the Book of Kings was writing; that author would surely not have omitted so honorable a title for Obadiah. Nor does it matter that he only published a small prophecy: it is enough that he published *some* prophecy, and even if he had left none behind, he still ought to be called a Prophet by reason of his office, even if not by his actions.

Finally, how loose is the logic <note>original: "conſeqventia"</note> of our Abarbanel: Both the Palace Governor and the deceased husband of the widow are said to have "feared GOD"; therefore, both were Obadiah. The argument would be: Obadiah the Palace Governor feared GOD. The anonymous husband of the widow feared GOD. Therefore, the anonymous husband of the widow is Obadiah the Palace Governor. We might similarly say: *Menasseh Ben Israel* is a *Rabbi*. *Abarbanel* is a *Rabbi*. Therefore, *Abarbanel* is *Menasseh Ben Israel*. Consequently, that Jewish opinion is more rightly rejected by the Jew Aben Ezra himself, and among the Church Fathers by Theodoret and Cyril on this passage; and among the Catholic commentators by Vatablus, Cornelius à Lapide, and Francisco Ribera in his *Second Prelude on the Prophets*.

<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: n. 9.</meta>

<summary>The author systematically refutes the identification of the Prophet Obadiah with other biblical figures of the same name, such as Ahab’s governor or the widow's husband, highlighting the logical fallacies in Abarbanel's traditional Jewish commentary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a scholarly debate regarding the life and timing of the prophet Obadiah. Having previously addressed and dismissed the legendary identification of the prophet with the palace official of King Ahab, the author now pivots to the "Chronological Examination," comparing the opinions of various Protestant and Catholic theologians to determine when Obadiah actually lived.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>AN EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL</header>

Number 9. Tirinus, etc. Among the followers of Calvin: Calvin himself and Drusius *on this passage*; Alting in his *Historical Theology*; Alsted in his *Chronology, pages 164, 166*; Hottinger in his *Philological Treasury, page 484.* Finally, among our own [Lutherans]: the Blessed Luther <note>The honorific "Blessed" (Latin: *Beatus*) was commonly used in 17th-century Lutheran scholarship for Martin Luther.</note> *Volume 8, Wittenberg edition, folio 441*; Gesner, Wigand, Pappus, Winckelmann, Himmel, Tarnovius *on this passage*; Gerhard in his *Exegesis, book 1, section 159*; Walther in his *Biblical Office, page 1030*; Micrelius in his *Syntagma, Part 1, page 275, and others.* 

For although they do not assign the same synchronisms <note>Historical alignments or contemporary timelines.</note> to our Obadiah—since Jerome, Cyril, Remigius, Rupert, Hugo, Ribera, à Lapide, Torniellus, and Salianus (who in his *Annals* assigns him to the year 3255 of the World <note>original: "Annū M. 3255," referring to *Anno Mundi*, or the year since the creation of the world.</note>) make him a contemporary of Amos, Joel, and Jonah, as well as Isaiah and Micah. Yet, according to the calculations of Helvicus, Amos, Joel, and Jonah flourished from the Year of the World 3120 to 3160. Funccius wishes for Obadiah to have been prominent in the Year of the World 3157, in the 22nd year of King Uzziah; Alsted says the same on page 166, although he correlates the 22nd year of Uzziah with the Year of the World 3159. Wichmann ascribes Obadiah to the Year 3416, while many other chronologists, such as Ubbo Emmius, Scaliger, Calvisius, Helvicus, and Petavius in his *Rational*, remain silent.

Nevertheless, none of those mentioned place him before the times of Hosea. If indeed something must be asserted, it seems most probable to us—along with the Blessed Luther, Pappus, Winckelmann, Wigand, Gesner, Gerhard, Himmel, Micrelius, as well as Drusius and others—that Obadiah prophesied around the time of the Babylonian deportation <note>The forced exile of the Jewish people to Babylon following the fall of Jerusalem in 586 BCE.</note> itself, alongside Jeremiah and the prophet Ezekiel. We rest in this general time period without determining the year precisely. This is quite clearly evident from the fact that the Prophet, from verse 10 to 15, reproaches the Edomites for their injuries, ambushes, frauds, and *malicious gloating over others' misfortunes* <note>original Greek: "ἐπιχαιρεκακίαν" (epichairekakian), a term describing the act of taking joy in the suffering of others.</note> during the Babylonian deportation of the Israelites; he describes all these things as if they had already happened in his own time. If, therefore, he were nevertheless said to have lived under Ahab, he would have reached nearly five centuries in age. The Blessed Gesner (page 12) and Cornelius à Lapide will provide more information on this.

## ABARBANEL.

***S**ince, therefore, Obadiah was an Edomite stranger who had received himself under the wings of the Divine Majesty <note>a. That is, he had become a Proselyte [a convert to Judaism].</note>, God showed him the destruction of his own homeland, so that he might thereby be consoled as a stranger, and to signify to him that his own nation <note>b. For if he knew his homeland was to be desolated, a longing for it could not take hold of him; rather, he congratulated himself that he had fled from there.</note> was to be utterly cut off. For it is more pleasing for a man to know what will happen in the future to his own nation and homeland than what will happen to another people. And for that reason, he saw no other prophecy but this one concerning Edom.*

<meta>catchword: EXA-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the prophet Obadiah lived during the Babylonian Exile (6th century BCE) rather than the 9th century BCE, citing his descriptions of the fall of Jerusalem as historical events. It also includes Isaac Abarbanel's view that Obadiah was a convert from Edom whom God consoled by revealing the future destruction of his former, wicked homeland.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general chronological debate among Christian scholars to a specific examination of the views of the Jewish commentator Isaac Abarbanel regarding the prophet Obadiah's origins and the ultimate target of his prophecy.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
-> ON OBADIAH <-

## EXAMINATION.

In providing his reasoning for why Obadiah prophesied specifically against the Idumaeans <note>The people of Edom, descendants of Esau.</note>, Abarbanel presupposes once more that the prophet was himself an Idumaean, as if it had pleased the Lord to arm an Idumaean against Idumaeans. To this point, he cites the proverbs mentioned above: one concerning an axe-handle made from the wood of the forest which later destroys the forest, and another concerning a hand wounding itself. To these may be added a third from the *Aruch* <note>The *Aruch* is a famous 11th-century dictionary of Talmudic and Midrashic Hebrew and Aramaic by Nathan ben Jehiel.</note> and *Kimchi* <note>David Kimchi (c. 1160–1235), also known as Radak, was a prominent medieval Jewish grammarian and biblical commentator.</note> regarding a craftsman who burns his mouth with the very spoon he fashioned. Abarbanel adds that God wished to console Obadiah in this way, since everyone desires to know the future fate of their homeland. While we do not deny this desire, we do not know how much consolation one can truly grasp from the foreknowledge of the destruction of one's own country and fellow citizens; for otherwise, everyone wishes their country well, and often more intensely than they should. It would be strange indeed if the ancestors of our contemporary Jews interpreted so many predictions regarding the total destruction of their land as "consolations." Furthermore, God intended to comfort not so much the person of a single Prophet, but the entire Jewish Church <note>original: "Ecclesiam Judaicam," here referring to the community of faithful Israelites.</note> through this prophecy. Specifically, He wished to prevent the Jews—upon seeing the Idumaeans (to whom servitude had been predicted) flourishing and ruling while they themselves were afflicted by them in various ways—from envying their lot or falling away from the faith in a state of wicked resentment. Compare Dr. Tarnovius <note>Paul Tarnow (1562–1633), a Lutheran theologian and professor at Rostock.</note>, page 7.

## ABARBANEL.

Furthermore, it cannot be said that he [Obadiah] foresaw in this prophecy that desolation of Idumaea caused by Nebuchadnezzar, as Rabbi Abraham Ibn Ezra <note>A celebrated 12th-century Spanish Jewish scholar and polymath.</note> claims; nor does it refer to the desolation brought by King Hyrcanus <note>John Hyrcanus (reigned 134–104 BCE), a Hasmonean leader who conquered Idumaea and forced the inhabitants to convert to Judaism.</note>. For our author says: *That every one may be cut off from the mount of Esau by slaughter.* And in the same sense, he says: *Nor shall there be any remaining of the house of Esau.* Now, it is well known that neither the destruction by Nebuchadnezzar nor that by Hyrcanus inflicted upon Idumaea was so universal that no one was left among them. From this, it is concluded that this prophecy speaks only of a future time when the Idumaeans shall be totally cut off and none of them shall remain as survivors—namely, when God shall have redeemed His people. Nor should it seem strange that the prophecy of Obadiah looks to such remote times; for even Balaam, who is almost six hundred years older than him, similarly predicted that final destruction of Idumaea. For he says in Numbers 24, verses 18 and 19: *And Edom shall be a possession, Seir also shall be a possession for his enemies; and Israel shall do valiantly. Out of Jacob shall come he* who

who should destroy the remnants from the city: just as I have explained these things in the first Herald of the book titled *The Preacher of Salvation* <note>original: *Prædicator salutis*; This refers to Abarbanel’s work *Mashmia Yeshu’ah* ("The Herald of Salvation"), which interprets messianic prophecies from a Jewish perspective.</note>. From this you may know, that the Prophet did not only prophesy concerning the Idumaeans <note>The people of Edom.</note> who were neighbors to the land of Israel, but also concerning the nation originating from there and afterwards spread through the whole world, which is the nation of the Christians, who are from the descendants of Edom <note>In medieval Jewish thought, Edom was frequently used as a coded reference for Rome and the Christian Church.</note>, as will be declared hereafter.

<margin>a. Here in Abarbanel's text, some things seem to have been repeated by a printer's error, which we have omitted. b. Indeed, almost nine hundred, according to our hypothesis. c. It is called *Mashmia Yeshu’ah*, a book by Abarbanel in which he explains prophecies yet to be fulfilled.</margin>

### EXAMINATION.

Now Abarbanel reaches the heart of his case and explains whom he thinks are targeted by this prophecy. He calls these Idumaeans the very *entrance* <note>original Greek: εἰσόδιον (*eisodion*), a prologue or entry-piece.</note> of the Prophecy; for after the title **The Vision of Obadiah** <note>original Hebrew: חזון עובדיה (*Chazon Ovadyah*)</note> it is immediately added: *Thus says the Lord to Edom.* Here it might first be cursorily investigated why Obadiah, about to denounce his prophecy in vain against his enemies, does not call it by the solemn word **burden** <note>original Hebrew: <term>משא</term> (*massa*), a term often used for a "weighty" or "burdensome" prophecy of doom.</note> or an "onerosity"? Ribera <note>Francisco de Ribera (1537–1591), a Jesuit biblical commentator.</note>, Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a Flemish Protestant Old Testament scholar.</note> in his notes on this passage, and B. Walther in his *Harmony* (p. 706) correctly observe that this is because a joyful outcome is predicted <note>Because the prophecy ends with the restoration of Israel.</note>. Furthermore, **vision** <note>original Hebrew: <term>חזון</term> (*chazon*)</note> is a common name for any prophecy, whether joyful or sad. For just as Prophets were anciently called "seers" (*1 Samuel 9:9*), so Prophecy is called a "vision." Hence the Targum <note>The ancient Aramaic translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> renders it: *The Prophecy of Obadiah*. But these things are mentioned only **in passing** <note>original Greek: ὡς ἐν παρόδῳ (*hos en parodo*).</note>.

To state briefly our own view on these Idumaeans: we believe the first part of the prophecy speaks of the true descendants of Esau <note>The historical Edomites.</note>, whose history Cocceius <note>Johannes Cocceius (1603–1669), a Dutch theologian.</note> recounts in this passage (p. 240); the latter part, however, refers to any nations whatsoever triumphed over by the Apostles and other spiritual Israelites, as we shall prove below. Regarding the fulfillment of the first part, we think there is no need to involve Sennacherib and the Syrians, whom Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a famous Dutch jurist and theologian known for his historical approach to prophecy.</note> brings in here. The injuries described here were inflicted upon the Israelites by the Idumaeans when the former were being deported to Babylon by Nebuchadnezzar. The penalty of retaliation predicted here was executed against the Idumaeans by that same Nebuchadnezzar, and others continued it. What the Idumaeans suffered from Nebuchadnezzar after the Jews were deported is not explicitly narrated by Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the 1st-century Jewish historian.</note>; nevertheless, it is likely that when Nebuchadnezzar (according to Josephus in *Antiquities* Book 10, Ch. 11) subjugated the Egyptians, Syrians, Ammonites, and Moabites after capturing the Jews, he also brought the Idumaeans under...

<meta>This page continues the "Examination" of Abarbanel’s commentary on the Book of Obadiah. The previous page ended with a discussion of whether the prophecy of Edom's destruction had been fulfilled in the past. Here, the author critiques Abarbanel's requirement that the destruction must be "total" to count as fulfillment, using historical evidence and Jewish legal principles to argue that the Idumaeans (Edomites) were indeed effectively destroyed as a nation by successive empires.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>ON OBADIAH.</header>

...cast off: as *Tirinus* <note>Jacobus Tirinus (1580–1636), a Jesuit biblical commentator.</note> rightly notes on *verse 16*. Our author [Abarbanel], however, gives the reason why the desolation by either *Nebuchadnezzar* or *Hyrcanus* <note>John Hyrcanus, a Hasmonean leader who conquered the Edomites in the 2nd century BCE.</note> cannot be understood here: because neither was total, which is required here, since *Obadiah* prophesies that all will be killed and no remnants left behind. But in truth, *Ribera* <note>Francisco de Ribera (1537–1591), a Spanish Jesuit theologian.</note> rightly observes on *Obadiah*, no. 86, that in Scripture "none are left" is said when only a few remain out of a great multitude, or certainly when those who remain are least fit for taking up war against enemies or for propagating their race and name in the future. *Ribera* (no. 87) adds examples regarding *Jehu*, who in *2 Kings 10:11* is said to have struck down all who were left of the house of *Ahab*, so that no remnants remained; nevertheless, some of Ahab's descendants remained in *verse 13* and *chapter 11, verse 2*. Likewise concerning the *Chaldeans* in *Zephaniah 1:2*. Compare *Zephaniah 2:6*, *Isaiah 24:1, 6*, and *Amos 9:1*. And the Talmudic rule is well known: <note>original Hebrew: בּיטוּל בּתר רוֹבּא (*bittul batar rova*)</note> *The small follows the majority, or it is not taken into account within the multitude.* <note>A legal principle in Jewish law where a minority is nullified by the majority.</note> *Kimchi* <note>David Kimchi (Radak), a medieval Jewish commentator.</note> on *Isaiah 59:16* takes "all Israelites" to mean the greatest part. *Rabbi Joseph Albo* in the book *Ikkarim* (Sermon 2, chapter 20) observes that <note>original Hebrew: רוֹב (*rov*), meaning "majority"</note> is sometimes taken for <note>original Hebrew: הַכֹּל (*ha-kol*), meaning "the all"</note>, meaning the greatest or some part.

Whether *Abarbanel* assumes the desolation by *Nebuchadnezzar* or *Hyrcanus*, we see nothing absurd: each sought the Idumaeans in war and brought them under the yoke, so that, although some of them remained, these were nevertheless so weak and terrified that they were counted for nothing. And so that no opening remains for *Abarbanel*, it is certain first that *Nebuchadnezzar* conquered the Idumaeans and thus carried out the law of retaliation predicted here in *verse 15*. However, even if he did not totally cut off the Idumaeans here, others after him did so, if *Abarbanel* wishes to insist so rigorously on a physical slaughter. For if we consult the historians, we find that after the Idumaeans were subjugated by the *Chaldeans*, they never again became independent <note>original: *sui iuris*, a legal term for being under one's own authority or self-governing.</note>, but were thereafter under the *Persians* and *Macedonians*, until they were defeated by *Judas Maccabeus* (*1 Maccabees 5:65*; *Josephus, Antiquities*, Book 12, chapter 11). Later, they were subdued again by *John Hyrcanus* and driven to adopt Jewish rites (*Josephus, Antiquities*, Book 13, chapter 17), at which point they began to be numbered among the Jews; and though they rebelled at times, they were nevertheless subjugated again by *Simon* <note>Simon bar Giora, a leader during the First Jewish-Roman War.</note> (*Josephus, Jewish War*, Book 4, chapter 30). And finally, when they joined the other Jews in rebelling against the *Romans* and came to *Jerusalem* to defend the common temple, they were subdued by *Vespasian* (Book 6, chapter 40). We read in *Hegesippus* <note>A 4th-century Christian historian who adapted Josephus’s works.</note> (Book 4, chapter 16) that he killed 10,000 of them, took 1,000 captives, and scattered the rest; since which time even the name of the Idumaean...

# EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

remains of them anywhere on earth: as *Tirinus* <note>Jacobus Tirinus (1580–1636), a Flemish Jesuit biblical commentator.</note> rightly notes on *verse 16*. Now we argue against Abarbanel: Whoever has been bodily destroyed, so that not even their name survives under heaven—in those people, the prophecy of Obadiah up to *verse 17* has been fulfilled. But the Idumaeans <note>The Latinized name for the Edomites, the descendants of Esau.</note> who lived near Palestine have been so destroyed. Therefore, this prediction is to be fulfilled in them, and not in Christians or Muslims. The "Major" premise is Abarbanel's own; the "Minor" premise has been proven thus far. Therefore, the conclusion is unshakable. We shall examine Abarbanel’s other minor objections below.

->ABARBANEL.<-

Furthermore, know that quite a few Prophets expressly prophesied about that Edomite desolation. In my opinion, there are nine of them. The first in time was *Balaam* (*Numbers 24:18-19*); the second after him in chronological order was our *Obadiah*; the third was *Isaiah* (*chapters 10:11 and 34:5*); the fourth *Joel* (*chapter 3:19*); the fifth *Amos* (*chapter 1:11*); after him the sixth was *Jeremiah* (*chapter 49:7 and following*); after him the seventh was *Ezekiel* (*chapters 23:29 and 36:5*); and the eighth, later than all these, was *Malachi* (*chapter 1:3*). The ninth, who likewise prophesied about Idumaea, was *David* (of blessed memory) in his Psalms; he was later than *Balaam* and earlier than *Obadiah* in terms of time. Since nine Prophets prophesied about this desolation, that is perhaps why Obadiah says: *We have heard a report from the Lord*, meaning, "So many Prophets along with me have heard this prophecy from the Lord."

->EXAMINATION.<-

*Christopher de Castro* <note>Christopher de Castro (1551–1627), a Spanish Jesuit who wrote commentaries on the Minor Prophets.</note> (on *page 279*) counts the same number of prophets predicting the destruction of the Idumaeans. However, because our author [Abarbanel] thinks Obadiah should be placed before the others in his chronological arrangement <note>original: χρονοταξεως (*chronotaxeos*)</note>, he was corrected by us above. Nevertheless, *Arias Montanus* <note>Benito Arias Montanus (1527–1598), a Spanish orientalist and editor of the Antwerp Polyglot Bible.</note> fosters the same opinion in his *Preface to the Minor Prophets* (page 4). He says, "If the order of all the prophets were to be established by their dates, it ought to have been defined in this way: that Obadiah was first, Jonah second, and after these Hoseas, Amos, and Micah should be placed," etc. And by *Christopher de Castro* (page 278), Obadiah is said to be the first of all those who committed their prophecies to writing. But neither of them solidly proves his opinion. Therefore, our business here will only be with Abarbanel, who above (on page 14) referred the reader to his *Preface to the Minor Prophets*, where he proves that Obadiah prophesied before Amos and Joel on the grounds that they borrowed certain things from him. But

<meta>This page continues the "Examination of Abarbanel" regarding the Book of Obadiah. The previous page listed Abarbanel’s chronological ordering of the prophets who spoke against Edom, placing Obadiah second after Balaam. The author is now critiquing the logic behind the ordering of the biblical canon and the specific phrasing of Obadiah’s prophecy.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

# ON OBADIAH.

With the same ease, we could assert that Obadiah borrowed some things from those other prophets. However, both claims are false, and no Prophet compiled his prophecies from others, as we will demonstrate later. But why is Obadiah placed after so many Prophets even though he was earlier than them? *Abarbanel* <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a Jewish statesman and commentator.</note> provides a reason in that same place. He says that whoever arranged the Prophets did not want to place *Obadiah* before the others who were clearly Jews, since he was a Proselyte <note>A convert; Jewish tradition often identifies the prophet Obadiah as an Edomite convert to Judaism.</note>. A childish reason and logic! *Let it pass, however:* if that were the case, *Obadiah* should have been placed in the very last position of all, since it is obvious that most of those who are placed after *Obadiah* were Jews by birth.

Next, *Abarbanel* proceeds to give a reason why *Obadiah* speaks in the plural: *We have heard*. For so the Hebrew text reads, although the Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> translated it as "I have heard" <note>original Greek: ἤκουσα</note>, and the Arabic as: "You have heard" <note>original: *Samitom*</note>. Furthermore, *Rabbi David Kimchi* <note>Radak (1160–1235), a prominent medieval Jewish grammarian and commentator.</note> asserts that this is the voice of the nations arming themselves for battle, and *Oecolampadius* <note>Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), a German Protestant Reformer.</note> does not reject this in his commentary on this passage <note>Latin: *h. l.* (hoc loco), meaning "in this place" or "on this passage."</note>. 

Most, however, believe these are the words of the Prophet—either of him alone, which would be an *Enallage* <term>Enallage: a grammatical figure where one form (like plural) is substituted for another (like singular)</term> of number, as noted by *Hugo, Robertus, Pareus* in his commentary (p. 371), and others; or that he is including himself with the rest of the Prophets who predicted the destruction of the *Idumaeans* <note>Edomites</note>, which is the most common opinion. This is the view held by *Ibn Ezra, Jerome, Remigius, Lyra, Montanus, Clarius, Cornelius à Lapide, à Castro, Ribera, Emmanuel à Sa, Tirinus, Menochius, Calvin, Mercer, Drusius, the Dutch* in their commentaries; and even our own *Winckelmann, Gesner, Tarnovius, the Weimar* commentators, and others in their works. 

Therefore, we do not reject *Abarbanel’s* opinion and reasoning; yet if *Obadiah* cast his eye back on those others who were speaking with him about the destruction of *Idumaea*, it is necessary that he prophesied with them or after them, but not long before them—and thus *Abarbanel’s* earlier assertion [about Obadiah's early date] can hardly stand. 

However, we are deeply pleased by the opinion of *Johannes Cocceius* <note>A 17th-century Dutch theologian known for his "federal theology."</note> on this passage (p. 243), namely that these words belong to the Word <note>original Greek: λóγoυ (*logou*), referring to the second person of the Trinity.</note> and the *Holy Spirit*, the Divine Persons, proclaiming from above <note>original Greek: ἄνωθεν (*anōthen*)</note> regarding the assault on *Bozrah* <note>A capital city of Edom.</note>. *Jerome* <note>St. Jerome, the 4th-century translator of the Vulgate.</note> also seems to have felt this way long ago, when he says regarding *Micah 4*: *“Moreover, I and my son and the Holy Spirit shall stir up,”* following what is written in *Genesis*: *“Let us make man in our image.”* And at the beginning of Obadiah: *“Arise and let us go up in battle.”* 

Indeed, *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 263–339).</note> in Book 5 of *The Proof of the Gospel*, chapter 24, says these are the words of the Son of God, who heard these words from the Father regarding the calling of the nations. And our esteemed <note>original Greek: ὁ πάνυ (*ho pany*)</note> *Calovius* <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a champion of Lutheran Orthodoxy.</note>, when he had reviewed this and other interpretations by *Cocceius* in his *System of Theology* (Vol. 3, Ch. 1), adds on page 21: *“We leave the judgment on these matters to the reader. However, that diligence which searches out the mysteries of faith in the Hebrew sources deserves praise rather than [censure].”*

<summary>This page debates the chronological placement of Obadiah in the biblical canon and examines why the prophet uses the plural "we have heard." It surveys various Jewish and Christian commentators, concluding with a Trinitarian interpretation of the text.</summary>

<header>EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL</header>

than that which obstructs them, lest they be permitted to draw the same from there. But we will bring more to the matter below.

## ABARBANEL.

Furthermore, I have already written in my commentary on Isaiah that it is customary for the Prophets that, when they foresee something in the Prophets about which another Prophet has already prophesied, they use the same voices and phrases in the account of that matter which some Prophet before them spoke. For they themselves, in their prophecies, did not hear things from the Lord in so many words and phrases; for this degree <note>A reference to the "level" or "rank" of prophecy.</note> belongs *a.* to the Lord of the Prophets <note>A title often used for God in this context, or alternatively referring to the unique status of Moses as the "master" of all other prophets.</note> because no prophet except him was allowed to reach it: for of him it is said: **And when Moses came to the tent of meeting to speak with him, he heard the voice speaking to him etc.** Numbers 7:89. And with those same divine phrases and voices which he had heard, he also wrote the law. But the other Prophets apprehended their prophecies either through a vision of parables or through the understanding of divine truths; but they themselves wrote down the visions in their own voices and words. Therefore, in the account of their Prophecies, they sometimes speak in their own language, and sometimes they utter their own words in the voices and phrases which another Prophet used before them. Do you not see that Moses our Master <note>original Latin: *Magister noster*; often used to translate the Hebrew *Rabbeinu*.</note> (of Blessed Memory) says in the song *Give Ear* <note>original Hebrew: *Haazinu* (האזינו); the Song of Moses in Deuteronomy 32.</note>, **For the Lord will judge his people and have compassion on his servants: because he will see that their strength has departed etc.** Deuteronomy 32:36. Nevertheless, David in Psalm 135:14 recites this very text: **For the Lord will judge his people and have compassion on his servants: the idols of the nations, etc.** Now it is certainly established *b.* that David did not act as a Prophet here; for although we may admit that he is a Prophet by reason of the style and words in which the Lord of the Prophets prophesied, nevertheless he received this verse in his words from the words of Moses. And you will find more of this kind among the rest of the Prophets. But it never happens to two Prophets that the same prophecy is identical in words as a whole and in all its parts; just as our Sages (of Sacred Memory) also said: *c.* **One argument can occur to several Prophets, but never do two Prophets prophesy in the same style.** I maintain: although a similar understood matter happened to so many Prophets in the prophecy of the Edomite desolation <note>The predicted destruction of the kingdom of Edom.</note>, which indeed nine Prophets saw, yet this is because the Holy Blessed God willed it, so that the matter...
<meta>catchword: scri-</meta>

<summary>Abarbanel explains that while Moses received the exact wording of the Law from God, later prophets translated divine visions into their own language, sometimes borrowing phrases from their predecessors (like David quoting Moses) while maintaining their unique individual styles.</summary>
<keywords>Isaac Abarbanel, prophecy, Moses, King David, Isaiah, Numbers 7:89, Deuteronomy 32:36, Psalm 135:14, Edom, biblical exegesis</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological "Examination" of the Jewish commentator Isaac Abarbanel’s theories on prophecy. The previous page introduced Abarbanel’s argument that while Moses received the literal words of God, later prophets only received divine concepts and expressed them in their own words—or even borrowed phrases from their predecessors.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
->ON OBADIAH.<-

the writings might be supported by the abundant testimony of many Prophets, as is said elsewhere in Isaiah 45:19: **I have not spoken in secret** <note>original Latin: "Non in occulto locutus fui"</note>; so that, if a message relied on the testimony of many Prophets, it might be better recognized as the word of the Lord. Since they therefore agree on the matter they have grasped, it could happen that a later prophet might take something from the words of a predecessor. However, it is not possible for those two to prophesy concerning all matters of the prophecy and its parts in the same style without the addition of a clarification or a change of words; for if they did, they would be "thievers of another’s words" <note>This refers to a Talmudic concept that true prophets do not use identical phrasing unless divinely intended, to distinguish them from false prophets who mimic others.</note>. This is exactly the case in our present controversy, where Obadiah prophesied about the desolation of Edom, and later Jeremiah came and saw the same thing. In recounting it, Jeremiah sometimes uses the words of Obadiah, whom he saw had already prophesied about it. Nevertheless, Jeremiah adds a certain clarification and more threats, as will be explained below; and by these points, the three previous questions are simultaneously resolved.

<margin>a. This is a title for Moses, the excellence of whose prophecy the Jews explain with a specific grammatical emphasis. You could also translate it as the "Prince of Prophets." b. Abarbanel has twisted us here <note>meaning: made the interpretation difficult</note>, though we believe we have followed his meaning very well. c. See Sanhedrin folio 89, 1 and Buxtorf’s Talmudic Lexicon page 1436. d. Or: interpreters <note>original Greek: ἐξηγητά (exēgētai)</note>.</margin>

### EXAMINATION.

Our author <note>Referring to the commentator being studied, likely a Christian scholar analyzing Abarbanel.</note> considers that the prerogative of Moses over other Prophets consisted, among other things, in the fact that God inspired him with the divine concepts and the very words simultaneously. For the rest of the prophets, He supposedly inspired the concepts alone, which were then to be expressed in either their own words or those of another Prophet; this also seems to be the opinion of Maimonides in *The Guide for the Perplexed, Part 2, Chapter 29.* In responding to Abarbanel, a certain person <note>original: "Nonnemo," likely referring to a specific but unnamed contemporary scholar.</note> fell into Scylla while wishing to avoid Charybdis <note>A classical metaphor for avoiding one danger only to fall into a worse one.</note>. Specifically, he denies that even for Moses all the words were divinely inspired, and asserts on page 214 that *the Holy Spirit permitted the sacred writers to use the words by which they would speak their revelations.* Indeed, he adds that individual things contained in Holy Scripture are not to be attributed to a specific divine revelation, citing George Calixtus <note>A 17th-century Lutheran theologian known for his "syncretism" and moderate views on inspiration.</note> in this sense. Thus, many people run into the opposite faults while trying to avoid vices. The response to the Rabbi should rather have been that the very words of the other Prophets were also "divinely inspired" <note>original Greek: θεόπνευστα (theopneusta), meaning "God-breathed."</note>, and that there is no special privilege for Moses here, as theologians clearly prove from Isaiah 51:16, Jeremiah 30:1, chapter 36:2, Psalm 45:2, 2 Samuel 23:2, etc. And if we say that the Prophets...

...that they brought forth certain things at their own discretion and in their own manner without divine inspiration, and likewise that certain things became known to them not through a special revelation but either through experience or the light of nature, then Scripture will not be the Word of God in all its parts—at least not in its substance. And since the two Prophets had to prophesy concerning the same matter, it pleased the Holy Spirit to inspire similar words in both.

## ABARBANEL.

Furthermore, in the exposition of this text: *We have heard a rumor from the Lord, and an ambassador is sent among the nations*, Rabbi David Kimchi <note>A celebrated medieval Jewish grammarian and commentator (c. 1160–1235) known as the Radak.</note> writes that it should be rearranged as follows: *The vision of Obadiah: We have heard a rumor from the Lord—namely, I and the rest of the Prophets who have prophesied about the desolation of Idumea; thus says the Lord God to Edom (that is, concerning Edom), that an ambassador has been sent among the nations*, where the spreading of the rumor is figuratively denoted, so that all nations might go up to war against Edom. But this exposition has no solidity, neither by reason of the coherence of the words—since it says: *And an ambassador was sent through the nations* (for how could it be said "And an ambassador," as if the Prophet were adding something to another speech, if this were the beginning of the speech?)—nor by reason of the matter itself: for how is it fitting for all kings to arm themselves for war against the land of Edom without cause? And what would be the business of this sent ambassador? And who would send him? Therefore, the true explanation must be sought from what I have indicated several times: that the Idumeans—by whom are understood all Christians, whose beginning and foundation were cast at Rome—will arm themselves to go up to lay waste to Jerusalem, where indeed is all the sanctity of religion and the tomb of the blessed Jesus, their God, just as they have already armed themselves several times to lay waste to those places for this very cause. # THE EXAMINATION.

One can find differing opinions regarding the position and sense of the first verse. Most attribute the words *We have heard a rumor from the Lord, etc.* to the Prophet and his colleagues, and thus they admit either a <term>parenthesis</term> <note>an explanatory word or phrase inserted into a passage</note>, a <term>hyperbaton</term> <note>an inversion of normal word order</note>, or an <term>asyndeton</term> <note>the omission of conjunctions between parts of a sentence</note> in the text. Those who believe the words of the first verse should be intercepted by a parenthesis include Ribera (no. 8), Emanuel Saa, Johannes Menochius, and Drusius; nor do D. Walther (in his *Harmony*, p. 706) and Cornelius à Lapide disagree: perhaps in this way: *Thus says the Lord GOD to Edom* (for we have also heard a rumor...

<summary>This page concludes Abarbanel's commentary on Obadiah 1:1, focusing on the geopolitical rumors of war, and begins a scholarly "Examination" of the verse's grammatical structure, citing various 17th-century Christian theologians and Hebraists.</summary>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the critical examination of Obadiah 1:1, focusing on the grammatical structure and the theological implications of the phrase "We have heard." The author compares various Christian interpretations against the Jewish commentary of Isaac Abarbanel.</meta>

# ON OBADIAH.

...heard, I and others, that a messenger has been sent by the Lord to the nations, saying: Arise, etc.) Behold, I have made you small, etc. Ribera, following Rupert, seeks a parallel example from *Acts 1:15*. <note>Francisco de Ribera and Rupert of Deutz were influential biblical commentators; Acts 1:15 describes Peter standing up among the disciples.</note> Arias Montanus, de Castro, and others recognize a <term>hyperbaton</term> <note>A rhetorical figure where the natural order of words is transposed.</note> here. Indeed, they think the words should be ordered thus: *The Vision of Obadiah: We have heard a report from the Lord and a messenger has been sent among the nations, etc. Thus says the Lord to Edom: Behold, I have appointed you small, etc.* Although our author <note>Referring to Isaac Abarbanel.</note> rejects another hyperbaton suggested by Kimhi, he nevertheless clearly hints that the words: *Thus says the Lord*, should be connected immediately with the second verse, while he says the first verse contains what Obadiah saw, and the second and following verses contain what he heard. Nor are there lacking those who, by conveniently supplying an <term>asyndeton</term> <note>The omission of conjunctions between parts of a sentence.</note> or a slight <term>ellipsis</term> <note>The omission of words understood by context.</note>, connect the Prophet’s words in this way: *Thus says the Lord: I also and the rest of my contemporary prophets have heard a report, etc.*—as Tarnovius suggests; or: *Thus says the Lord, and we know this because we have heard a report, etc.*—as Jacobus Tirinus suggests; or: *Thus says the Lord, and such a report we have heard, etc.*—as others feel. But the opinion of Cocceius <note>Johannes Cocceius, a 17th-century theologian known for his "Covenant Theology."</note>, which attributes the words of the first verse, just as they lie, to the <term>Word</term> <note>original Greek: λόγῳ (logō)</note> and the Holy Spirit, seems to us not to be rejected. It is analogous to the faith, preserves the natural series of the words, does not destroy the <term>continuity</term> <note>original Greek: συνέχειαν (synecheian)</note> of the text, sharply presses against the Jews or those who favor Jewish interpretations, and would not have displeased Jerome, Eusebius, or others. 

So the sense here is: *Thus says the Lord GOD (the Triune God) to Edom:* a <term>report</term> <note>original Hebrew: שְׁמוּעָה (shemuah)</note> or <term>hearing</term> <note>original Greek: ἀκοὴ (akoē)</note> (that is, a decree made in the most holy council: <term>"Let us make"</term> <note>original Hebrew: נַעֲשֶׂה (na'aseh); a reference to the plural language in Genesis 1:26.</note> and the <term>watchers</term> <note>original Hebrew: עִירִין (irin) from Daniel 4:14.</note>) *we have heard* (I, the <term>Word</term> <note>original Greek: λόγος (logos)</note>, conscious of and the interpreter of all the secrets of the divinity, <term>speaking what I have seen with the Father</term> <note>original Greek: λαλῶν ὃ ἑώρακα παρὰ τῷ πατρὶ; reference to John 8:38 and 3:11.</note>, and the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father, John 15:26, <term>because he will take from what is mine and declare it</term> <note>original Greek: ὅτι ἐκ τοῦ ἐμοῦ λήψεται καὶ ἀναγγελεῖ; John 16:14-15.</note>) *from the Lord* (from **GOD** the Father, as in Genesis 19:24) *and a messenger has been sent among the nations, etc.* 

Certainly, our author <note>Abarbanel</note> had felt the force of this passage; hence on *page 11* he proposed such a doubt: *How can God say of Himself: "We have heard a report," in the plural? And that He heard it from the Lord? As if He were constituted outside of Himself. If indeed these words are the Prophet's concerning himself, how can he say: "Thus says the Lord"?* Whether his twisted explanation by a mere <term>antithesis</term> <note>original Greek: ἀντίθεσις (antithesis)</note> satisfies this doubt, let the judgment be in the power of all wise men. The Rabbi would have ceased to wonder at the Lord speaking of Himself in the plural and hearing from the Lord, if he had believed the mystery of the Trinity. Indeed, de Castro also thinks it stands against this opinion that Jeremiah in chapter 49 says in the singular: *I have heard*; although even there the <term>Word</term> <note>original Greek: λόγος (logos)</note> alone might not inconveniently be said to speak. But let theologians discuss this matter more fully. We must now look both into the author of the war, the herald, and the war itself...

<meta>catchword: ob-</meta>

<summary>The author analyzes the plural phrasing "We have heard" in Obadiah 1:1, debating whether it refers to a group of prophets or provides evidence for the Christian doctrine of the Trinity (Father, Word, and Spirit).</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological debate regarding Obadiah 1:1, specifically focusing on the causes of the war against Edom and the identity of the "messenger" mentioned in the text. It transitions from grammatical analysis to a discussion of divine providence versus human agency.</meta>

# EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

...object. The more remote cause of war is, without doubt, God himself, since wars and the overthrows of kingdoms do not happen by chance or by the blind impulse of fortune, in the way that the atoms of *Democritus* <note>Democritus was an ancient Greek philosopher who proposed that the universe was made of tiny, indivisible particles called atoms moving randomly.</note> are driven (which are the words of *B. Gesner* <note>Balthasar Gesner, a Lutheran theologian.</note>), but **God** himself sends them, and indeed by a just judgment because of the preceding sins of men (*Daniel 4:14; Sirach 10:8*). It is also certain that **God** exercises his vengeance upon impious nations through other impious men and tyrants, as instruments for executing his wrath. Hence *Assyria* is called the rod of his fury (*Isaiah 10:5*); *Nebuchadnezzar* his servant (*Jeremiah 25:9*); and *Cyrus* his anointed (*Isaiah 45:1*). Therefore, although the proximate cause of the war to be brought against the Edomites was *Nebuchadnezzar*, nevertheless **God** himself, as the primary cause, is said here to conscript soldiers, send heralds, sound the trumpet-call, and draw up the battle line. Compare *Isaiah 5:26 and following*. Furthermore, concerning the "messenger" sent among the nations: to Jerome, this is the mediator of **God** and men, *Christ Jesus*, the <note>original: "Ἀρχιστράτηγος"</note> Commander-in-chief of his people (*Joshua 5:14; Daniel 12:1*). To Hugo, Castro, Grotius, and *B. Gesner*, it is an angel, either as an inciter or an executor (as in *Exodus 12:29; Psalm 78:49; Isaiah 37:36*, etc.). To Lyranus, Ribera, à Lapide, Menochius, Mercerus, Our Author <note>Likely referring to a specific Christian commentator the text follows, or the primary opponent of Abarbanel in this context.</note>, and others, it refers to a secret instinct from **God** and a movement stirred up among the nations to take up arms against the Edomites. To us, along with Albertus, *Arias Montanus*, the Vinarienses, and others, he seems to be a herald of the Babylonian King, or his sent legates, who were to conscript soldiers from various peoples subject to the King and declare war on the Edomites. Moreover, regarding the words: *Arise, let us rise against her for war*, which Cyril, Aben Ezra, Kimchi, Our Author, *Arias Montanus*, Vatablus, Menochius, Sa, and Ribera wish to be the words of the confederated nations encouraging one another to aggressively attack the war; others say they are the words of the legate stirring up the nations; others say they belong to both the legate and the nations together, so that he says: *Arise*, and they respond: *We shall rise for war*. We consider these to be the words of **God** himself through the messenger, animating the nations to attack the enemies and, as it were, enlisting them as <note>original: "συνέργους"</note> co-workers. Thus, the sense of *verse 1* is: *Thus says the Triune God to Edom: We have heard the report or the proclamation of the fatal decree against you—I the <note>original: "λόγος"</note> Word and the Holy Spirit from the Lord **God** the Father—nay, a messenger has already been sent by us through Nebuchadnezzar among the nations, to say: Rise, let us rise up together against her* (that is, the <gloss>daughter</gloss> or <gloss>land</gloss> of Edom, especially its metropolis Bozrah, *Jeremiah 49:13, 14*) *for war*. But Our Author <note>Referring here to Isaac Abarbanel.</note> differs <note>original: "διὰ παντὸς" (entirely/at all points)</note> entirely from this explanation, and he thinks so absurdly about the object of the war that no one could be more foolish. He wrote his commentary on Obadiah in 1505 or 1506 AD, when Jerusalem was <column-break/> still...

It also surveys various scholarly interpretations of the "messenger" in Obadiah 1:1, ranging from Christ or angels to human heralds.</summary>

# ON OBADIAH

...at this point, it would be under the power of the Sultan of Egypt. He <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a Jewish statesman and philosopher whom the author is critiquing.</note> predicts, therefore, that the Christians will be the first to seize Jerusalem from the Egyptians, and that the news of this deed will be spread among the Ishmaelite <note>The author uses "Ishmaelite" as a common historical term for Muslim nations.</note> nations. These nations, ignited by zeal and seeking to avenge the slaughter of the Egyptians—to whom they are bound by the bond of religion—would then attack Jerusalem (now occupied by the Christians) or assault the Christians themselves. These fantasies and delirious dreams of the Rabbi have already been abundantly refuted by actual events, as we shall now show **evidently** <note>original: "ὀφθαλμοφανῶς"</note>.

No one until now, except for Our Author <note>Likely referring to Abarbanel, the target of this critique.</note>, has ever asserted that the city of Jerusalem is the object of this war, unless we consider Cocceius <note>Johannes Cocceius, a 17th-century Dutch theologian.</note>, who claims that by "Bozrah" <note>An ancient city in Edom.</note> one should understand Jerusalem—but a Jerusalem inhabited by Jews and Edomites—who might respond to the Rabbi according to his own hypothesis. We, on the contrary, prove invincibly against Our Author that the Jerusalem inhabited by Christians cannot be the object of this war.

Ancient Jerusalem before *Titus* <note>The Roman Emperor who destroyed the Second Temple in 70 AD.</note>, as far as can be gathered from sacred and other histories, was occupied twenty-two times (but never by Christians), and was finally completely cut off by *Titus*. It remained a "den of thieves" for sixty years until the Emperor *Aelius Hadrian* <note>Hadrian, who suppressed the Bar Kokhba revolt.</note> tore down the remaining ruins from their foundations and, in 132 AD, founded a new town. He called it *Aelia Capitolina* after his own name. Later, around the time of *Constantine the Great*, the Christians restored the name of Jerusalem to it. Compare Eusebius, *Church History*, Book 4, Chapter 6; Calvisius, *Chronology*, page 475; Christ. Heidmann, *Palestine*, Chapter 2, Section 135, 137, and following; Alsted, *Chronological Treasury*, page 272 and following; Henr. Bunting, *Travels through Holy Scripture*, page 50 and following.

This new Jerusalem, under the leadership of Godfrey of Bouillon <note>A leader of the First Crusade.</note>, did indeed later become a royal seat of Christians in 1099 AD. However, that kingdom could not complete its hundredth year, for it was completely extinguished by Saladin, the Sultan of Egypt, in 1187 AD. And although in 1228 AD *Frederick II* recovered the city, it was later snatched from him in 1246 AD by Casano, King of the Tatars, and from him by the Sultan of Egypt in 1263 AD, who held it during the time of Abarbanel and beyond.

Our "new prophet" <note>A sarcastic reference to Abarbanel.</note> therefore predicts that the Christians, as they were several times accustomed to doing, would attack Jerusalem and thus the Egyptians, and would thereby bring fatal destruction upon themselves. But the actual event, turning out quite the opposite, has scattered these omens, so that there remains for Our Author the punishment appointed for false prophets in *Deuteronomy 18:21 and following*. For when Abarbanel—as testified by Rabbi David Ganz—went "to the lowest parts of the earth" <note>A poetic way of saying he died.</note> at Padua in the year of the world 5269 (that is, 1509 AD), eight years later Jerusalem was taken—not by Christians—but by the Turkish Emperor Suleiman <note>Historical note: While the text names "Suleiman" (Suleiman the Magnificent), the conquest of Jerusalem from the Egyptian Mamluks actually occurred in 1516-1517 under his father, Selim I.</note> from the Egyptians, along with the entire kingdom of Egypt...

<meta>catchword: ...snatched</meta>

The author argues that Abarbanel’s predictions regarding a Christian reconquest of Jerusalem and a subsequent retaliatory war by Muslim nations were proven false by the Ottoman conquest of the city in the early 16th century.</summary>

# THE EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

...it was seized by the Turks and is possessed to this very day and the current year of 1666. Therefore, what the Egyptians do not possess cannot be snatched from them by the Christians. Away, then, with this fiction of Abarbanel, along with that other one regarding *Ezekiel* <note>The author cites "Ezech. 183. c. 4.," which likely refers to a specific page in Abarbanel’s commentary on Ezekiel rather than a biblical verse.</note> concerning the forty years during which, according to the tradition of the ancients, the Jews are to wander in the "desert of the nations" before the appearance of the Messiah. He sets the beginning of this period in the year of the world 5224 (1464 AD) and its end in the year 5261 (1505 AD)—the very year in which he wrote those fictions of his, dying four years later. From that time, he believed the liberation of Israel would begin. He adds, however, that it is possible *that its beginning might be the year of the world 5265 (1505 AD) and its completion in the year 5291 (1531 AD).*

But, by my Christ, how far from the mark this little false-prophet <note>The author uses the derogatory term "Prophetaster."</note> has wandered! The Jews continue to wander to this very day, and since the year 1464, not forty, but more than five times forty—that is, two hundred years—have passed, yet no hope of a temporal liberation has shone upon the Jews. Indeed, there have been those wretched men who, ever since they rejected the Prince of Life <note>A common Christian title for Jesus.</note>, have "sold smoke and dyes" <note>original: "fumos fucosvé venderent" — a Latin idiom meaning to sell deceptions or empty promises.</note> to those convinced of the Messiah's coming. But these impostors have always been the cause of either a piteous slaughter or a distinguished financial fine.

For example (not to speak now of *Theudas* and *Judas the Galilean* <note>Two figures mentioned in the New Testament (Acts 5) as failed revolutionary leaders.</note>), when they received *Bar Kokhba* <note>Leader of the Jewish rebellion against Rome (132–136 AD). His name means "Son of a Star," referencing a messianic prophecy in Numbers 24:17.</note> under Hadrian around the year 120 AD—who arrogated that name to himself from *Numbers 24:17*—he rebelled against the Romans with a most unhappy outcome. He made Betar, a city in Palestine, the seat of his kingdom, and several tens of thousands of Jews fell there. On this matter, beyond the Talmudists, one may further read *Juchasin* <note>The "Book of Lineage," a historical work by Abraham Zacuto.</note>, *Zemach David* <note>"Sprout of David," a chronicle by David Gans.</note>, *Meor Enajim* <note>"Light of the Eyes" by Azariah dei Rossi.</note>, Scaliger’s notes on the *Chronicle of Eusebius*, Genebrard’s *Chronology*, Buxtorf’s *Talmudic Lexicon* and *Jewish Synagogue*, Galatinus, Samuel Petit, Gerson’s *Talmud*, and Joseph de Voisin in his notes on the *Dagger of Faith* by Raymond Martini.

Likewise, in the time of Theodosius the Younger, a certain *Pseudo-Moses of Crete*, having promised that he would lead his fellow tribesmen through the sea back to their ancient lands, drowned a great multitude of people in the waves, as *Socrates* and *Nicephorus* relate. Similarly, in the time of Leo the Isaurian, a certain *Syrian* impostor calling himself the Messiah miserably seduced many, as *Baronius* reports from *Theophanes*. Then there was the Pseudo-Messiah *David El David* <note>Commonly known as David Alroy, a 12th-century messianic claimant in Persia.</note> who, around 1139 AD, was said to have freed himself from prison through magical arts and to have crossed the River Gozan by spreading out his cloak. He was rumored to have traveled a ten-day journey in a single day. When he was later captured, in order to escape a more grievous punishment, he requested that his head be cut off as a sign, claiming that he would return to life on the third day...

...[liberation which he] promised, but until now it lies in the shadow of death; the Jews were fined a great sum of money, as Maimonides <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher.</note> relates in his *Epistle* found in Buxtorf <note>original: "Epist. ap. Buxt." Johannes Buxtorf was a famous Christian Hebraist; the text cited is likely Maimonides’ "Letter to the Yemenites."</note> page 444, and in the *Shevet Yehuda* <note>The "Scepter of Judah," a 16th-century history of Jewish persecutions.</note> folio 25b. Around the same time, there was another **False Messiah** in Persia who, relying solely on his initial success, said there was no need for other proofs to establish faith in his messiahship, as can be read in the same work at folio 26.2. When he had cheated the hope of the Jews, they were fined money and, moreover, for the sake of mockery, were forced to walk barefooted and without stockings and to carry long logs of wood.

So too another, around the beginnings of the **Ismaelite kingdom** <note>The author uses "Ismaelite" to refer to the early Islamic Caliphate.</note>, according to the testimony of Maimonides, gave a sign that he would be a leper at night and in his sleep, but beautiful in the morning and during the day; he became the cause of a harsher captivity. Another False Messiah in France in the year 1155, by the same witness, destroyed himself and his entire assembly. Likewise, around 1167, **David Al-Muffer** in Moravia, carrying himself as the Messiah and attempting to make himself invisible through magical tricks, was finally killed, and the Jews were fined money. Concerning **David Elroi**, a False Messiah finally killed by his father-in-law in 1169, see *Rabbi Benjamin in his Itinerary* <note>Benjamin of Tudela, a 12th-century traveler.</note>.

And in 1175, at Cordoba, a False Messiah was the cause of ruin for nearly all the Jews in Spain, as is related in the *Shalshelet Ha-Kabbalah* <note>A 16th-century Jewish historical and genealogical work.</note> folio 45. **Rabbi Lemmlein** in the year 1500 predicted that the Messiah would arrive in that very year; he was the author of the advice to the people that they should tear down their ovens, because he considered it most certain that in the following year they would be baking their cakes in the Holy Land, as narrated in the *Shalshelet* at the place cited and in *Tzemach David* <note>"The Plant of David," a chronicle by David Gans.</note> page 150.

In the year 1534, a little man named **Rabbi David Reubeni** said he was sent by the Ten Tribes living beyond the Caspian Mountains to the Pope and the King of Portugal to seek assistance; just as in 1644 **Aaron Levi**, otherwise known as **Antonio de Montezinos**, told Menasseh Ben Israel and others in Amsterdam that those same tribes were hiding in America.

But concerning those False Messiahs, consult also Gerson’s *Talmud* in the cited place, D. Müller’s *Judaism* book 1, chapter 6, Schickard’s *Jewish Royal Law* near the end, Vorstius on the *Tzemach* page 292 and following, the Distinguished Gentius’ *History of the Jews* page 169, Hoornbeek’s *On Convicting the Jews* book 3, chapter 1, and others.

Truly, as the vanity of man is beyond belief once he has begun to rave, we hear that the Jews, though deceived so many times, are not yet wise, but even today they follow some impostor and petty prophet <note>The author uses the derogatory Latin "Prophetastrum."</note> **Rabbi Nathan Levi** <note>This refers to Nathan of Gaza, the prophet of the contemporary messianic claimant Sabbatai Zevi.</note> in the East, and again conceive a most vain hope from which they have been cast down so often. These people, perhaps impatient for peace, will again bring trouble upon their own beast of burden <note>A Latin idiom meaning they are bringing disaster upon themselves by their own actions.</note>. We Christians certainly have as many arguments for the correctness of our faith <note>original: "ορθοδοξίας" (orthodoxy)</note> regarding the arrival of the Messiah as the Jews have accepted False Messiahs. It has happened to them just as our Messiah, blessed forever, predicted long ago in John chapter 5...

# EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL
<note>The Latin title is "Examen Abarbenelis." Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508) was a Jewish statesman and philosopher whose commentaries often contained sharp critiques of Christian interpretation.</note>

<note>The original printer noted that tight binding obscured some characters; these have been reconstructed based on context.</note>

John 5:43: *I came in the name of my Father and you did not receive me: If another comes in his own name, him you will receive.* But where are we drifting? We must return to **Our Author** <note>Referring to Abarbanel.</note>; shall we address his opinion, or rather his fiction, by which he strives to prove that a slaughter is to be inflicted upon the Egyptians by Christians, based on Ezekiel 30:9? Here we would wonder even more at the man's extreme impudence, were it not that the Talmudic saying from the tractate *Beitzah* (folio 25, column 2) were known to us:
<note>original: שלשה עזין הן ישראל באומות כלב בחיות תרנגול בעופות</note>
"There are three impudent <note>Or "bold/brazen."</note> ones: the Jew among the nations, the dog among the animals, and the rooster among the birds." For regarding which enemies of Egypt the Prophet actually speaks, he hints in the immediately following passage: *I will take away the multitude of Egypt by Nebuchadnezzar King of Babylon.* **O soulless body of a Rabbi!** <note>original: ὁ ἄψυχος σῶμα Rabbini! A Greek-Latin hybrid insult suggesting the Rabbi's interpretation lacks any living spirit or reason.</note> Finally, that Jerusalem is not the object of the war predicted by Obadiah is clearly evident to the eye from Jeremiah, who in chapter 49:13 names **Bozrah** <note>An ancient city of Edom.</note> explicitly, against which verse 14 implies the war is directed. However, **Our Author** <note>Abarbanel.</note> understands Bozrah to mean **Rome**, from which we argue: Bozrah is not Jerusalem. Bozrah is the object of this war. Therefore, the object of this war is not Jerusalem. With this point defeated, all the delusions built upon such a ruinous foundation collapse of their own accord.

## ABARBANEL.

*Verse 1: BEHOLD, I HAVE MADE YOU SMALL AMONG THE NATIONS etc., through to verse 10: BECAUSE OF THE WRONG DONE TO YOUR BROTHER JACOB etc. Interpreters comment on the words: "Behold, I have made you small among the nations," that the past tense is used in place of the future; namely, that Edom will be small at the time of its future desolation in the number of its men, and despised and esteemed as nothing in comparison to the nations that will come against it, whereas once the pride of its heart seduced it to go up against the land of Israel—as if it dwelt in the clefts of the rock and its seat was on high, from which it might say in its heart: "Who will cast me down to the ground?" But it will not happen thus (says God) as you think. For though you exalt yourself like an eagle in your excellence and place your nest and seat among the stars, yet I will cast you down from there when the day of your desolation comes. So too they note regarding the words: "When thieves come to you" etc., that here also the past tense is used in place of the future, so that the sense is that spectators seeing Edom in ruins will wonder and say: "What has happened to you? If thieves had plundered you—"*

# ON OBADIAH

...*if they had been there at night, where you had slept and noticed nothing, so that you could not have stopped them or risen up against them—would they not have stolen only as much as was enough for them and as much as they could carry, and yet left something behind? And likewise: If men like grape-gatherers in a vineyard had come to you, how could it be that they would not leave behind some gleanings? How is it that no one remains as a survivor from among you? How were the most hidden things of* Esau* thoroughly searched out, sought after, and shaken out, so that the enemies did not leave anything, even of his most hidden treasures? Regarding what is said:* <note>original: נדמית — Hebrew: "nidmeta," meaning "you are destroyed" or "cut off."</note> *it signifies to them: You are cut off, just as in that passage from* Hosea 4:6: <note>original: "Excisus est populus meus, ut non agnoscant" — My people are cut off (or destroyed) for lack of knowledge.</note> *Furthermore, your friends, in whom you trusted, brought help to your enemies; for all your confederates and* <term>salt-companions</term> <note>original: "consalini" — those with whom you shared salt or meals; intimate allies.</note> *in whom you trusted, drove you to the borders while you felt nothing of it. For they pretended to apply a bandage and medicine to your wound, yet there was no wisdom in you to understand their trickery; for on that day the wise men shall perish from the mountain of* Edom *and understanding from the mountain of* Esau*. This is the method of the interpreters, and it is indeed to be approved, if only you are willing to allow the Past tense to be explained by the Future. Nevertheless, it seems better to me in these passages of Scripture to explain the Past as Past and the Future as Future.*

*Now, I have already shown several times that from* Idumea <note>The biblical land of Edom, located south of the Dead Sea.</note>*, which is neighbor to the land of Israel, there came forth Kings who reigned in* Italy *and who built* Great Rome*, a leader among the nations and a princess among provinces; and thus all of* Italy *and* Greece *was filled with* Idumeans*, as were all the Western Provinces. Accordingly, the Prophets call that entire nation by the name of* Edom *and* Esau*, because such was the beginning of its lineage. Thus they name as* Idumeans *both those whose land was near the land of Israel, and the* Romans*, who arose and were drawn from them; so that all of them together and each of them individually appear in* Holy Scripture <note>original: "S. Sa." standing for "Sacra Scriptura."</note> *under the name of* Edom*. Therefore, what the Prophet says: "Behold, I have made you small among the nations," can be explained regarding the mountain of* Esau*, from which the* Idumean *Kings came forth. For that was a small land and its people were few, whom the Kings of* Judah*, the* Maccabean *Kings, and the Kings of the family of* Herod *subjugated during the time of the Second Temple, to such an extent that the* Idumean *kingdom was small among the nations and greatly despised. But after so many princes and peoples had come forth from there and spread themselves through* Italy*,* Greece*, and all the Western Provinces, they grew and were increased and strengthened beyond measure, and* Roman Kings *from E—*

...arising from the house: and from here the pride of their heart seduced them, so that they wished to subject the whole world to themselves. But it can also be said that he speaks specifically of Rome itself, <margin>e.</margin> which he calls Edom for the reasons we have previously mentioned, when he says: *Behold, I have made you small among the nations.* For Rome from the beginning was a small thing, indeed a dwelling of shepherds and a land for livestock; but afterward it was inhabited and became a small city, and its affairs reached such a point that it said: *I am, and there is no one besides me* (Isaiah 47:8). Therefore he says: *The pride of your heart has deceived you, as if you lived in the clefts of the rock,* in a high and lofty seat, from which you might say: *Who will cast me down to the <margin>a.</margin> ground?* But it shall not happen so. For though you exalt yourself like the eagle, which is the bird flying higher than any other even to the highest region of the air, and though you place your nest among the stars, which are eternal and incorruptible, behold, I will nevertheless cast you down from there, says the <margin>d.</margin> Lord, even to the lowest hell. So also Jeremiah says of Edom in chapter 49, verses 16 and 17: *Your terrifying power has deceived you, and the pride of your heart... And Edom shall become a desolation,* etc. But the whole matter comes back to the same point.

<margin>a.</margin> Therefore the following words are to be attributed through <term>prosopopeia</term> <note>original: "prosopopœjan" — a rhetorical device where an imagined or absent person is represented as speaking.</note> to the onlookers of the ruin, not to the Prophet; this is the opinion of David Kimchi on this passage. <margin>b.</margin> Specifically regarding Isaiah 34 and 35, to Jeremiah, and so forth. <margin>c.</margin> They are called the Hasmonean Kings, who are elsewhere called the Maccabees. Regarding the name, consult Buxtorf, *Talmudic Lexicon*, page 844; Scaliger, *On the Emendation of Time*, page 410; L’Empereur on *Middoth*, page 26; Fuller, *Miscellanea*, book 2, chapter 13; Hottinger, *Philological Treasury*, book 2, chapter 2, section 1, page 519; Mayer, *Sacred Philology*, part 1, page 148; and others. <margin>d.</margin> The Hebrew word <note>original: כיפה — "kipa."</note> could also be translated as "rock," but the sense seems more fitting if it is rendered as "highest branch" or "summit" (see *Talmudic Lexicon*, page 1075). <margin>e.</margin> Namely, that it was founded by the Idumaeans, as he had said before.

### EXAMINATION.

Regarding the second verse, the Interpreters split into two parties. Some wish it to contain a threat, and thus the <term>past tense</term> <note>original: "Præteritum" — the perfect tense, often used in Hebrew prophecy to indicate the certainty of a future event as if it had already happened.</note> is used instead of the future to indicate the certainty of the prophecy, so that the sense is: I will shake out the pride with which you swell, for I will most certainly cause you to be the most contemptible of all. So say Rabbi David Kimchi, Lyranus, Rupertus, Vatablus, Ribe...
