# ON OBADIAH.

*Emmanuel Saa, Oecolampadius, Drusius, Junius,* and also *B. Tarnovius.*
But others, without such a <term>grammatical shift</term> <note>original: "enallage" — a rhetorical or grammatical substitution of one form for another, such as tense or person</note>, prefer to interpret the *reproach and emphasizing of pride* in this sense: *Look, you are so obscure and ignoble among other nations and have no reason for insolence, yet you carry yourself no differently than if you were invincible.* Thus say *Theodoret, Remigius, Arias Montanus, à Castro, à Lapide, Mercer, Rodolph Gualther, Paraeus, the Belgians,* as well as *B. Gesner.* But while that <term>grammatical shift</term> is not so inconvenient—rather, it must necessarily be admitted later on—nevertheless we concede to *Abarbanel* that here the <term>past tense</term> <note>original: "Præteritum"</note> can more conveniently be retained. Yet regarding his opinion on the object and fulfillment of this and the following sections, we protest with full voices <note>original: "plenis tibiis reclamamus" — literally "we cry out with full flutes," an idiom meaning to disagree strongly and loudly</note>. Specifically, he leaves a choice to the reader: whether one wants to understand the Edomite nation, which was small and lowly at the beginning but grew and became proud after leading colonies into Italy, Greece, or elsewhere; or indeed the city of Rome, which, having progressed from small beginnings, became the head of the world. 

But 1. How could he satisfy us, who is inconsistent with himself and, fluctuating strangely, measures the sense of Holy Scripture by his own uncertain conjectures? 2. Although this prophecy could be applied in its own way to Rome—which was once a village and later, having been brought into the form of a city by Romulus, grew to the highest peak—it is not to be explained as referring to it; and although <note>original: "$\gamma\nu\eta\sigma\iota\omega\varsigma$" — genuinely</note> it would take nothing away from Christians if we were to say even here, with *Mercer*, that the Antichrist and his seat are targeted, nevertheless the sense, connection, and scope of the words, as well as the outcome, suggest otherwise. 3. Since we have already cut the sinews <note>shattered the strength of the argument</note> of all the small arguments of *Abarbanel* and others for an Edomite origin of the Italians in the preface, we do not wish to dwell longer on this absurd commentary. Indeed, with more right, we could throw this <note>original: "$\epsilon\gamma\kappa\lambda\eta\mu\alpha$" — accusation or charge</note> back at the authors themselves, along with *Cocceius* on this passage. However, it will suffice for us on this occasion to have opposed a <note>original: "$\dot{\alpha}\xi\iota\sigma\kappa\acute{\epsilon}\psi\iota\nu$" — worthwhile consideration</note> of the genuine sense to *Abarbanel’s* <note>original: "$\dot{\alpha}\beta\lambda\epsilon\psi\acute{\iota}\alpha$" — lack of sight or oversight</note>. 

The Lord says: **BEHOLD**, I mean you, Edomite nation, you, O Edom: what reason do you have for being fierce? You are not ignorant of the lessening of your status and the loss of the birthright of your prideful parent Esau (Genesis 21 verse 23; chapter 27, verse 31): You, following in your father's footsteps, **I HAVE PLACED SMALL AMONG THE NATIONS**, (that is, the smallest of nations, since the Hebrews usually express the superlative with such a <term>circumlocution</term> <note>original: "periphrasi"</note>. Compare *Glassius Philologia* book 3, tract 1, chapter 16, page 417.) In the division of the land among the peoples (Deuteronomy 32 verse 8), I assigned to you the sterile and rugged mountains of Seir (Deuteronomy 2 verse 12), a region not only enclosed by small borders but also deserted, harsh, and inaccessible (Malachi 1 verse 3). ### EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

...it is either of the Swiss or of those who inhabit the Bohemian Alps in the neighborhood, as [Gesner] says. The Idumaeans were like our Alpine people (specifically in Italy, according to Cornelius à Lapide), and since you are small in number, wealth, and strength, you are also **GREATLY DESPISED**, inglorious and obscure, always exposed to the plunder of neighbors (Malachi 1:4). But however small you may be, you believe yourself to be something great, and the more you are depressed, the more you "raise your crests" <note>A common idiom for becoming proud or boastful</note>. For nevertheless the **PRIDE OF YOUR HEART** (Grotius: *Your idol. That idol* was called *kobe* <note>original Greek: κὁβὲ</note>, announcing destruction to enemies but falling against them instead. However, Grotius looked to the parallel passage in Jeremiah 49:16, where *your terribleness* <note>original Hebrew: תפלצתך - tiphlatsecha</note> is added; yet it rather denotes the horror which the Idumaeans falsely thought they struck into other nations) **HAS DECEIVED YOU** (The Vulgate: *has lifted you up*, reading it with the letter *shin*, as *your lifting up* <note>original Hebrew: שיאתך - shi'atacha</note>, although in Jeremiah 49:16 it translates: *has deceived*) so that you exalt yourself, O **YOU WHO DWELL** (in the Hebrew word *shokeni* <note>original: שוכני</note>, which for Kimchi, Buxtorf in his *Thesaurus p. 103*, Drusius, and others is a <term>paragogic</term> <note>an extra letter added to the end of a word for emphasis or style, common in biblical poetry</note> form, and for Mercer it is emphatic and connoting habit; it could also be a first-person suffix for *ni* as in *seeing me*, *finding me*, *sending me*, *digging me*, etc., though of personal significance, as the 3rd person suffix in *his love* in Ecclesiastes 7:13, but the first view seems truer) **IN THE CLEFTS** (the Greek Septuagint translates: *in holes* <note>original Greek: ἐν ὀπαῖς</note> or caverns, rather than in "circuits" or "circles" from the root *chug* according to Kimchi, or in "terror" according to Rashi) **OF THE ROCK**, in caves, in which, according to Jerome, the Idumaeans had little dwellings because of the heat of the sun; or one who built his cities and fortresses on steep cliffs and inaccessible crags, and from there **IN THE HEIGHT OF HIS DWELLING** (<term>Hypallage</term> <note>a figure of speech where the natural relations of words are shifted, here meaning "his highest dwelling"</note>), on the highest peak of the rock, **SAYING** boastfully **IN HIS HEART**, that is, thinking (as in Psalm 4:4 and Psalm 10:6), **WHO SHALL CAST ME DOWN TO THE GROUND?** 

This is no different than Arimazes, who, holding an inaccessible rock in the region of Sogdiana with 30,000 armed men, was asked by Alexander the Great to surrender. He mocked him and asked: *Can Alexander fly?* as Curtius records in Book 7. But truly, why do I linger on those caverns of yours: **THOUGH YOU LIFT UP** (namely your nest, a noun which must be assumed <term>in common</term> <note>original: "apo koinou" — a rhetorical device where a word from a later clause is understood to apply to the current one</note> from the following words, as is clear from Jeremiah) **LIKE AN EAGLE** (which flies and nests higher than other birds, as Pliny testifies, whence it is said to "seek the heavens" in Proverbs 23:5 and to nest in the highest mountains in Job 39:27) indeed, even though **YOU SET** (the Hebrew *shim* <note>original: שים</note> "to set" is an <term>apheretic infinitive</term> <note>a shortened form of a verb missing its first syllable, used here to represent a future action</note> used for the future) your nest **AMONG THE STARS** (it is all the same whether you understand this as the highest mountains, whose peaks seem to hide among the stars, or as a hyperbolic hypothesis referring to the stars themselves).

## ABARBENEL.

But when he further says: *If thieves come to you, etc.* <note>Referring to Obadiah 1:5</note> If we wish to understand *Edom*, whom he mentions, as the *Idumaea* near *Jerusalem*, it is implied that this *Idumaea* was devastated many times—namely by *David*, the Kings of *Judah*, and others, likewise by *Nebuchadnezzar* and the Kings during the time of the Second Temple, who brought many evils upon it, but did not overturn it fundamentally, for it was always left inhabited. It is a wonder, however, how the things of *Esau* <note>Esau is the biblical ancestor of the Edomites</note> were searched out in those desolations made by them, and how accurately they investigated his treasures and secrets, which previously lay hidden from those enemies.

### EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

<meta>The catchword from the previous page was "Quo"; the first word here "remus" completes the phrase "Quocirca diceremus" (Therefore we would say).</meta>

[...Therefore, we would] say that Rabbi Abarbanel is a rabid beast, if he were imagined to be irrational. However, let us follow in his footsteps, even if unwillingly. He says that if **Idumaea** <note>The biblical region of Edom, south of the Dead Sea</note> bordering **Palestine** is understood here, the Prophet's mind is that it was indeed often seriously desolated, but never totally. But, he adds, if **Rome** is understood, the goal is that although it was captured and plundered by various peoples—such as the **Gauls** (in 385 BC), the **Goths** (especially under King **Alaric** in 410 AD and **Totila** in 550 AD), and the Westerners (such as by the Christian Emperor **Frederick Barbarossa** in 1167 AD and, after Abarbanel's death, by **Charles V** <note>The Sack of Rome in 1527</note>; for by "Westerners," our author [Abarbanel] understands any other Christians, as can be seen from his *Commentary on Ezekiel, page 198, column 1*)—it was nonetheless never fundamentally destroyed.

But let us commit a *latter before the former* <note>original Greek: ὕστερον πρότερον (hysteron proteron); a rhetorical device where the last thing is put first</note>: the Rabbi's latter hypothesis [regarding Rome] has already been completely overturned by us. We concede the former hypothesis [regarding Idumaea], yet we do not equally approve of Abarbanel’s *Thesis*. For he thinks that all the unfortunate fates of the Idumaeans, except for the final **utter destruction** <note>original Greek: πανωλεθρία (panolethria)</note>, are presented here in one single embrace; we, led by better reasoning, judge that **Nebuchadnezzar's** expedition against the Idumaeans is specifically predicted.

In this, almost all interpreters agree **unanimously** <note>original Greek: ὁμοθυμαδὸν (homothymadon)</note>, except that some, such as the Blessed **Winckelmann** of Weimar and others, explain the "thieves and grape-gatherers" metaphorically as the **Chaldeans** <note>The Babylonians</note>; others take them literally, so that through a certain **contrast** <note>original Greek: ἀντίθεσιν (antithesin)</note> it might be suggested that the condition of the Idumaeans would have been more tolerable if they had been plundered by thieves or bandits than it will be when the Chaldeans arrive. So say Saint **Jerome**; **Castro**, **a Lapide**, **Ribera**, **Tirinus**; **Calvin**, **Oecolampadius**, **Junius**, **Drusius**, **Mercer**, **Gualther**, **Paraeus**, the **Dutch** [translators], the Blessed **Gesner**, **Tarnovius**, and others.

But let us look a little more deeply into the context itself. That is, the Prophet wishes to say: You boast gloriously of your strength, even though you are most fearful. For **IF THIEVES CAME TO YOU** (invaded you) or **IF NIGHT BANDITS, HOW YOU WOULD FALL SILENT!** You would not dare to shout even at these thieves of yours, but instead, feigning a deep sleep out of fear, you would lie silent and allow them to carry away everything they wanted, and you would lose your life along with your goods. (The **Chaldean** <note>The Targum, an Aramaic paraphrase</note> is in this sense: *How have you fallen into a deep sleep, until they stole their fill?* The **Syriac**: *How you would fail.* **Rashi**: *How you would have been put to sleep in silence.* Others say otherwise, for the word *nidmah* <note>Hebrew: נדמה</note> has **many meanings** <note>original Greek: πολύσημον (polysemon)</note>. The **Septuagint** <note>Ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> and the **Arabic**: *Where have you been cast down?* **Ibn Ezra**, **Kimchi**, **Rupert**, **Pagninus**, **Vatablus**, **Junius**, the **Dutch**, Blessed **Luther**...)

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Obadiah 1:5, moving from Abarbanel’s general theories to a line-by-line philological and historical examination of the text. It transitions from the discussion on the "utter destruction" of Edom to how the Babylonian conquest differed from typical raids by thieves or grape-gatherers.</meta>

# ON OBADIAH.

*How you are cut off!* <note>original Latin: "Quomodo excisus es?" — referencing Obadiah 1:5</note> (**Arias Montanus** reads: *How, or to whom, would you have been likened?*) <note>Arias Montanus (1527–1598) was a Spanish Benedictine monk and editor of the Antwerp Polyglot Bible</note> even so, your condition would be tolerable, however miserable: for would those thieves **NOT STEAL ONLY UNTIL THEY WERE SATISFIED?** They would not take everything; they would leave at least something, either through satiety or forgetfulness. It is the custom of thieves that when they have packed up what they think is enough for themselves, they desert the rest; they are too fearful to search through everything with great accuracy. So **IF GRAPE-GATHERERS CAME UPON YOU** most greedily; if the enemies despoiled you of your goods just as these men do the vineyard of its clusters, the situation would still not be entirely desperate, for **WOULD THEY NOT LEAVE** at least some **CLUSTERS**, namely those that tend to escape the eye while hiding under the leaves?

But the Chaldeans <note>The Babylonians, who conquered the region in the 6th century BC</note> will act differently with you: they will leave you almost nothing; for **HOW** accurately **THE THINGS OF ESAU WILL BE SEARCHED OUT** by them! (Understand here his goods, or <term>matmunei</term> <note>Hebrew: מטמוני; "hidden treasures"</note> according to **Kimchi**; or <term>ta tou Esau</term> <note>Greek: τὰ τοῦ Ἠσαῦ; "the things of Esau"</note>; or the <term>sons of Esau</term> <note>Hebrew: בני עשו</note>; or <term>those who belong to him</term> <note>Hebrew: אשר היו לעשו</note> according to **Mercer** and **Vatablus**; or the <term>house of Esau</term> <note>Hebrew: בית עשו</note> according to **Drusius**.) How nothing will escape their eyes and hands! **AND HIS HIDDEN THINGS WILL BE SOUGHT OUT** by them! The treasures hidden in underground caverns! (*For, as **Jerome** says, the entire southern region of the Idumaeans, from Eleutheropolis as far as Petra and Haila, has small dwellings in caves; and because it is a southern province, it uses underground huts on account of the excessive heat of the sun.*) <note>Jerome was a 4th-century Church Father known for his Latin translation of the Bible (the Vulgate)</note> These enemies will survey your very pits and caverns, with **Jehovah** himself uncovering them and all your things, as seen in *Jeremiah 49:10*. Our exposition of the sense and connection of the words is confirmed both by the text and by the very event that followed under **Nebuchadnezzar**. For although **Josephus**, describing the kingdoms defeated after Jerusalem in *Antiquities Book 10, Chapter 11*, does not mention Idumaea, **Jeremiah** (in *Chapter 25, verses 19 and 26*), predicting the victories of Nebuchadnezzar which Josephus reviews briefly in the cited place, makes explicit mention of Idumaea; and it is likely that Nebuchadnezzar, having subjugated the Ammonites, attacked the neighboring Moabites and proceeded from their land through Idumaea into Egypt.

# AN EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

...when he deceitfully <margin>a. leads him</margin> from his house. **Rabbi Solomon Jarchi** <note>Also known as Rashi (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French commentator on the Bible and Talmud.</note> indeed explains the words: *To the border they have sent you,* as follows: *To the border where the war was, for they seduced and incited you to go there, and at last persuaded you to move for war in that place, after which they withdrew from you and brought you no help.* And **Rabbi Aben-Esra** <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (c. 1089–1167), a Spanish Jewish philosopher and biblical exegete.</note> explains it in this sense: *To the border they have let you go, namely when your fugitives went into captivity.* Regarding the phrase **In your bread**, while you were still in your own place—and this is what the word <term>Lachmekha</term> <note>Hebrew: לחמך; "your bread"</note> signifies—*they placed a plaster and a medicine, but they did not do so in war.* <note>The Hebrew word "mazor" in Obadiah 1:7 is notoriously difficult; here it is translated via the Latin "emplastrum" as a medical dressing, though modern scholars often translate it as "snare" or "trap."</note> Although, by this reasoning, there would be no prudence in Esau to understand their deceit, they can nevertheless be explained in this way: <term>Lachmekha</term> means *That the men of your peace and those eating your bread place a plaster and medicine—which is a periphrasis for counsel—under you, that is, under your wound; but there is no <margin>b. prudence</margin> in it, that is, in the plaster, because they do not notice what they are doing with their medicine and preparation; therefore, O Edom, there is no benefit to you from that deed of theirs.*

Behold, however, the Prophet narrates all these things because of what happened to the Edomite nation many times in former times, on account of its blasphemies, reproaches, and the pride of its soul; and at the same time to signify to it that the future war is not prepared in the same way. For behold, he says, on that day which I determine for the future desolation of Edom—*since war is waged either <margin>c. by wisdom</margin>, as it is said: By wise counsel you shall wage war and safety is in a multitude of counselors (Proverbs 24:6); or by the strength of the warriors, as Rabshakeh said: Counsel and strength are for the war (2 Kings 18:20)*—**I will destroy the wise men from Edom and prudence from the mountain of Esau**, namely, so that no good counsel or knowledge may be found among the **Edomite Christians** <note>In medieval Jewish commentary, "Edom" or "Esau" was frequently used as a coded reference to Rome and the Christian Church.</note> when they go up to that war. And as for whether they act by power, he adds: **Your warriors shall be terrified, O Teman**, <note>Teman was a major city or region in Edom, named after a grandson of Esau.</note> implying that even their strong men are destined to perish; so that, destitute of both counsel and power, they shall fall before their enemies. And this is what he says: **So that everyone may be cut off from the mountain of Esau by slaughter**, that is, so that every one of those who have their family, name, and origin from the mountain of Esau may be cut off. These are, <margin>d. in general, all Christians</margin> whose destruction will be by slaughter, that is, *from that huge carnage which will befall them; for no "gleanings" shall be left from the Idumaeans in that future war, as were left from other wars brought against them.* He says: **Teman**, because the warriors of Teman (that is, of the south) are famous for their strength.

<header>ON OBADIAH.</header>

...by his strength. And thus **Jonathan** <note>The Targum of Jonathan, an influential Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible.</note> translates: **And your strong ones, the inhabitants of the southern land, shall be reached.** And **Rabbi Solomon Jarchi** <note>Rashi (1040–1105), the foundational French commentator on the Torah and Talmud.</note> explains: **Your strong men shall be terrified and tremble, so that they turn and take flight into the southern land;** for from there the **Edomites** shall come to that war; and this explanation is sound. Look, then, with these things presupposed, the punishments inflicted in other wars—which formerly happened to the land of **Edom** neighboring **Jerusalem**—are <gloss>g.</gloss> [comparable to] that which might happen to **Rome**, the head of the **Edomite nation**. <gloss>e.</gloss> Thus far the reasoning is the same, if we look at the future threat, that there will be a universal desolation of the nation called **Edom**, because it originated from there.

But in chapter 1 of the **Tractate on Idolatry** <note>The Talmudic tractate *Avodah Zarah*, which deals with laws regarding interactions with non-Jewish cultures and religions.</note> it is said: **Behold, I have made you small among the nations.** **Rabbi Joseph** <note>A prominent Babylonian sage (Amora) of the 3rd-4th century.</note> handed down: *This is because among you, they do not appoint a King who is the son of a King.* **You are greatly despised:** *Because you have neither a scripture nor a language of your own, and because they appoint a King through petition [election].* <gloss>E. g. f.</gloss> **Severus, the son of Antoninus**, <note>original: *Asverus Fil. Antonini*. This refers to the Roman Emperor Septimius Severus, whom rabbinic tradition sometimes associated with the Antonine dynasty.</note> etc. Clearly, these sages understand the present text as referring to **Rome**, yet not regarding the threat of some future punishment as other interpreters do, but rather as a past matter: namely, that there were **Caesars** in Rome who nevertheless did not hold the empire <gloss>g.</gloss> in such a way that it passed by way of inheritance to a son—as do the **Ishmaelite** <note>A common medieval term for Arab or Muslim rulers.</note> kings or the kings <gloss>h.</gloss> who are among us—but instead, they obtained the empire through the **Election of the Roman Princes** according to their votes and wills. Neither do the **Romans** have a scripture or a language of their own, but they received both from the **Greeks**, as stands written in their own books.

---

a. We think the Rabbi means that the confederates will deceitfully lead the Edomites to the borders, just as one is often politely escorted from a house under the guise of friendship. This is more fitting than if we were to ascribe to the Rabbi's words a violent dragging, which friends are not accustomed to use.
b. He suggests, less cautiously, that the enemies will act in the application of the plaster. <note>Referring to the medical metaphor used for "counsel" on the previous page.</note>
c. Our copy had: **in wisdom** <note>original: *be-chokhmah*</note>.
d. Or: **Christians**, but the sense requires: **in war** <note>original: *be-milchamah*</note> in general.
e. The Rabbi means that by reason of the outcome and final destruction, it is the same whether one understands **Idumaea** or **Rome**, since the former is punished through the latter.
f. With respect to the judgment of others, we think **Septimius Severus** is hinted at here, who, since he is read to have been educated by **Marcus Aurelius Antoninus the Philosopher**, is called his son...

In medieval Jewish commentary, Edom was frequently used as a coded reference for Rome and the Christian world.</note>, *when they go up to that war. # ON OBADIAH.

...by his strength. And thus **Jonathan** <note>Targum Jonathan, the ancient Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Prophets.</note> translates it: *And your brave ones, the inhabitants of the southern land, shall be crushed.* And Rabbi Solomon **Jarchi** <note>Rashi (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French commentator.</note> explains: *Your brave ones shall be terrified and tremble, so that they turn and take flight into the southern land*; for from there the Idumaeans will come to that war, and this explanation is sound. Behold, therefore, with these things presupposed, the punishments inflicted in other wars—which long ago happened in Idumaea near Jerusalem—and those which may happen to Rome, the head of the Edomite nation, <margin>e.</margin> follow the same reasoning thus far, if we look at the future threat: that there will be a universal desolation of the nation called Edom, because it originated from there. 

But in the first chapter of the *Treatise on Idolatry* <note>The Talmudic tractate Avodah Zarah, which discusses interactions between Jews and the surrounding Roman/pagan world.</note> it is said: *Behold, I have made you small among the nations.* Rabbi **Joseph** handed down this teaching: *Because among you they do not appoint a King who is the son of a King.* *You are greatly despised:* *Because you have neither scripture nor a language [of your own], and because they appoint a King by petition,* <margin>f.</margin> for example, the son of Asverus, the son of Antoninus, etc. <note>This likely refers to the Roman succession; "Asverus" may be a corruption of Severus.</note> Clearly, these writers understand the present text as referring to Rome, yet not concerning a threat of future punishment in the manner of other interpreters, but concerning a past matter: specifically, that there were Caesars in Rome, but they did not have an imperial power <margin>g.</margin> that passed by way of inheritance to a son, as was the case with the Israelite kings or the Kings <margin>h.</margin> who are with us now; instead, they obtained the empire through the Election of Roman Princes according to their wishes and judgments. Nor do the Romans even have a scripture or a language of their own, but they received both from the Greeks, as is written in their own books.

<margin>a.</margin> We think the Rabbi means that the confederates will deceitfully lead the Edomites even to the borders, just as someone is customarily led out of a house as a courtesy under the guise of friendship. This is more fitting than if we were to ascribe to the Rabbi's words a violent dragging, which friends are not accustomed to use. <margin>b.</margin> He less cautiously implies that the enemies will act in the application of a plaster <note>A medicinal bandage; here used metaphorically for a deceptive "remedy" or trap.</note>. <margin>c.</margin> Our copy had: <term>with wisdom</term> <note>original Hebrew: "בחכמה" (be-chokhmah)</note>, but the sense requires: <term>in war</term> <note>original Hebrew: "במלחמה" (be-milchamah)</note>. <margin>d.</margin> Or: Christians in general. <margin>e.</margin> The Rabbi intends, by reason of the outcome and the ultimate destruction, that it is the same whether Idumaea or Rome is understood, since the former is punished through the latter. <margin>f.</margin> Without prejudice to the judgment of others, we think Septimius Severus is hinted at here, who, since he is read to have been educated by Marcus Antoninus the Philosopher, is called his son here...

<meta>This page continues the critique of Isaac Abarbenel’s commentary on the Book of Obadiah. The previous page discussed the identification of Edom with Rome; here, the author highlights logical and chronological inconsistencies in Abarbenel's interpretation of the timing of the prophecy's fulfillment.</meta>

...it seems to be said: for in foreign history the Jews do not follow that which is *unaccountable* <note>original Greek: τὸ $\dot{\alpha}\nu\upsilon\pi\epsilon\upsilon\theta\upsilon\nu\omicron\nu$, referring to a person or thing not subject to audit or responsibility.</note>. <margin>g.</margin> Or: *Hereditary*.. <margin>h.</margin> Against the kings of Castile; for he [Abarbenel] wrote his works under Ferdinand V, surnamed the *Great*.

### EXAMINATION.

Our Rabbi [Abarbenel], who above on page 10 refused to admit any division in the Prophecy of Obadiah, and who below—from the end of the prophecy to the beginning—concludes and infers that if the end looks toward a future time, the same must be said of the whole prophecy; now he establishes a truly massive chasm between verse 7 and the one immediately following. 

For he claims that verse 7 deals with the past disasters of Idumaea; while verse 8 deals with a certain future war for which the Jews wait in vain to this very day. But if Rome is "Idumaea" to him here, Obadiah in verse 7 cannot be speaking of disasters already inflicted in his own time, but of future ones. Thus, Abarbenel will be forced to admit an *Enallage* <term>Enallage: A grammatical substitution where one tense is used for another, such as using the past tense to express the certainty of a future event.</term> of the Past for the Future, which he completely rejected on page 35, saying *it seemed better to him to explain the past through the past and the future through the future*. And even if he most strongly wants to understand this as Rome, we have shown long ago that he cannot. 

But if he admits that it refers to the Idumaea bordering Palestine (as he certainly should, and as we leave the choice to the reader), Obadiah is not speaking of disasters brought by Nebuchadnezzar or the Maccabees; for even if those were past in Abarbenel's time, they were still future in Obadiah's time, and thus the previously denied *Enallage* would have to be conceded. 

If Abarbenel tries to reform his view and decides that Obadiah is speaking of disasters already inflicted in his own time, a gap <note>original Greek: $\delta\iota\dot{\alpha}\sigma\tau\eta\mu\alpha$, an interval or distance.</note> of more than 2,392 years is necessarily placed between verse 7 and 8. For according to his own view, Obadiah lived in the court of Ahab <margin>e.</margin> in the year 3034 after the creation of the world, according to David Ganz <note>David Ganz (1541–1613) was a Jewish chronicler and astronomer; his "Zemach David" is a standard work on chronology.</note>. And today—when the war our Rabbi predicted has still not happened—the Jews count 5426 years from the creation of the world. Who would believe the Prophet would make such a leap with no change or shift in style? 

It is more obvious that verse 8 is connected with verse 7. Since the Prophet says in verse 8: *On that very day* <note>original Hebrew: ביום ההוא, *Ba-yom ha-hu*</note>, a determination of some day must either necessarily precede or follow, so that it follows as a relative pronoun. But no determination of the day on which the wise men were to be destroyed from Idumaea is added after it. Therefore, it must have preceded it, and the sense is: *On that day* (namely, the day on which they shall drive you to the borders, etc., from verse 7), *I will destroy the wise men*. 

But so that Abarbenel's other *follies* <note>original Greek: $\phi\lambda\upsilon\alpha\rho\epsilon\acute{\iota}\alpha\iota$, meaning nonsense or idle talk.</note> may be more easily exposed, we will briefly add a solid *explanation* <note>original Greek: $\acute{\epsilon}\xi\dot{\eta}\gamma\eta\sigma\iota\nu$, exegesis or interpretation.</note> of the text here: Among friends and allies...

# ON OBADIAH.

...you also place your trust, O Edom! But they will finally betray you to the enemy, and thus return to you reciprocity and retaliation <note>original: *ἀντιπεπονθὸς & talionem*</note>, since you also so perfidiously and insolently insulted the Jews, to whom you should have been most closely joined. Namely, with craftiness, *EVEN TO THE BORDERS* of your jurisdiction (*Kimchi*, *Vatablus*: of the enemies; but both can be admitted, for in contiguous land the border of one and the other is the same) *THEY SHALL SEND YOU OUT* (either having followed you like allies <note>original: *σύμμαχοι*</note> acting in a common cause against the Chaldeans, or persuading you to go there with clever advice; *Drusius*, *B. Tarnovius* and others put it thus: they will dutifully and honorably lead your ambassadors, sent to seek reinforcements, to the limits of their region according to custom, yet all the while carrying a crafty fox beneath a stale chest; *Gvaltherus* explains <term>שלחך</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Shalachcha*, "sent you"</note> as: they will send reinforcements to you as far as the borders, and then withdraw: *Aben-Esra*, *Kimchi*, *Castrus*: they will follow you even as you are now heading into exile, feigning sympathy <note>original: *συμπαθείαν*</note> even with fake tears; finally *Ribera*, *Cornelius à Lapide*, *Tirinus*, *Junius*, *Oecolampadius* explain the text as being about a violent expulsion from one's own seats. However, the Prophet does not predict hostile force, but rather such things as happen under the appearance of friendship and could not at first be detected by the Idumaeans. Likewise, the opinion of *L. de Dieu* on this passage does not greatly commend itself to us, as he understands "the border" to mean death (or the boundary of life) according to the character of the Arabic language.) *ALL THE MEN OF YOUR COVENANT* (your confederates, a Hebraism) the Ammonites, Moabites and other neighbors (as *Paræus* proves, not the Assyrians as was the mind of *Grotius*) but in truth *THEY SHALL DECEIVE YOU*, later deserting you and betraying you to the Chaldeans, by the same fraud toward you with which they once, joined with you against the Jews, finally rose up against you yourselves in *2 Chronicles 20:23* (an event not predicted here, as *Castrus* thinks, but its memory is merely refreshed) *THEY SHALL PREVAIL AGAINST YOU* (finally using force, or rather) with their clever suggestions, *Jeremiah 38:22.* *ALL THE MEN OF YOUR PEACE* (compare *Isaiah 41:12, Jeremiah 20:10* and the previously cited passage) friends, from whom you feared no evil, *AND OF YOUR BREAD* (*Aben-Esra* explains: *In your bread*; *Raschi*: your brother Jacob placed *your bread* as a stumbling block, because he acquired your birthright with a pottage of lentils; but this is clearly irrelevant <note>original Greek: *ἀπροσδιόνυσον*, literally "nothing to do with Dionysus."</note>. *Capellus* in *Critica Sacra, book 6, chapter 4* conjectures that the reading should be <term>לוחמך</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Lochamecha*</note> *Your warriors*, a poorly timed criticism which *Buxtorf* corrected in *Anticritica, page 878.* More correctly, the Chaldean [Targum], *Kimchi*, our Author, *Vatablus*, *Mercerus*, *Drusius*, etc., think that <term>לחמך</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Lachmecha*, "your bread"</note> is said elliptically for <term>אוכלי לחמך</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Ochlei lachmecha*</note> *those who eat your bread, Psalm 41:10*; most conveniently from <unclear>noverit</unclear>

# EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL

<margin>A new interpretation</margin> The phrase <term>men of your bread</term> <note>Hebrew: *anshei lachmecha*</note> is repeated. So say Johann Tarnow, Drusius, Junius, Gualther, the Dutch translators, etc. These are your household members and daily dinner guests. THEY SHALL PLACE <note>Hebrew: *yasimu*</note>. This future tense indicates that the preceding past-tense verbs were used prophetically to represent the future. (Compare Glassius, *Sacred Philology*, book 1, tract 4, section 1, chapter 4). A WOUND <note>Hebrew: <term>mazor</term>, meaning a wound, from the root *zur*, to compress, because surgeons usually compress gaping wounds or squeeze out gore. Compare Hosea 5:13, Isaiah 16; see also the works of Junius, Vatablus, Mercerus, Gualther, Tarnow, and others.</note> Drusius renders this as *an ulcer*, as if to say: they will turn your very feasts into abscesses and your own destruction. The Dutch: *Geswel* <note>Dutch: "swelling" or "tumor"</note>. The Talmudists (Sanhedrin, chapter 11, folio 92) explain it as a disease or faintness <note>Hebrew: *choli*</note>, followed by Rashi. Our translator <note>referring to Martin Luther</note> says: *A plaster*. Cocceius: *Filthiness*. Regarding the general sense, others say: *An ambush*. Thus the Septuagint <note>Greek: *enedra*</note>, the Syriac <note>Syriac: *Cmine*</note>, the Arabic <note>Arabic: *Macamina*</note>, and the Chaldean <note>Chaldean: *Offendiculum*, meaning a stumbling block</note>. Our "Great Man" <note>A title for Martin Luther</note> says most excellently: *They will betray you* <note>original German: "Sie werden dich verrathen"</note>. UNDER YOU (others not inconveniently translate this as: *Secretly from you*, as in 2 Samuel 3:12; so Kimchi, Drusius, Tarnow, etc. But if <term>tachtecha</term> is translated as *Under*, the whole phrase is more conveniently explained in the following way): just as bird-catchers place snares beneath birds to deceive them, so will those people lay traps for you. Or: those guests of yours, as if hiding things under your rugs and pillows (which were used in feasts then), will conceal something <term>lethal</term> <note>Greek: *traumat-phtheron*</note>, a sword, or some other instrument of injury (so Calvin, Mercerus, Pareus). Yet THERE IS NO UNDERSTANDING IN HIM (According to Vatablus, Mercerus, and others, there is a "change of person" here, which is why the Arabic, Chaldean, and others translate it as: *In you*. Others think the word <term>tebunato</term> ("his understanding") is constructed like the verb *bin* with the preposition *be*, so the sense is: *There is, namely in Esau, no noticing of that inflicted wound*. Louis de Dieu asserts this in *On the Old Testament*, page 683, and our translator had this in mind, nor do we disapprove. It could, however, also be an apostrophe to the Jewish people, as if to say: "See now, my people, how stupid Edom has become, who otherwise seems to himself <term>wise and understanding</term> <note>Greek: *sophos sunetos*</note>, that he notices none of these things!") WILL I NOT IN THAT VERY DAY, in which those things shall happen, SAYS THE LORD, bring it about (that is, it will most certainly happen) THAT I WILL DESTROY (for the letter *Vau* in the Hebrew word for "and I will destroy" is not superfluous <note>Greek: *pleonazei*</note>, as the *Fe* of the Arabs is, according to Mercerus) THE WISE MEN (not soothsayers, augurs, or similar people, as Lyra thought, but) counselors and men prevailing in wisdom and skill for conducting affairs FROM EDOM, AND UNDERSTANDING FROM THE MOUNT OF ESAU (that is, Mount Seir; however, by a <term>synecdoche</term> <note>a figure of speech where a part represents the whole</note> all of Idumea is understood, because this phrase is <term>explanatory</term> <note>Greek: *exēgētikon*</note> of the former) so that they may cease to trust in their own wisdom, for which they have hitherto been famous (especially the wisdom...

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological commentary on the Book of Obadiah, specifically focusing on verse 8 and transitioning into verse 9. It explores the downfall of the Temanites, renowned for their wisdom and military strength, and the geographic significance of Teman within Idumaea (Edom).</meta>

### ON OBADIAH

The Temanites were celebrated for their praise, which is why Jeremiah 49:7 asks: *Is there no longer wisdom in Teman?* Indeed, Eliphaz the Temanite, the friend of Job, was an Idumaean. Many believe he founded the town of *Teman* in Idumaea, naming it after his son (*Genesis 36:11*), just as Cain named *Enoch* after his own son (*Genesis 4:17*). However, most scholars claim he originally came from another city called *Teman*, located beyond the Sea of Galilee in Trachonitis. But now, <note>original: "Φάσκόντες εἶναι σοφοὶ μωρανθήσονται"</note> **claiming to be wise, they shall become fools.** And do not seek protection in your own strength, which you have polluted until now, while you prepared your sustenance by the sword (*Genesis 27:40*) and were always most ready for war (compare Josephus, *The Jewish War, book 4, chapter 15*). At that time, behold, their whole bodies **SHALL TREMBLE** with fear <note>The Septuagint uses the emphatic Greek word *ptoēthēsontai*, meaning they shall be terrified and struck senseless. The Syriac translator, using the word *yitbaru* meaning "they shall be plundered," does not capture the emphasis of the Hebrew word, which signifies being "broken as if by fear."</note> even **YOUR STRONG MEN** and famous heroes. <note>The Septuagint and the Arabic version translate this as "warriors."</note> **O TEMAN** <note>This refers to all of Idumaea by a "synecdoche"—a figure of speech where a part represents the whole—or an argument from the greater part.</note>. Our translator <note>referring to Martin Luther</note> translates *Teman* as a general term with the Chaldean version, although elsewhere he concedes it is a proper name. For in his commentary on *Ezekiel (page 190, chapter 4)*, he says: *Behold, Teman was the firstborn of Eliphaz, the son of Esau, and his land was called Teman after his name; it was the royal city of Idumaea.* The geographer Adrichomius also says on *page 125*: *Teman is the metropolis of Idumaea and the royal seat of the princes of Edom; located in the mountains of Seir, in the southern part of Idumaea, it is five miles distant from Petra in Arabia.* It was named after *Teman*, the firstborn of *Eliphaz*, who was the firstborn of *Esau*. *From this city, the entire southern region of the Idumaeans took its name.* In Jerome's time, it was a village <note>Jerome, *On Hebrew Places*</note>. Therefore, although the word *Teman* elsewhere sometimes denotes "the south" <note>derived from the root *yamin*, because the south is the "right-hand" side of the world when one faces the east</note>, here it is the proper name of the city against which Jeremiah (chapter 49), Ezekiel (chapter 25:13), and Amos (chapter 1:12) also prophesy alongside Obadiah. This is done so **THAT EVERY MAN** <note>Meaning "every person" according to some, or "every noble" according to others; for the Hebrew word *ish* (man) is sometimes used with this emphasis, as in Psalm 3:3, Psalm 49:3, and Isaiah 2:9. See also Drusius, *Hebrew Questions*, book 1, question 21.</note> **MAY BE CUT OFF**. Truly, in the phrasing of Scripture, the "cutting off of a man" implies the lack of an heir or a successor in a lineage (compare *1 Kings 2:5, 8:25, 9:5; 2 Chronicles 6:16; Jeremiah 33:18, 35:19*, etc.). The sense, therefore, is: That the propagator of the Edomite lineage may fail, especially upon the royal throne. **FROM THE MOUNT OF ESAU, BECAUSE OF** so great and horrendous **A SLAUGHTER** <note>Hebrew: *miktel*. The Vulgate and Septuagint versions make this the beginning of the following verse; but the Hebrew codex...</note>

<summary>This page analyzes the destruction of Teman's intellectual and military elite. It identifies Teman as both a specific royal city in Edom and a symbol for the entire nation, emphasizing that the "cutting off" refers to the total end of the Edomite royal lineage.</summary>
<keywords>Teman, Idumaea, Edom, Eliphaz, wisdom, military strength, biblical geography, royal lineage, Obadiah 1:8-9, philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic critique of Isaac Abarbenel’s commentary on the Book of Obadiah. The previous page discussed the downfall of the Temanites and their misplaced confidence in their own wisdom. This page addresses the grammatical structure of verse 9 and debates the historical versus future fulfillment of the prophecy.</meta>

# EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL

The Hebrew [Abarbenel] disagrees. Cocceius <note>Johannes Cocceius (1603–1669), a prominent Dutch theologian and Hebraist</note> believes (on page 248) that the word **מִקְטָל** <note>Hebrew: *mi-qatal*, meaning "from slaughter"</note> is not governed by the verb **וְכָרַת** <note>Hebrew: *ve-charat*, "and he shall be cut off"</note>, but rather by **חַתּוּ** <note>Hebrew: *chattu*, "they shall be dismayed"</note>, as if to say: "your strong men shall tremble because of the slaughter." However, this word carries an *Atnach* <note>a Hebrew cantillation mark that serves as a major pause or semicolon in the middle of a verse</note>, and thus "from slaughter" is more correctly referred back to the verb in the first half of the verse.

Therefore, the logical progression of the prophecy up to verse 9 is as follows: O tiny nation of Edom (verse 2), why are you so proud? Where do you get such a spirit? Is it from your fortified citadels and castles (verse 3)? I will drag you down from there (verse 4). Is it from your riches? I will strip you of them so thoroughly that nothing remains even in your most hidden cupboards (verse 5). Is it from your friends and allies? They will deceive and betray you (verse 7). Is it from your military wisdom? Even that will fail you (verse 8). Is it from your strength? You shall become utterly weak (verse 9). Thus, stripped of all protection, you shall become completely miserable. In this context, everything is plain and clear.

But we must also discuss when, against whom, and through whom this prophecy reached its fulfillment. We believe that what has been said so far has already been fulfilled among the Idumaeans (the descendants of Esau), beginning with **Nebuchadnezzar**, and then continued and completed by others. Our opponent [Abarbenel] believes these things are to be understood as occurring only in the future—perhaps at the "Greek Calends" <note>an ancient idiom for "never," as the Greeks did not use "Calends" in their calendar</note>—to be carried out against all Christians (whom he identifies with Edom) by the Turks or others, or even by the Jews themselves.

The basis of his assertion, as far as we can see, is that the true Idumaeans and the Christians (whom he views as "disguised Idumaeans") have often been severely afflicted, but never entirely wiped out; yet the text here says that "everyone from Mount Esau shall be cut off." Therefore, he argues, this prophecy has not yet been fulfilled.

But in truth: 
1. This passage suggests not so much the physical death of every individual, but the "civil death" of the nation, by which the Idumaeans were to lose their leadership, their royal status, and their **autonomy** <note>original: *αὐτονομίαν* (autonomian), the right of a people to live by their own laws</note>; compare Isaiah 34:12. 
2. In such instances of total destruction, what happens to the majority is often ascribed to the whole, following the common practice of naming a thing by its most significant part, as we have mentioned before. 
3. All punishments, and even total destruction, are understood to be conditional upon a lack of repentance. This is clearly seen in Jonah 3:2, 4, 5, and 10. 
4. Indeed, the Jews will not entirely deny that the Idumaeans repented, since under **Hyrcanus** <note>John Hyrcanus (reigned 134–104 BCE), a Hasmonean leader who conquered the Idumaeans and forced their conversion to Judaism</note> they were received into the Jewish covenant as "proselytes of righteousness."

But whatever the case may be, our position remains secure; we know that the Idumaeans were so utterly destroyed, even as individuals, that their name and memory survive nowhere on earth. Lest we seem to say this without proof, for the sake of Abarbenel and his kinsmen, we shall investigate the fate of the Idumaeans somewhat more deeply, from the time of **Nebuchadnezzar** until our own day. Abarbenel does not deny that Nebuchadnezzar subdued Idumaea, nor does he dispute it. It remained, therefore, under his successors until the final Babylonian...

<summary>The author debates the timing of Obadiah's prophecy, arguing against Abarbenel's view that it refers to a future destruction of Christians. Instead, the author asserts the prophecy was fulfilled historically through the total loss of Idumaean national identity and autonomy.</summary>
<keywords>Abarbenel, Obadiah 1:9, Idumaea, Esau, Nebuchadnezzar, John Hyrcanus, repentance, autonomy, Hebrew grammar, Christian-Jewish polemics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the historical discussion of the downfall of Edom (Idumaea), specifically identifying which world powers fulfilled the prophecy of Obadiah at different stages in history.</meta>

# ON OBADIAH

Cyrus—who subdued Asia and the entire East according to Herodotus (Book 1) and Justin (Book 1, chapter 8)—stripped the king of the Medes, Nabonidus, of his royal dignity. This is according to Berosus in Josephus’s *Against Apion* (Book 1), Metasthenes in Eusebius, and Ptolemy in the *Canon* (see also Scaliger’s *On the Correction of Time*, Book 6, page 277). This is why Ezra 1:2 states that God had given him "all the kingdoms of the earth" <note>original Hebrew: *kol mamlechot ha-aretz*</note>. Accordingly, the Idumaeans were subject to the Persians until Alexander the Great, who, among other things, seized Syria, Judea, and all neighboring regions on this side of the Euphrates from the Persians, as witnessed by Josephus in *Antiquities of the Jews* (Book 11, chapter 8), Justin (Book 11, chapter 10), Curtius (Book 4, chapter 5 and following), and Plutarch’s *Life of Alexander* (pages 679, 682, etc.). Saint Jerome says that in six years Alexander gained possession of a great part of Europe, all of Egypt, Syria, Palestine, and all of Asia as far as India. Furthermore, Blessed Martin Luther understands the "young man" who was to invade Idumaea in Jeremiah 49:19 to be Alexander.

After the death of Alexander and the division of his kingdom, Idumaea was under the rule of the Seleucids <note>The Greek dynasty founded by Seleucus I Nicator, ruling over much of the Middle East</note>, as gathered from 1 Maccabees 4:29, 61 and chapter 6:31. Indeed, Gorgias was "governor of Idumaea" <note>original Greek: *tēs Idoumaias stratēgos*</note> under Antiochus Eupator, as seen in 2 Maccabees 4:14-15 and chapter 12:32. Nevertheless, Castoborus the Idumaean, cited in Josephus’s *Antiquities* (Book 15, chapter 11), clearly hints that it was also at times subject to the Lagids <note>The Ptolemaic dynasty of Egypt, founded by Ptolemy I Soter, son of Lagus</note>. For he says that Idumaea was once subject to the ancestors of Cleopatra; she was of the Lagid line, specifically the daughter of Ptolemy Auletes, granddaughter of Lathyrus, and great-granddaughter of Physcon, etc.

But the Maccabees also invaded it on various occasions. For in the time of Antiochus Epiphanes, Judas Maccabaeus desolated it and leveled the city of Mareshah to the ground (1 Maccabees 5:3, 5; Josephus *Antiquities*, Book 12, chapters 11 and 12). He is also read to have done this in the time of Antiochus Eupator (2 Maccabees 10:16). Furthermore, John Hyrcanus <note>A Hasmonean (Maccabean) leader and high priest (ruled 134–104 BCE)</note>, having captured all the cities of Idumaea, forced the inhabitants—who previously worshipped the local god Coze <note>original Latin: *Deastrum Coze*; referring to Qos, the national god of the Edomites</note> (Josephus *Antiquities*, Book 15, chapter 11)—to undergo circumcision and adopt the Jewish religion. He "changed their constitution to the Jewish customs and laws" <note>original Greek: *tēn politeian autōn eis ta ioudaiōn ethē kai nomima*</note>, from which time they began to be counted among the Jews, "so that they were henceforth Jews" <note>original Greek: *hōste einai to loipon ioudaious*</note>, as Josephus says in *Antiquities* (Book 13, chapter 17). 

Zonaras <note>A 12th-century Byzantine chronicler</note> also reports regarding Hyrcanus that, after subduing the Idumaeans, he permitted them to remain in their homeland "if they would be circumcised and follow Jewish customs" <note>original Greek: *ei peritemnointo kai chrōntai ethesin ioudaikois*</note>, and that out of love for their homeland they accepted "both circumcision and the rest" <note>original Greek: *kai tēn peritomēn kai talla*</note>. Ammonius also says of the Idumaeans that "they were called Jews" <note>original Greek: *eklēthēsan ioudaioi*</note>. However, Josephus calls Herod the Idumaean a "half-Jew" <note>original Greek: *hēmiioudaios*</note> in *Antiquities* (Book 14, chapter 24). (Scholars debate elsewhere regarding Herod, whether he should be called an Idumaean and a foreigner, or a Jew and a native. The latter is defended by Scaliger in his notes on Eusebius, Isaac Casaubon in his *Exercises against Baronius*, Drusius in his notes on Sulpicius, and Torniellus in his *Annals*; the former view is held by Serarius in his book *Herod and the Rabbis*, and Selden in *On the Law of Nature and Nations*. Both...)

<summary>This page traces the political history of Idumaea (Edom) through the Persian, Greek, and Hasmonean periods, detailing how various conquerors like Alexander the Great and John Hyrcanus fulfilled biblical prophecies through conquest and forced conversion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical investigation into the identity and fate of the Idumaeans, specifically addressing their integration into the Jewish nation and their eventual disappearance as a distinct people following the Roman-Jewish Wars and the arrival of Islamic and Crusader forces.</meta>

->### AN EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL<-

Yet, in a certain respect, this is true. Herod was an **Idumaean** by his ancestral lineage, but a **Jew** by religion and through his **kinship** <note>original Greek: *homophyloseōs*, meaning being of the same tribe or race</note> within the family and name of Judah. In a word: he was a "Judaized" Idumaean, much like those described in Esther 8:17 <note>original Hebrew: *mit-yahed*, meaning to become a Jew or profess Judaism</note>. It is in this sense that Herod calls the Jews his "kinsmen" <note>original Greek: *homophylous*</note> in the writings of Josephus (*Antiquities*, Book 15, chapter 14; compare with Havenwegel, page 117; Hoornbeek, page 161; and others). Furthermore, on account of this conversion of theirs, the Idumaeans repeatedly called Jerusalem their **homeland**, their **mother-city**, and their **common city** <note>original Greek: *patrida, mētropolin, and koinopolin*</note> (see Josephus, *The Jewish War*, Book 4, chapter 16, etc.). Thus, at that time, the Idumaeans merged with the Jews into a single people. We read that they were in this same state under King **Alexander Jannaeus** <note>A Hasmonean King of Judea, 103–76 BCE</note> (*Antiquities*, Book 13, chapter 23) and likewise under **Herod**, the son of Antipater (*Antiquities*, Book 15, chapter 7).

Later, when **Titus Vespasian** invaded Judea and Idumaea (compare Josephus, *War*, Book 4, chapter 26 and Hegesippus, Book 4, chapter 16), the Idumaeans came under the leadership of **John, Jacob, Simon, and Phineas** to defend their fatherland and their shared Temple. They were indignant at being excluded from these, as they viewed themselves as converts and kinsmen (*War*, Book 4, chapter 16; Hegesippus, Book 4, chapter 6). After **Simon bar Giora** <note>A primary leader of the Jewish rebellion against Rome</note> had laid waste to Idumaea—which had been betrayed by **Jacob** (*War*, Book 4, chapters 30–31; Hegesippus, Book 4, chapter 23)—he drove the inhabitants into Jerusalem (chapter 34). There, 5,000 of them initially waged an internal war against **John of Giscala** <note>original Latin: *Johanni Lev.*, referring to John the son of Levi</note> and the Zealots (*War*, Book 4, chapter 34; Book 5, chapter 1; Hegesippus, Book 5, chapter 8). Eventually, however, joining their forces, the Jews and Idumaeans attempted to repel the Romans from the city (*War*, Book 5, chapter 19; Book 6, chapter 12; Hegesippus, Book 5, chapter 10).

This continued until, despairing of their situation, the Idumaeans formed a plan to defect to the Romans. Most were prevented from starting this plan by Simon, and those who did manage to defect were considered so worthless that they could hardly find buyers when sold as slaves (*War*, Book 6, chapter 40; Hegesippus, Book 5, chapter 47). Finally, when the city was completely occupied, the Idumaeans—along with the rest of the Jews—were slaughtered, carried away, sold off, and dispersed (*War*, Book 6, chapter 24; Book 7, chapter 30). From that time on, hardly any **Idumaeans** (that is, the descendants of **Esau**) remained in Idumaea. Instead, Idumaea experienced the same fates and changes as Palestine. It was first snatched from the Romans, along with all the territory from Mesopotamia to the Red Sea, in 615 AD by **Khosrow**, the King of the Persians. It was restored by **Siroes**, but snatched away again by the **Caliph Umar** in 636 AD. From that point on, the **Saracens** <note>A medieval term for Arabs or Muslims</note> held Palestine and Idumaea until the Crusades <note>original Latin: *bellum sacrum*, "the holy war"</note>.

Indeed, **Godfrey of Bouillon** had not yet invaded Idumaea, as we gather from Jacques de Vitry (*History of Jerusalem*, chapter 21). However, **Baldwin** <note>Baldwin I, the first Crusader King of Jerusalem</note> did invade it, as witnessed by Albert of Aachen (*The Jerusalem Expedition*, Book 12, chapters 20 and 21). In the year 1116, passing over the Jordan and traversing **Syria Sobal**—to which region authors like Brother Brocardus (*Travels*), Marinus Sanuto Torsello (*Secrets for True Crusaders*, Book 3, part 14, chapter 1), and others refer as Idumaea—he descended through the vast wilderness to the **Red Sea**, according to the account of William, Archbishop of Tyre (*History of the War*, Book 11, chapters 29 and 26), who tells a very similar story to Jacques de Vitry (chapters 28 and 39).

<summary>This page traces the historical merger of the Idumaeans into the Jewish nation through conversion, their joint defense of Jerusalem against the Romans, and their ultimate disappearance as a distinct ethnic group following the Arab and Crusader conquests.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical argument against identifying Christians with the Idumaeans (Edomites). While the previous page discussed the political and religious merging of Idumaeans into the Jewish nation during the Hasmonean and Herodian periods, this page tracks the territorial history of Idumaea through the Crusades and the Ottoman conquest to prove that the biblical prophecies were fulfilled in a literal, historical sense.</meta>

->## ON OBADIAH<-

Fulcher of Chartres, in his *History of the Expedition to Jerusalem* <note>original Latin: *Gest. Franc. c. 43*</note>, and Albert of Aachen (Book 12, chapters 31–32) report that when the **Idumaeans** brought disaster upon the troops of Joscelin, they paid four thousand **bezants** <note>original Latin: *Byzantiorum*; a gold coin used in the Middle Ages</note> to King **Baldwin II** for their security. During the time of **Amalric** <note>King of Jerusalem, 1163–1174</note>, **Saladin**, the Sultan of Egypt, is said to have followed the plains of Idumaea and ascended to **Syria Sobal** <note>a region in the Transjordan centered around the castle of Montreal/Shobak</note>, as recorded by William of Tyre (Book 20, chapter 29). Thus, Idumaea returned once more to Saladin, who also seized Jerusalem from **Guy of Lusignan** in the year 1187.

In the meantime, Palestine—and especially Jerusalem—underwent other changes of fortune. It is recorded by Bunting, Alsted, and others (whose opinions we also cited on page 31) that the land fell to Emperor **Frederick II** in 1229, to **Ghazan** <note>original Latin: *Casano*</note>, the King of the **Tatars** <note>Mongols</note> in 1246, and surrendered again to the Sultan of Egypt in 1263. However, the claim regarding Ghazan is highly doubtful. Many others, including **Hayton of Armenia**—an excellent and eyewitness authority on Tatar affairs—testify that most of the Tatar Khans were rather favorable to the Christians and frequently attempted to expel the **Saracens** from Jerusalem for the benefit of the Christians. (Compare his *Book on the Tatars*: chapter 23 on Mongke, chapters 28 and 30 on Hulagu, chapter 35 on Abaga, chapter 38 on Arghun, chapter 40 on Baydu, and chapters 43–44 on Ghazan).

It seems more likely to us that Idumaea and Palestine remained under the Egyptian Sultans for 330 years, until **Selim**, the Emperor of the Turks, occupied the region in 1517 after defeating Sultan **Tuman bay II** <note>original Latin: *Tomumbejo*</note> and hanging him before the palace gate. The Idumaeans remain subject to his successor, **Ahmad**, to this very day. Consequently, during the entire time that the Sultans and Turkish Emperors held Idumaea, it was inhabited for the most part by Saracens and Arabs, as eyewitnesses like **Brocardus**, **Breydenbach**, and others testify.

In this time, the land changed not only its inhabitants but also its name. **Jerome** reports that in his time, Idumaea was called **Gabalena**; perhaps from this, the neighboring **Beersheba** is today called **Giblim**, according to Brocardus. Albert of Aachen (Book 12, chapters 8 and 31) asserts that the peoples of Idumaea were called *Bidumos* in his day, and just as Idumaea is called *Bidum* today, J. Langius notes it was sometimes called *Borsia*. Others report that the most significant part of it is called by the common name for a desert, *Barraab*.

Up to this point, we have established two things that no one—unless they are a complete follower of **Carneades** <note>a Greek philosopher known for extreme skepticism; here meaning someone who denies the obvious</note>—could deny:

1.  The prophecy of Obadiah, up to verse 9 and even verse 17, was truly and fully fulfilled in the Idumaeans, the **ancestors** <note>original Greek: *progonois*</note> of **Esau**.
2.  Christians—at least those who are **genuinely** <note>original Greek: *gnēsiōs*</note> such—can in no way be called Idumaeans. They cannot be called so by region, for then the Saracens and Arabs would be the Idumaeans; nor by religion, for Christians do not worship the Idumaean god **Gozen** <note>likely referring to the Edomite deity Qos</note>—otherwise, the Jews would be more rightly called Idumaeans, since the Idumaeans became their converts. Nor can they be called so by lineage or kinship; for if that were the case, the whole world would be Idumaean, since there is hardly a corner of the earth where the Christian religion has not reached. Yet the true Edomite lineage...

<meta>This page continues the author's polemic against Isaac Abarbanel’s interpretation of biblical prophecy. Specifically, it addresses the Jewish claim—represented by Abarbanel and his sources—that "Edom" or "Idumaea" in the Bible refers to the Roman Empire and, by extension, the Christian world. # EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL

...in the clefts of the rock, holding the height of the hill: though you make yourself as high as the eagle, yet I will cast you down from there, says the Lord. <note>The author is quoting Jeremiah 49:16-19, comparing Edom's pride to an eagle nesting in high rocks.</note> v. 17. Thus Edom shall be a desolation, and everyone passing by it shall hiss at all its plagues. v. 18. He attributes the first desolation to Nebuchadnezzar, King of Babylon, whom he mentions in the first prophecy; but the second, which he records in the later prophecy, he does not refer to Nebuchadnezzar. This proves that Nebuchadnezzar is not the agent of that second destruction.*

II. *The second point is this: that in the first desolation mentioned in the first prophecy, the Prophet determines a certain duration for that destruction and servitude—namely, for 70 years or until the time of the grandson of Nebuchadnezzar. However, in the later destruction of Edom described in the second prophecy, no such time limit is found, because that destruction is to be universal, total, and perpetual. This again proves that the later desolation should not be attributed to Nebuchadnezzar.*

III. *The third point is this: you will not find it said of the first desolation (which Jeremiah records in the earlier prophecy) that no one was to be left as a survivor or a fugitive among the Edomites. On the contrary, it is mentioned there that all those nations, including Edom, were to serve Nebuchadnezzar, his son, and his grandson. From this it is proven that many of the Edomites were left remaining, living in their own land, but as servants.*

(IV.) *As for the later deportation, which the Prophet mentions in the later oracle, he says of it in this place: "As Sodom and Gomorrah were overthrown with their neighbors, says the Lord, no one shall dwell there nor shall a son of man stay there." This teaches that this particular desolation is universal and absolute, and consequently it is not the same one that Nebuchadnezzar brought upon Edom.*

From generation to generation it shall lie waste; for ever and ever no one shall pass through it, etc.* chapter 34, verse 9 and following.

Thus, all the remaining verses are similar in their tone of threat to the words of *Jeremiah*; and I have already proven in that same place that it is impossible for that prophecy to be explained in any other way than as referring to a future time and to the *Roman* empire, as well as the province itself. And *Abraham ibn Ezra* <note>original: "Aben-Esra"; a famous 12th-century Spanish Jewish scholar.</note> is indeed remarkable, for in his *Commentary* on *Isaiah* regarding that same section, he writes: the words prove that a future time is being looked toward there, and there are those who say that *Bozrah* is *Constantinople*. Now, if this section (of *Isaiah*) deals with a future time, then the later prophecy of *Jeremiah*—which I mentioned and which matches Jeremiah’s words in its phrasing—must also necessarily be referred to the future. Thus, it appears clearly enough that *Obadiah* also, in his prophecy and discourse, agrees with *Jeremiah* regarding the words of that later prophecy mentioned; so that those three—namely *Isaiah* in the said section, *Jeremiah* in the later prophecy, and *Obadiah* in his present prophecy—necessarily prophesied about a future time and not about the desolation caused by *Nebuchadnezzar*.

VI. The sixth point is that *Jeremiah*, in the later prophecy which he issued concerning *Edom*, as I said, adds toward the end:

> *Therefore hear the counsel of the Lord, which He has taken against Edom, and the thoughts that He has cherished against the inhabitants of Teman: shall the shepherd boys not drag them away? Shall they not lay waste their dwellings?*

Certainly, these "shepherd boys" are not the *Israelites*, as *Rabbi David Kimchi* <note>A prominent medieval Jewish commentator and grammarian (1160–1235).</note> thought, but the *Persians*, as the <term>sages of blessed memory <note>original: "h. sap. f. m." (horum sapientum felicis memoriae); a standard way to refer to Talmudic rabbis.</note></term> say. They have received the truth through tradition: that *Rome* shall fall by the hands of the *Persians*; and they prove it from this passage. We also have...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Abarbanel's commentary on the prophecy against Edom. It focuses on identifying "Edom" as Rome and argues that the predicted destruction is a future event, rather than one already fulfilled by the Babylonians.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL</header>
<sig>VII. A</sig>

...reason 1. through the <term>*Haggadah* <note>The non-legalistic, narrative, and homiletic part of Rabbinic literature.</note></term>, proving that the matter stands according to the words of the *Sages of blessed memory* <note>original: "Sap. f. m." (Sapientum felicis memoriae)</note> because you will find *Jeremiah* prophesying about the desolation of *Babylon*, in these very words in *chapter 50, verse 45*: *Therefore hear the counsel of the Lord, which he has decreed against Babylon etc. Surely the least of the flock shall drag them away.* We know, however, that the desolation of *Babylon* was carried out by the *Persians* and not by the *Israelites*. From this, it is concluded that *Rome* too will fall by the *Persians* and, consequently, this desolation of *Idumaea* <note>The Greek and Latin name for the biblical land of Edom.</note> was not brought by *Nebuchadnezzar* but is to be brought by the *Persians*. 

But indeed, who are the *Persians*? Behold, all historians assert that the nation of the *Turks* originated from the *Persians*: And in the <term>*Gemara* <note>The second part of the Talmud, consisting of rabbinical commentary and analysis of the Mishnah.</note></term> *Rabbi Simai* and other *Rabbis* dispute concerning *Tiras* in *Genesis chapter 10, verse 2*. One said: *He is a Turk*; the other side said: *He is a Persian*; the rest settled the matter, saying he is a *Persian* and nonetheless a *Turk*. It is manifest, therefore, from all these things that the desolation which *Obadiah* predicted for *Idumaea*—just as *Jeremiah* declared it with equivalent words and with <term>clarifying detail <note>original: "epexegetî" (from the Greek epexēgēsis), a clarifying addition or explanation.</note></term>—can by no means be explained as the desolation caused by the *King of Babylon*, but necessarily looks toward a future time. 

Nor should it offend you what *Jeremiah* has in his later prophecy in *verse 12*: *Behold, those whose judgment was not to drink of the cup have surely drunk; and are you the one who should go unpunished? You shall not go unpunished, but you shall surely drink, etc.* and in the same way he speaks of the cup in the earlier prophecy. For every divine vengeance to be inflicted on one or more nations is usually expressed by the name of a "cup." Do you not see what *Isaiah* says to the *Israelites* in *chapter 51, verse 22*: *Behold, I have taken out of your hand the cup of trembling, even the dregs of the cup of my fury; and I will put it into the hand of those who afflict you.* And *Abraham ibn Ezra* <note>A renowned medieval Spanish Sephardic Jewish scholar and commentator.</note> long ago admitted that this threat looks to the future. 

I wonder, however, why he is not ashamed to write regarding the book of *Lamentations, chapter 4*, that it was said concerning the desolation which *Nebuchadnezzar* brought upon *Idumaea* and concerning the visitation of the Second Temple: *The punishment of your iniquity is accomplished, O daughter of Zion; he will no more carry you away into captivity; he will visit your iniquity, O daughter of Edom.* For the words, *The punishment of your iniquity is accomplished, O daughter of Zion*, do not fit the Second Temple; and how, I ask, was that fulfilled which he adds: *He will no more carry you away into captivity?* Since this passage must necessarily be explained as referring to the future liberation, it follows that the words, *He will visit your sin, O daughter of Edom*, must only be explained as referring to the desolation that will occur in the end times.

# ON OBADIAH

VII. The seventh point is—and you should know—that the end of the prophecy of Obadiah clarifies its beginning, and thus the whole prophecy looks toward a future time. This is certain, first from what is said: *And the deportation of this army of the children of Israel, who are among the Canaanites, as far as Zarephath <note>original: "Zarpat"</note> etc.* This is a prophecy which <term>Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a celebrated Spanish Jewish scholar, grammarian, and biblical commentator.</note></term> himself is forced to refer to a future time. Second, it is certain because it is added: *And Saviors shall ascend Mount Zion to judge Mount Esau etc.* This was fulfilled neither in the time of the desolation by *Nebuchadnezzar*, nor in any other past time. Here, then, are seven trustworthy testimonies and seven sources of responses which you may hurl back at the words of *Aben Esra*, and <note>original: "p. diruas dentes..."</note> break the teeth in the mouths of the "wise of the nations" <note>This refers to Christian scholars or secular historians who followed Ibn Ezra's literal, historical interpretation rather than the traditional Jewish view of Edom as Rome.</note> who follow him in this opinion.

Furthermore, as for what the same author writes—that we are not today in the "Edomite" captivity <note>The Jewish tradition that the Roman Empire and subsequent Christian world are the spiritual and political descendants of Esau/Edom.</note>—as he mentions in his *Commentary on Daniel*, this is an error. It occurred to him because he thinks *Rome* is included under *Greece*, and that *Alexander the Great* <note>original: "Alexander Macedo"</note> reigned over it, and that we are now under the power of the *Turks*. But it is not so. For *Alexander* never ruled *Rome*, nor did he ever come into *Italy* during his entire life; rather, departing from his land of *Macedonia*, he waged war against the kings of *Persia*, and having grown strong there, he established a wide empire and finally died. Behold, however, this is the truth: that the *Romans* always waged war with the *Greeks*. When the *Jews* were superior to the *Greeks* in the time of the *Maccabees*, the *Romans* entered into treaties and peace with the *Jews* because they had brought war against their common *Greek* enemies. From that familiarity, the *Romans* later pushed into the affairs of the *Jews*, and having become superior to them, they laid them waste. Indeed, *Titus*, who devastated them, was a General of the *Roman* army, as the whole matter is clearly set forth in their books. Thus, our captivity is under the *Romans*, and it will endure under them until the Lord looks down and sees from heaven. Those who are called *Ishmaelites* <note>A common term for Muslims in medieval literature.</note> today were at that time subject to *Rome* and were counted among the *Romans*; they had all received the law of *Jesus of Nazareth* at the command of the *Emperor Constantine*. *Aben Esra* himself taught this long ago regarding *Daniel*. Consequently, all *Israelites* who are today under the power of the *Ishmaelites* and *Christians* are considered to be in the "Edomite" captivity, while the Prophets predict that *Idumaea* is to be desolated at the time of the liberation from the *Ishmaelites*.

<meta>This page continues the "Examen Abarbanelis," a systematic rebuttal of the Jewish scholar Isaac Abarbanel’s interpretation of the prophecy of Obadiah. The author is refuting the claim that Obadiah refers only to a far-future destruction of Edom (symbolizing Rome/Christianity), arguing instead for a multi-stage historical fulfillment.</meta>

<header>TO OBADIAH. 59</header>

Now he [Abarbanel] begins to fortify the thesis which he has so far presented to us in a rather bare and crude manner, using certain reasons, with Jeremiah called upon as a witness above all. We praise the honesty of Ibn Ezra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a distinguished Spanish Jewish scholar, poet, and grammarian known for his literal approach to the biblical text.</note>, because truth, even when heard from an enemy, is beautiful; but we shall receive Abarbanel’s flimsy <note>The Latin *ficulnea* literally means "made of fig-wood," a classical idiom for something weak or useless.</note> arguments as they deserve. This vain man counts seven arguments, though he has barely brought forward six, hoping for victory from the number of arguments if he cannot win by their weight. Otherwise, his reasons come down to this:

1. Whatever destruction is not attributed to Nebuchadnezzar by the Prophets, he is not the author of it. But the later destruction of Edom <note>Idumea</note> predicted by Jeremiah chapter 49, Isaiah chapter 34, and Obadiah, is not attributed to Nebuchadnezzar. **Therefore [he is not the author].** However, the earlier destruction of Edom, which is different from that one, is attributed to Nebuchadnezzar in *Jeremiah 25:21*.

2. If no end-point is set for a destruction, but rather all limits are denied, Nebuchadnezzar is not its author. But for the later destruction etc. **Therefore.** On the contrary, a certain limit is fixed for the earlier one in *Jeremiah 25:11 and 27:7*.

3. Of which destruction no remains were to be left, just like the destruction of Sodom, Nebuchadnezzar is not its author. But from the later destruction etc. **Therefore.**

4. Whatever destruction is predicted for Bozrah <note>Bozrah was a major city in Edom.</note> could not be fulfilled in the Edom near Palestine; for that land had no Bozrah. The later destruction is predicted for Bozrah. **Therefore.**

5. Whatever destruction was to be brought by the Persians, Nebuchadnezzar is not its author. But the later destruction of Edom was to be brought by the Persians: for it is clear from *Jeremiah 50:45* that the "youngest of the flock" <note>original Latin: *pueros pecuarios*, referring to the humble or young ones mentioned in the prophecy.</note> mentioned in *Jeremiah 49:20* were the Persians. **Therefore.**

6. If the end of a prophecy looks toward the end of times, so does its beginning: for the end ought to correspond to its beginning. But the end of Obadiah’s prophecy (verse 21) looks toward future times. **Therefore.**

These are Abarbanel’s "Achillean" <note>An "Achillean" argument refers to one considered invincible or the strongest point in a case.</note> arguments (if it pleases Homer’s gods!), in which he commits a nearly constant "ignorance of the refutation" <term>ignoratio elenchi: a logical fallacy where the speaker addresses the wrong point or fails to meet the opponent's actual claim.</term>; for Christians do not claim that Nebuchadnezzar was the adequate and sole cause of the Edomite destruction viewed as a whole, but rather an inadequate or partial cause. Indeed, everything predicted by Obadiah up to verse 9 happened under him; however, others concurred in the total destruction <note>original Greek: πανωλεθριαν (*panolethrian*), meaning utter ruin or complete annihilation.</note> predicted in verses 9, 11, 16, and elsewhere. Nebuchadnezzar began the destruction of the Edomites; the Persians, Greeks, and Maccabees continued it; Titus Vespasian <note>Roman Emperor who, as a general, conducted much of the war against the Jews which also affected surrounding regions like Edom.</note> completed it. Thus all the Edomites perished utterly and completely, but not all at once or in a single moment. Therefore, Abarbanel mostly argues with ghosts and the phantoms of his own brain, rather than with Christians. Nevertheless, we will address the individual arguments specifically. 

Response to the First Argument: There is no [validity to it]. 1. It is...
