<meta>This page continues the "Examen Abarbenelis," a systematic rebuttal of the Jewish scholar Isaac Abarbanel’s interpretation of the prophecy of Obadiah. The previous page ended with the third of Abarbanel's six arguments, which the author is now refuting point-by-point.</meta>

### EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL
<center>60</center>

<margin>I.</margin>
**Response:** I respond to the first premise of the connection, if he is speaking of an explicit mention. It would follow with equal ease that, since Jeremiah's prophecy is expressly referred to no one, no one would be the author of the destruction. For whoever Abarbanel, or anyone else, might name as the authors of that fantastic war, we will similarly assume concerning them: But the destruction is not expressly referred to these specific people. **Therefore**, they are not its authors. In the same way, the destruction of Moab and Ammon in *Jeremiah 48 and 49* is not <term>explicitly</term> <note>original Greek: aparrhēdēn</note> referred to Nebuchadnezzar. **Therefore**, will Nebuchadnezzar not be the author of that either? 2. Implicitly, however, and by consequence, the Edomite destruction is referred to Nebuchadnezzar in Obadiah. We establish it this way: Whosoever’s destruction Obadiah explains the cause of, he hints at that destruction by making mention of it. But he explains the cause of the destruction brought upon the Edomites by Nebuchadnezzar in *verses 10 and 11*, as Abarbanel himself does not deny. **Therefore**, he hints at it. But Abarbanel is ignorant of the reason why the Edomite destruction in *Jeremiah 25* is referred to the author Nebuchadnezzar, but not so in *Jeremiah 49*? Let him therefore learn it. *Jeremiah 25* deals precisely with that partial destruction of the Idumæans <note>Edomites</note>, of which Nebuchadnezzar was the adequate author; *Jeremiah 49* deals with the destruction of the Idumæans viewed universally and totally, of which there was not just one author. There, therefore, Nebuchadnezzar alone is named; here, in a successive destruction, a single certain author is not assigned.

To the **II.** **Response:** By limiting the first premise: Nebuchadnezzar is not the *sole* author of it, which we grant with both hands. Moreover, in *Jeremiah 25 and 27*, a certain limit is fixed for the Edomite destruction, because the discourse is precisely about the destruction by Nebuchadnezzar; but the total destruction predicted in *Jeremiah 49* had no limit, because from a total loss <note>of a state or condition</note> back to its possession, a return is never granted. 

To the **III.** **Response:** Likewise by limiting the first premise: Since Jeremiah predicts that simply and absolutely no remains will be left, Nebuchadnezzar is not the sole author of that destruction, in which sense we concede the argument. However, even so, the second premise is not certain enough. For a conclusion is not always granted from the prediction of a punishment to its execution: for here, as we have previously warned, the exception of repentance is valid, and thus the prediction is hypothetical, even though it appears absolute according to the letter. The Jews, indeed, wish this to be said only of themselves and, because of their own <term>hatred of mankind</term> <note>original Greek: misanthropia</note>, they close off every entrance to repentance for the Gentiles; but the <term>Lover of Mankind</term> <note>original Greek: Philanthrōpos</note>, Jehovah, feels otherwise in *Jeremiah 12:14, 15, 16 and chapter 18:7, 8*. Now, that the Idumæans at some point performed repentance, not even the Jews will deny, since they know they were made proselytes under Hyrcanus <note>John Hyrcanus (reigned 134–104 BCE), the Hasmonean leader who forcibly converted the Edomites to Judaism.</note>; nor will the Christians deny it, since the Idumæans...

<meta>This page continues the "Examen Abarbenelis," specifically the refutation of the Jewish scholar Isaac Abarbanel's arguments concerning the destruction of Edom (the Idumæans). ### ON OBADIAH
<center>61</center>

Christ’s audience included [Idumæans], as counted in Mark 3:8, and together with the proselytes in Acts 2:11, Idumæans were undoubtedly present at Peter’s sermon. Furthermore, as we noted above on page 21, universal terms used in the prediction of punishments are not always to be taken with such rigor that they allow for no exception of individuals. Indeed, Jeremiah 49:11 clearly exempts certain ones from among the Idumæans, although Vatablus <note>François Vatable, a 16th-century French humanist and scholar</note>, following Kimchi <note>David Kimchi, a medieval Jewish biblical commentator</note>, attaches another meaning to those words; for this reason, our opponent <note>Abarbanel</note> decided those words should be plainly omitted from his lengthy citation as they were poorly suited to his purpose. 

And even if the Jews are the most rigid of critics <term>Aristarchuses</term> <note>A reference to Aristarchus of Samothrace, known for his harsh and meticulous literary criticism</note>, what more do they require for the total destruction <note>original Greek: panolethrian</note> of the Idumæans, seeing as all their posterity has been wiped from the face of the earth? For if any do survive, having been grafted into other nations over so many generations, they no longer recognize their original lineage at all. 

But Abarbanel may say that what happened long ago to Sodom and the Sodomites—to whom the Prophets compare the Idumæans—has not yet happened to them and their land. But the Rabbi is ignorant of the fact that it is a custom of the Prophets, when they are about to announce punishment to sinners, to place before their eyes some notable example of divine wrath and vengeance. They do this to affect them more deeply and stir their emotions <note>original Greek: pathē</note> more vehemently, not because they intend for them to perish in every respect and precisely in that manner and no other. 

Thus, a destruction similar to that of Sodom is predicted for Babylon in Jeremiah 50:39–40; and Babylon was indeed destroyed, yet in such a way that some of its ruins still remain by the Euphrates. It was not submerged by fire and brimstone sent from heaven like Sodom (compare Diodorus Siculus, book 2, chapter 9; Strabo, and others). Similarly, by reason of its desolation, Moab is compared to Sodom and Ammon to Gomorrah in Zephaniah 2:9. Neither, however, was overturned in that exact manner. Though both remain largely deserted, the former is called "Syria Sobal" today, and the latter Philadelphia <note>The ancient Greek name for Rabbath-Ammon, modern-day Amman, Jordan</note>. 

Therefore, such predictions are to be explained through metaphor (and according to many, through hyperbole) and not literally <note>original Greek: kata gramma</note>. If Abarbanel refuses to admit this, let him swallow this morsel: the sins of the Israelites are described throughout the Holy Scriptures as being equal to those of the Sodomites, or even greater (Isaiah 1:10, 3:9; Ezekiel 16:48; Amos 4:11; Lamentations 4:6). For those whose sins are equal, equal punishments await them, just as total destruction <note>original Greek: panolethria</note> like that of Sodom is certainly predicted for the Israelites who transgress the law in Deuteronomy 29:23. 

Were the Israelites, therefore, also to be extirpated like the Sodomites? Whatever the Jews might respond to that, we shall respond to our opponent. And even if they take everything most literally, since they cannot deny that the Idumæans sometimes performed penance, they will at least concede that if not total destruction, such...

<sig>I 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...such</meta>

<summary>The author argues that prophetic comparisons to the destruction of Sodom are often metaphorical or hyperbolic rather than literal, noting that while the Idumæan nation has vanished, individuals were spared or converted.</summary>
<keywords>Idumæans, Edom, Sodom, metaphor, hyperbole, Abarbanel, Kimchi, Vatablus, prophecy, destruction</keywords>

->## EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL<-

<center>62</center>

...nevertheless turned away the manner of **total destruction** <note>original Greek: *panolethrias*</note>. And how does our Prophet himself say that the Israelites will occupy Idumæa, if it is to be destroyed like Sodom?

**To the 4th Response.** By denying the reasoning added to the Major Premise. Certainly, just as Abarbanel played the bad mathematician in the calculation of his arguments, he now plays the worst geographer. In place of a reason, he adds here that *Bozrah in Jeremiah 48:24 refers to the land of Moab.* Thus, also on *Jeremiah 49, folio 147, column 3*, he says: *In Idumæa there is no city called Bozrah, but rather in Moab, as Jeremiah said above in chapter 48, verse 24:* "Upon Kerioth and upon Bozrah and upon all the cities of Moab." *But in Idumæa there is no Bozrah; rather, Rome is so called, etc.* <note>Abarbanel is following a medieval Jewish tradition that identified the biblical Edom and its cities as symbolic of the Roman Empire and, by extension, the Christian world.</note> Truly, this is a weak inference <note>original Latin: *elumbis*, literally "boneless"</note> and one consumed by a hectic fever: "There is a city called Bozrah in Moab. Therefore, there is no city of that name in Idumæa." If Abarbanel thinks this follows logically, he deserves to be laughed at by all the novices of logic <note>original Latin: *pullis Logicorum*, "the chicks of the logicians"</note>. 

But we respond to this ridiculous man seriously. Some believe that the Bozrah of Moab was Edomite—not geographically or by position, but politically and by territory—since the Idumæans occupied it along with the rest of Moab on more than one occasion. So says **Bunting** on page 204. And **Rashi** <note>Shlomo Yitzchaki, the preeminent 11th-century Jewish commentator</note> on Amos 1:12 says: *Bozrah was situated between Moab and Idumæa* <gloss>and also located after Edom</gloss> *but it was attributed to Idumæa.* 

However, besides others, **Adrichomius** <note>Christian van Adrichem, a 16th-century priest and cartographer of the Holy Land</note> on page 80, followed by **Heidmann** in *Palestine*, chapter 10, number 26, establish a triple Bozrah. The first, which is called **Bozra** by Ptolemy and is now commonly called **Busrit**, is identified by Heidmann in the cited passage as being in Trachonitis; but according to Brocard, William of Tyre (Book 16, chapter 8; Book 13, chapter 2, etc.), Jacques de Vitry, and others, it is the metropolis of all Arabia Petræa. This city has Mount Seir in its vicinity—which is also called Sanir and Hermon—and it is distinct from the mountain near the desert of Paran which is attributed to "Lower Idumæa" (as Josephus calls it), which is the region we primarily mean by the name Idumæa. Some contend, however, that both mountains were once inhabited by Esau. (Compare Brocard and Sanutus, Book 3, Part 14, chapter 4). The second Bozrah was in Moab (*Jeremiah 48:24*). The third was in Idumæa. 

To these, we add a fourth: **Bazra in Chaldea**, which is also called Bosra, Bassora, Balsara, Beçoro, and Teredon <note>This refers to the modern city of Basra in Iraq</note>. (Compare Schickard’s *Tarich*, pages 145 and 183; the Persian Nasir al-Din and the Tatar Ulugh Beg in the *Geographical Tables* edited by Greaves, pages 14 and 46). 

Regarding the Bozrah of Idumæa, Adrichomius says on page 118: *Bozrah is a distinguished city in Idumæa, situated not far from the city of Teman; it takes its name from its very strong fortification.* And indeed, in Isaiah 34:6, 63:1, Jeremiah 49:13, 22, and Amos 1:12, Bozrah is clearly referred to Idumæa. To crown this evidence, let the testimony of **Bechai** <note>Bahya ben Asher, a 13th-century Spanish rabbi and scholar</note> be added, who says in his commentary on the *Pentateuch* (folio 46, column 1): *the places of Edom mentioned in the Text are Bozrah and Teman.* Now, Teman [is] of Idumæa...

### ON OBADIAH. ###

Furthermore, our author <note>Referring to Abarbanel</note> proves very weakly that the Bozrah of Idumæa is rather Rome, which is so called because of its fortification. But in the same way others, cited by Ibn Ezra and rejected by Abarbanel, would make Constantinople into Bozrah; and we, by the same reasoning, might prove Paris or Cairo to be Bozrah, since all of these can make the palm of victory for size and fortification ambiguous for Rome, and they would belong to Idumæa by the same right as Rome itself.

**To the 5th Response.** The Major Premise must again be limited: Whatever is to be brought about by the Persians alone, Nebuchadnezzar is not the cause of its destruction. Thus, however, the Minor Premise turns out to be false. And the passages cited hardly prove that the Idumæans are to be desolated by the Persians, let alone by them alone. For, first, it is an inconsistent consistency <note>original Greek: *anakolouthon akolouthia*</note>: by the "shepherd boys" in *Jeremiah 50*, the Persians are understood. Therefore, also in *Jeremiah 49*. Certainly, nothing prevents the Babylonians from also being called shepherd boys. For <term>צעירי הצאן</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Tze'irei ha-tzon*</note> or *shepherd boys* refers in general to the vilest camp-followers, the dregs of the soldiers, whether they be Babylonians, or Persians, or Rutulians, or Trojans. 

But let it be: let those shepherd boys be Persians. Idumæa was certainly desolated under Cyrus, even by the Persians, as we said above. But this does not suffice for the Rabbi. To him, the Idumæans themselves are Christians, and the Persians are Turks originating from Persians; once this metamorphosis is assumed, it follows that Christians will one day be cut off by the Turks. Who would have sought such a fish in such a whirlpool! <note>A Latin idiom meaning "Who would have expected to find such a significant (though in this case, bizarre) conclusion in such a strange argument?"</note> But 1. That Christians are Idumæans is—to speak as mildly as possible—not the truth. 2. That the "Persians" here are Turks is a very great assumption <note>original Greek: *mega lian aitēma*</note>. Abarbanel proves this: 
1. From the discourse of the Talmudists concerning Tiras in *Genesis 10*, and from the Talmudic tradition by which it is held that Rome will fall by the Persians. 
2. From the consensus of historians asserting that the Persians gave origin to the Turks. 

But we respond to the 1st: In that discourse, the Talmudic brothers disagree, which moves us very little, even where they do agree. He therefore deals in great trifles who busies himself trying to convince a Christian using the testimonies of disagreeing Talmudists. Thus, let that tradition about Rome falling by the Persians pass away with the rest of the lies; even if it were most true, it still would not make Idumæans out of Christians and Turks out of Persians. As for *Tiras*, from whom Rashi—following the Talmudists for the most part with eyes closed—derives the Persians, the more common opinion of interpreters is that he gave origin to the Thracians. Regarding the 2nd: Abarbanel would have done something worthwhile if he had produced clear testimonies from classic historians concerning that origin of the Turks. Otherwise, Persians are deduced from Elam, the son of Shem, in *Genesis 10:22*. The Turks, however, from Togarmah,

<meta>This page continues the logical refutation of Isaac Abarbanel’s arguments regarding the prophecy of Obadiah and its application to Rome (as Edom). The previous page ended with a discussion on the identity of the "shepherd boys" mentioned in Jeremiah and how they relate to the desolation of Edom by the Persians or Babylonians.</meta>

# AN EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL #

the grandson of Japheth <note>original: "nepote Japheti"</note> [Genesis 10:3], and they are said to have first burst into Asia from the north through the narrow passes of the Caucasus. **To the 6th Response.** The major premise <note>In a formal logical argument, the "major premise" is the broad general rule from which a specific conclusion is drawn.</note> is mostly false. Not all prophecies have a uniform object, whether real or personal, and thus they do not always end in the same place where they began. However, a prophecy that deals with the same subject proceeds in order and succession, so that the things to be fulfilled soonest occupy the first place, and the last things occupy the extreme end. Generally, the Prophets terminate their visions in the times of the **New Testament**, and thus again it is not permitted to conclude from the time of the fulfillment of the later parts of a prophecy to the fulfillment of its beginning. Nor does the exception hold that the end must correspond to the beginning; this is denied and is only admitted in the sense that the end does not destroy the beginning or contradict it, so that in a continuous prophecy there is a consistency of order, which a different time of fulfillment does not take away. 

But there are two more points to be briefly addressed before we dismiss our author. The first is that he mocks **Ibn Ezra** for interpreting a passage in *Lamentations 4* <note>original: "Thren. IV."</note> as referring to the destruction of the Second Temple. For, he says, if that is the case, why is it said: *He will no more carry thee away into captivity?* <note>Lamentations 4:22</note> But in truth, the Prophet is speaking only of the Babylonian exile, and he implies that nothing more remains for the present punishments of the people—at this time, specifically—not denying that God has reserved another punishment for them if they continue to sin. 2. Regarding what he says: that the Jews under the **Turks** are also considered captives of the Christians, because the ancestors of the Turks were once Christians under **Constantine**. But this is a most bold-faced lie <note>original: "calidissimum est mendacium"</note> arising from utter ignorance. Constantine the Great did not force the ancestors of the Turks into the Christian religion—their name was not even known at that time—but perhaps he converted the ancestors of the inhabitants whom the Turks later drove out. And perhaps the Jews living in great numbers in Arabia, Persia, India, Tartary, and many places in Africa are also to be considered as being under the power of the Christians? If it is permitted to affirm everything in this way without reason, who will ever be innocent?

## ABARBENEL. ##

Furthermore, these things having been thus truly demonstrated, we return now to the exposition of the words of **Obadiah**, where I say in addition: *Just as we do not accept from Rabbi Ibn Ezra that this entire prophecy deals with the destruction of the First Temple, so we do not listen to Rabbi David Kimhi when he writes that the entire prophecy speaks of the destruction of the Second Temple, whose authors were the Romans.*

It is clear from this that the Edomites themselves were not the ones causing the desolation, but that they came to watch the destruction and therefore "widened their mouth" <note>This phrase refers to mocking or speaking boastfully against someone in distress.</note>. But I say: that the Prophet here commemorates both how the Edomites looked upon the Israelites in the destruction of the first temple, how they acted wickedly toward them in the destruction of the second temple, and how they also afflicted them in their final exile. And since Obadiah foresaw this a long time ago and before the rest of the Prophets who prophesied about it, he uses brief words and says: *Because of the violence against your brother Jacob, shame shall cover you,* [Obadiah 1:10]. He says this because the Edomites rejoiced in the first destruction and hurled taunts, saying: *Lay it bare, lay it bare even to its foundation* (Psalm 137:7). 

Furthermore, what he says next—*And you shall be cut off forever*—refers to what they did during the time of the second temple, where the Edomites themselves desolated and set fire to the Sanctuary of the Lord. It is as if he were saying: At first, indeed, because of the injury you did to your brother Jacob, shame will cover you, because you did not maintain the bond of brotherhood and love with him; but afterwards you did a far greater injury, for which shame will not merely cover you, but you shall be cut off forever. He explains both things—namely, what they did in the first destruction—when he says: *On the day when you stood on the other side,* as if to say: you indeed committed the mentioned injury on that day when you stood opposite, while strangers were capturing the forces of the Israelites and foreigners were entering their gates and casting lots over Jerusalem. 

And the meaning of the saying, *Even you were like one of them,* is that although you contributed nothing to the desolation at 그 time, because you were there, you are considered like one of the destroyers, who were the Babylonians. For it was not fitting for you to act as a spectator on the day of your brother, the day of his calamity; as if to say, even that very sight was unseemly for you—to know of and watch their destruction—just as the sages of blessed memory <term>f. m.</term> <note>Abbreviation for the Latin *felicis memoriae*, used to honor deceased scholars.</note> say: "Do not strive to see a wretched man in the time of his ruin." Nor was it enough for you to go and watch the destruction, but you also committed another injury when you rejoiced, for it was not proper to be glad for the Jews <note>original: "Judæos"</note> at the time of their destruction; but furthermore, you did evil in that you "magnified your mouth," hurling taunts against them. ### AN EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL ###

*on this he says: **And do not boast with your mouth in the day of distress,** [Obadiah 1:12] i. e., it was not your place to boast in the day of distress. After he had explained the injury they committed in the first destruction and had called them "brothers"—because those Edomites who committed that injury were neighbors to the land of Israel, joined to them in peace and brotherhood—he then commemorates what they did in the destruction of the second temple. But there he does not call them brothers, because the authors of that desolation were the Romans, whose genealogy is Edomite <note>In medieval Jewish exegesis, particularly that of Abarbanel, the Roman Empire and the subsequent Christian world were identified as the descendants of Edom (Esau).</note>; meanwhile, they were not as closely related to the Ishmaelites <note>Likely referring here to the neighboring desert tribes or perhaps used as a general term for regional non-Jewish groups.</note> as those neighbors were. This is why he says: **Do not enter the gate of my people in the time of their calamity,** [Obadiah 1:13] as if he were saying: "But there is also a greater sacrilege which you committed afterward, for which you shall be cut off forever." That is, it was not the place of the Roman Edomites to enter the gate of my people to subjugate and desolate them on the day of the calamity I brought upon them, just as Nebuchadnezzar <note>The Babylonian king who destroyed the First Temple in 586 BCE.</note> had done. In the same way, Ezekiel says in chapter 25, verse 12: **Because Edom has acted in taking vengeance against the house of Judah.** And Joel says in chapter 3, verse 19: **Because of the injury to the children of Judah, in whose lands they shed innocent blood**, all of which signify the desolation of the second temple. And using the same phrase with which Obadiah had commemorated the first destruction, saying: **And strangers shall enter his gates**, he now commemorates the second destruction: **You shall not enter the gate of my people.** For this is a <term>periphrasis</term> <note>A roundabout or indirect way of speaking.</note> for the subduing and occupation of Jerusalem. Furthermore, what he says next—**Neither should you have looked on their misery in the day of their calamity**—refers to what Josephus Gorionides <note>The *Josippon*, a 10th-century history of the Jews written in Hebrew, which was widely attributed to the historian Josephus in the Middle Ages.</note> records as having happened during the siege of Jerusalem when Titus <note>The Roman general who captured Jerusalem and destroyed the Second Temple in 70 CE.</note> came against it. Specifically, when the Romans were attacking Jerusalem, the Edomites living in their cities near Jerusalem—due to the treaty that existed between them and the Jews—came to their aid and entered Jerusalem. However, when they saw their ruin and that the Jews were being forced into captivity because of the distress and the magnitude of their servitude, they too set their hands to plundering and spoiling. Thus, regarding the Romans, who are of Edomite origin and family, he says: **Do not enter the gate of my people in the day of their calamity**; but regarding the remaining Edomites, who came from Idumaea bordering Judea to help with their forces and afterwards turned into enemies, he says: **Nor should you even look upon his misery**. That is, just as it had not been right for the Edomites who are in Italy to enter the gates of Jerusalem to destroy it, so also for you, O Edom our neighbor, from—*

->ON OBADIAH.<-

it was not their place to come to gaze upon his misfortune on the day of his calamity, nor was it proper to lay a hand upon his army to plunder him on the day of his calamity. Furthermore, as for what he says: *neither should you stand at the crossroad* <note>original Hebrew: לפרק (*le-pereq*)</note> *to cut down his fugitives,* [Obadiah 1:14] this indicates the time of this current exile, wherein we sit in the land of Edom and weep because of the afflictions and persecutions with which they affect us. For the word *pereq* signifies a division and a breaking, in which sense the root is used in Zechariah 11:16: *and he shall tear off their hooves* <note>original: יפרק (*yepareq*)</note>, and in 1 Kings 19:11: *dividing* <note>original: פרק (*pōrēq*)</note> *the mountains and breaking the rocks.* It is as if he were saying: when the Jews had gone into captivity, it was not your place to press upon that rupture and fracture of theirs to destroy his fugitives, who had been snatched from the sword of destruction; nor was it your place to prepare a prison and the harshest servitude for those Jewish fugitives. This is what he means when he says: *neither should you deliver up his survivors in the day of distress.* Even Isaiah calls this destruction of yours a "prison" in chapter 24, verse 22, saying: *and they shall be shut up in the prison.* 

**In summary:** The Prophet commemorates the wickedness of the Idumæans <note>Edomites</note> and the evils they brought upon the Israelites, both in the first destruction, when Nebuchadnezzar desolated them while they rejoiced and spoke boastfully, and in the second destruction, the authors of which were those Idumæans who came from the others, as well as in this destruction, where they brought upon them the most grievous persecutions, slaughters, and imprisonments.

<note>a. Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian.</note> does indeed relate that certain Idumæans deserted to the Romans; however, the same illustrious witness states that they shared the same fate as the Jews in the final destruction.</note>

## EXAMINATION.

The Prophet had thus far reviewed the punishments of the Idumæans; he now subjoins the causes for these, yet not without further threats. However, the method we have observed up to this point requires us to demonstrate here, against Abarbenel, both the true sense of the prophecy and its certain fulfillment. We declare the mind of the Prophet thus: "Lest you think, O Edom, that you are being rebuked and punished while innocent, I will now expose to you your crimes by which you have provoked my wrath. Namely, **BECAUSE OF** the overt **VIOLENCE**, the plundering, and the slaughters (Habakkuk 2:8, 17) **AGAINST YOUR BROTHER** (that is, toward your brother, in an objective and passive sense), namely the nation of **JACOB**, for the descendants of brothers are also sometimes called brothers; towards the Israelites, you cast off all brotherly affection—imitating the habits of your forefather <note>Esau, the brother of Jacob.</note>—against the laws of nature (Amos 1:11, 12). **SHAME SHALL COVER YOU** because of the consciousness of your evil deeds, so that wherever you turn, you shall carry your ignominy around you like a garment (Psalm

## EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL

### Page 68

35. (Compare Obadiah v. 10; Psalm 109:18, 19, 29). And therefore, at last, YOU SHALL BE CUT OFF from the very root, as if hacked down and deleted from the memory of the living, along with your destruction <note>original Greek: πολλείᾳ (likely *apōleia*)</note> and all your posterity (regarding the Hebrew term *karat* <note>original: כרת, meaning "to cut off"</note> or "cutting off" <note>original Greek: ἐκκοπῇ</note> see also Sixtus Amama in *Antibiblica Biblica*, book 3, p. 606 and following) FOREVER and entirely. This is meant without hyperbole, though Castrus, à Lapide, and others believe hyperbole is necessary here. This happened on that DAY, IN WHICH YOU STOOD OVER AGAINST HIM, watching from afar (Psalm 38:12; 2 Kings 2:7) to see what would happen to the Jews. You were ready to join the enemies if the situation turned against the Jews, even though, for the sake of a friend and brother, you ought to have approached closer and offered help. On 그날 (that DAY), I say, IN WHICH STRANGERS—the Chaldeans (though Grotius wrongly identifies them as Syrians based on a false hypothesis)—LED HIS FORCES and his wealth INTO CAPTIVITY to Babylon. And those FOREIGNERS, like victors in a triumph, ENTERED THE GATES of his cities (others interpret "cities" here as a synecdoche <note>a figure of speech where a part represents the whole</note>) AND OVER JERUSALEM the metropolis THEY CAST LOTS. This was to decide who should attack it first, as noted by Winckelmann and the scholars of Jena, or rather—since the entry through the gates was already mentioned—it refers to the casting of lots among the soldiers to divide the citizens and goods of the occupied city. This casting of lots by ancient soldiers was done, as Drufius notes, with small wooden dice or tokens thrown into a jar, a garment's fold, or a helmet (Compare Joel 3:3; Proverbs 1:14). Then ALSO YOU—you, I say, for such behavior is not so surprising from strangers—behaved no differently THAN IF you were ONE OF THEM. You were consenting to and rejoicing in the deed (Psalm 13:6-7) and were plundering the city and burning the temple along with them (1 Esdras 4:45). 

But, O Edom, if there is still any room left in you for warning and repentance, do not continue to act this way. Since the word *al* <note>original: אל</note> is a prohibitive particle <note>a word used to issue a command or "thou shalt not"</note>, we judge these and the following words to be the voice of GOD, who out of His supreme love for mankind <note>original Greek: φιλανθρωπίᾳ</note> and long-suffering <note>original Greek: μακροθυμίᾳ</note> is dissuading and forewarning the Idumaeans. This view is shared by the Septuagint, Cyril, Tarnovius, Drufius, Junius, Calvin, Cocceius, Ribera, and Gualtherus (though we do not fully grasp the subtle hint of sarcasm Gualtherus sees here). However, because the absolutely negative particle *lo* <note>original: לא, meaning "not"</note> was not used, we can scarcely accept the opinion of Jerome, Theodoret, Hugo, Lyranus, Winckelmann, Gesner, Cornelius à Lapide, and others, who claim these are the words of God or the Prophet predicting a future state—as if to say: "Being completely occupied by your own misfortune, you will not be able to look upon, harass, or afflict the Jews in the future." Furthermore, since the particle *al* does not allow for a past tense (whether a literal past or a future tense changed into a past), we cannot agree with Pagninus, Arias Montanus, Vatablus, Prado (on Ezekiel 25:13), Mercerus, Grotius, the Dutch translators, or others on this point...

(We admit the pluperfect subjunctive in this sense: *You should not have looked*, that is, you ought not to have looked, etc.) **DO NOT LOOK** with joyful eyes, or with pleasure and a desire for the outcome (see *Psalm 22:18; Psalm 41:8; Micah 4:11*) **UPON THE DAY**, or the time of visitation <note>original: "visitationis," a theological term for God's judgment</note> **OF HIM** (for a "day" is sometimes attributed to a person specifically when something memorable happens, the memory of which is thereafter recalled. *Compare Psalm 37:13; Psalm 137:7; Micah 7:4; and below at verse 15.*) **THE DAY**, I say, **OF HIS ALIENATION**, in which he was compelled to live as an exile and an outcast among strangers and foreigners; **NOR**—so that you might not rejoice over his misfortune <note>original Greek: ἐπιχαιρεκακῇ (*epichairekakē*), meaning to gloat over someone's distress</note>—**SHOULD YOU REJOICE OVER THE PEOPLE OF JUDAH ON THE DAY OF THEIR DESTRUCTION, NOR SHOULD YOU MAGNIFY YOUR MOUTH**, as if insulting the wretched with an inflated mouth, parted lips, and a lolling tongue, using swollen, magnificent, and "foot-and-a-half-long" <note>original Latin: "sesquipedalibus," a Horace-inspired term for overly long, bombastic words</note> words (compare *Psalm 12:4, 5; Psalm 35:19, 25, 26; Psalm 22:8; 1 Samuel 2:3; Lamentations 2:16; Ezekiel 35:12, 13*) **ON THE DAY OF THEIR DISTRESS; NOR** should you, wishing to triumph along with the Chaldeans, so shamelessly and insolently **ENTER THE GATE** (Coccejus explains this passage less effectively as "the tribunal of affection") **OF MY PEOPLE**, who are most beloved even in the midst of their distress, **ON THE DAY OF THEIR CALAMITY; NOR**, while so many enemies are already gloating in their hearts, should **EVEN YOU** feed your eyes on this pitiable spectacle and look with joy **UPON HIS MISERY** (punishment as in *Amos 3:6*; misfortune as in *Genesis 19:19*) **ON THE DAY OF HIS CALAMITY; NOR SHOULD YOU STRETCH OUT** <note>original Hebrew: תשלחנה (*tishlachnah*). This is a feminine plural verb form, which is unusual here</note> (The author suggests: "You, O Edomites, who otherwise carry a feminine spirit, have now conceived boldness in your bellies since you see everything exposed to the plundering of the Chaldeans." Rabbi Kimchi, Mercerus, and Tarnovius explain it as: "Let your hands stretch themselves out" [intransitively]. Ribera suggests: "The hands of the Chaldeans shall not let you [go]." However, we strongly doubt that intransitive meaning of the word "stretch" <note>original Hebrew: שלח (*shalach*)</note>, and Ribera’s omission seems too harsh; therefore, we prefer to admit an emphatic "change of gender" <note>term: enallage of gender</note> with an omission of the noun **HAND** <note>original Hebrew: יד (*yad*)</note>, which is otherwise frequently constructed with this verb. Indeed, throughout the text, the Hebrew language leaves things to be understood by the attentive reader which can easily be gathered from the sense, as Mercerus advises on this passage) **FOR HIS WEALTH ON THE DAY OF CALAMITY, NOR SHOULD YOU STAND**, O Edom, **AT THE CROSSROADS** (*The Septuagint translates:* <gloss>at the passages out</gloss> <note>original Greek: ἐπὶ τὰς διεκβολάς</note>. *The Vulgate:* <gloss>In the exits</gloss>. *The Syriac:* <gloss>at the narrow entrance</gloss> <note>original Syriac: על מפקתא</note>. *The Arabic:* <gloss>at their mountain passes</gloss> <note>original Arabic: עלי מפתחתא</note>. Castrus and Tarnovius: *In the Breach*, i.e., in the broken places of the city, to which Castrus quite aptly applies the history of *2 Kings 25:4*. Kimchi, in his *Book of Roots*, explains "breach" <note>original Hebrew: שבר (*sheber*)</note> as a fracture or calamity, such that "to stand at the crossroads" <note>original Hebrew: עמד על פרק (*amad al pereq*)</note> is to stand over and threaten the ruin of another, to whom one...

<meta>This page continues the critique of Isaac Abarbenel's interpretation of Obadiah, focusing on verse 14 and the identification of the "Idumaeans" (Edomites) across different historical periods. The author argues against Abarbenel's tendency to link these prophecies to the Roman Empire and later Christians.</meta>

# THE EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL

Our author also emphasizes this point. The Hebrews usually explain this word—which is quite obscure and found only once or twice in the sacred text—at this passage as a *crossroads or a parting of ways*, derived from the root *perek*, meaning "to break," because at that point the road is, as it were, torn or broken. This is also how Martin Luther, Pagninus, Vatablus, Cornelius à Lapide, (Mercerus, and others) explain it. They observe that this was done so that none of the Jews might escape: **DO NOT STAND AT THE CROSSROADS** to slaughter **HIS FUGITIVES** who had perhaps slipped out of the city, of whom there was a great number, as is clear from 2 Kings 25:4–5. Instead of intercepting and killing them like bandits, it would have been more fitting for a kinsman <note>The Edomites were traditionally seen as the descendants of Esau, brother of Jacob (Israel), making them "kinsmen."</note> to show them the way. **AND DO NOT HAND OVER** or deliver into perpetual prisons to the enemies **HIS SURVIVORS IN THE TIME OF DISTRESS**. Unless you want to perish completely, do not add affliction to the afflicted; for God has a **just eye** <note>original Greek: ἔνδικον ὄμμα (*endikon omma*), a poetic term for divine justice that sees all crimes</note> and the day of the Lord is near.

Thus, no difficulty remains in the text itself. However, we must look further into the fulfillment of this prophecy: who committed this fault and when, and whom the added threats affect. We should not linger on the opinions of others, such as those who want to see the insults of the Idumaeans <note>Edomites</note> against the Israelites during the time of King Ahaz here (as read in 2 Chronicles 28:17); or Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius, a famous Dutch jurist and theologian</note>, who pushes these things to the time of Sennacherib and beyond; or Cocceius, who looks to the times of Antiochus and the Herods. Along with our author, we also reject the opinion of Kimchi <note>David Kimhi, a medieval Jewish commentator</note>, who asserts that all these things happened around the destruction of the Second Temple.

However, we judge our author's <note>Abarbenel's</note> exposition to be much more absurd. He claims that the words of verses 11 and 12 apply to the true Idumaeans during the time of the Babylonian exile; verse 13 to the true Idumaeans and their Roman **successors** <note>original Greek: ἐπιγόνους (*epigonous*)</note> at the time of the final destruction of Jerusalem; and verse 14 finally to the latter ones alone—that is, all Christians—who detain and afflict the Jews. 

But first, we must note in this passage a sycophantic **twisting** <note>original Greek: στρεβλότης (*streblotes*), meaning a distortion or crookedness</note> of the clear text. In a very confused process, Abarbenel drags some things toward the destruction of the First Temple, some toward the Second, and some toward the old and true Idumaeans, and others toward his own "new and fictional" Idumaeans. Sometimes both are joined together, or even set against each other. For example, during the siege of Jerusalem, the "Pseudo-Idumaean" Romans besieged the true Idumaeans; and without any doubt, these fictional Idumaeans did not treat the others more gently than, in the play by Plautus, the disguised Sosia treated the real Sosia <note>A reference to Plautus's comedy *Amphitruo*, where the god Mercury disguises himself as the slave Sosia and beats the real Sosia to convince him he doesn't exist.</note>. But this is to twist the sense of Holy Scripture, not to teach it. 

Second, verses 12, 13, and 14 contain a warning against an action described in verses 10 and 11, as both the parallelism of the words and the particle of prohibition clearly prove. Those acts listed in verses 10 and 11 belong to the time of the Babylonian exile.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly critique of Isaac Abarbenel’s commentary on the Book of Obadiah. The previous page established the historical context of the Edomites' crimes against Jerusalem during the Babylonian siege. This page argues against Abarbenel's timeline, asserting that the "Day of the Lord" mentioned by the prophet refers to the immediate aftermath of the Babylonian conquest rather than a far-future judgment.</meta>

# ON OBADIAH

Therefore <note>Latin: *E.*, abbreviation for *Ergo*</note>, this is also a warning against the deed, which certainly does not imply that the sin is yet to be committed, but rather calls them back from the sin that is currently being committed. 3. Those things said from verse 10 onwards took place before that "Day of the Lord" mentioned in verse 15. And yet, that Day of the Lord began immediately following the Babylonian deportation. Abarbenel does not deny the former point. We prove the latter from this: because that Day of the Lord also pertained to the Moabites, Ammonites, and others who likewise afflicted the Israelites; not long after the Israelites were deported, these nations were punished by Nebuchadnezzar, as is clear from verse 16 and Jeremiah chapters 48 and 49. Therefore, it is necessary that the events reviewed in verses 10, 11, 12, and following, took place shortly after the Babylonian deportation and indeed before that Day of the Lord which the Idumaeans <note>The Edomites</note> shared with the Moabites, Ammonites, and others. 4. Finally, the outcome itself clearly shows the meaning of these verses. Let subsequent and other passages be carefully compared: *Psalm 137:6-7, 2 Kings 25:4-5, Lamentations 2:16, 4 Esdras 4:45, etc.* <note>The text cites Psalm 136, but in modern Bibles, the lament over Jerusalem and Edom is Psalm 137.</note> Furthermore, let the added prophetic threats concerning the perpetual extermination of the Idumaeans move neither Abarbenel nor anyone else. The Prophet reviews the sin of the Idumaeans, for which they deserved destruction, as it happened in the time of Nebuchadnezzar; he calls the Idumaeans back from it, although, as the event showed, he did so in vain. However, Nebuchadnezzar alone did not bring total destruction upon them, as we have abundantly proved above.

->ABARBENEL<-

**FOR THE DAY OF THE LORD IS NEAR UPON ALL NATIONS**, and so on until the end of the book. *He [Abarbenel] means to say: You ought not, O Edom, to have done all those things, for the Day of the Lord is near to punish all the nations that did evil to the Israelites and deported them from their land; likewise, He will pour out His indignation upon those who afflicted them during their exile. Just as He punished all these, so He will also punish you; in the same way you acted, it shall be done to you; God will return your retribution upon your own head. Namely, as you killed, you yourself shall be killed by the sword; as you burned Jerusalem with fire, so you shall perish by fire, sulfur, and a storm-wind sent by the Lord from heaven. And do not let it perhaps disturb you that it says: For the Day of the Lord is NEAR, even though this threat looks toward very remote times. For the word* near <note>original Hebrew: קרוב (*karov*)</note> *does not always imply the speed of the time in which the event will occur, but is used more often regarding the possibility and ease of the thing itself, as in Deuteronomy 30:14: "The word is very near to you."*

<summary>This page argues that the prophecies of Edom's destruction were fulfilled shortly after the fall of Jerusalem by Nebuchadnezzar, refuting Abarbenel's attempt to push these events into a distant, end-times future.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Examine of Abarbenel," a scholarly critique of the Jewish commentator Isaac Abarbenel’s interpretation of the Book of Obadiah. # THE EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL

Indeed, that word also shares the notion of war, as in: "In the day when he waged war," the day of <term>קרב</term> <note>Hebrew: *qerev*, meaning "battle" or "warfare"</note> battle (*Zechariah 14:3*). "Wisdom is better than weapons of <term>קרב</term> war" (*Ecclesiastes 9:18*). "He has redeemed my soul in peace from the <term>קרב</term> battle that was against me" (*Psalm 55:19*). In this sense, you could also explain this passage: because the "Day of the Lord is <term>קרוב</term> <note>Hebrew: *qarov*, meaning "near" or "approaching"</note> near," both because the matter is possible and certain before God, and because that day is one of war and conflict for the Lord against all nations, so that he may repay each according to their merits. 

Furthermore, as for what he says next: *As you have drunk*, the sense is this: You should not say, O Edom, that there is any <term>favoritism</term> <note>Greek: *prosopolepsia*, the act of judging based on status or person rather than facts</note> here, as if I would not also punish the Assyrians and other peoples who afflicted the Israelites no less. For, behold, just as you Edomites drank—namely, the dregs of the cup of horror—upon my holy mountain through your slaughter and most heavy defeat, so too shall all the nations drink who carried away my people. It could also be explained as if, with the words *As you have drunk*, the Prophet turns to the Jews, whom he mentioned above; namely, that just as they drank the cup of horror on the holy mountain of the Lord during the desolation of Samaria, the deportation of the ten tribes, and the first and second desolations of Jerusalem, so also shall all nations drink as they engage in battles with one another. They shall not only drink but also exhaust the dregs and be consumed to such an extent that they will not even recognize their former place. In this sense, he says: *And they shall be as if they had not been*.

But do not think that the Israelites who will be in that land will fall or be consumed in that war in the same way: *Behold, in Mount Zion there shall be salvation and there shall be holiness*, that is, the uncircumcised or the polluted shall no longer pass through there. *And the sons of Jacob*—that is, all twelve tribes—*shall possess their possessions*, that is, the lands of those nations. Other interpreters explain "possessions" as their resources and spoils. 

Furthermore, the Prophet indicates that those tribes who are to return from the lands of Assyria, where they had been deported—and which are called here the *house of Joseph* and the *kingdom of Ephraim*—shall be like a fire and a flame to ignite and burn the *house of Esau*. For these tribes shall come with other nations prepared for that war and will join battle with the Christians <note>Abarbenel and other medieval Jewish commentators often used "Edom" and "Esau" as codes for Rome and the Christian world</note> and devour them, so that no survivor remains of the house of Esau. Our sages of blessed memory <note>Latin: *p.m.*, likely *piae memoriae*</note> discuss this in the sense that the descendants of Esau shall only fall by the hands of the descendants of Rachel. Nor does Amos contradict this when he says in chapter 9, verse 12 <note>The original text cites chapter 2, but the quote "possess the remnant of Edom" is found in Amos 9:12</note>: *that they may possess the remnant of Edom*. For in that place, the discussion is about the land which some of them are to possess; but here, our Prophet says that no one will [remain as a survivor]...

# TO OBADIAH.

*...at that time for the house of Esau with respect to the people—namely, the Edomites and the Romans—nor will anyone remain of them; and thus the fifth question is resolved.* <note>Abarbenel typically structured his commentaries by posing specific questions (dubia) about the text; here the author concludes the answer to the fifth one.</note> *But regarding the lands, our author says this:* And they shall possess the mountain of Esau toward the South. *That is: besides that notable vengeance which they will take upon the Edomites in that war, they shall further possess—namely, the previously mentioned sons of Joseph—the southern cities and the mountain of Esau, which is to the south of the Israelite land, as well as the plain, which is the land of the Philistines. Besides those things which they will acquire anew, they will also recover what they previously held, namely the field of Ephraim and Samaria. And just as the sons of Joseph shall expand their borders, so the Benjaminites shall possess Gilead, which formerly belonged to the Manassites; for the Manassites, and likewise the remaining tribes, shall extend their boundaries: for from what he mentions, the reasoning is to be applied to those he does not mention. Moreover,* Rabbi Solomon <note>Rabbi Solomon ben Isaac, known as Rashi (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French commentator.</note> *explains the words:* And they shall possess the <term>Negev</term> <note>Hebrew: הנגב (*ha-negev*), referring to the arid southern region of Israel.</note> the mountain of Esau, *as follows: The Israelites who lived in the southern part of the Israelite land shall possess the mountain of Esau, which is similarly to the south; and the Israelites who were in the plain shall possess the land of the Philistines and the mountain of Ephraim and Samaria; the Benjaminites shall possess Gilead; and the Manassites, to whom Gilead once belonged, shall spread themselves out beyond the borders of the Israelite land toward the east.*

*As for what he further says:* <term>And the captivity of this host...</term> <note>original Hebrew: וגלות החל וגו' (*Ve-galut ha-chel...*). This refers to Obadiah 1:20.</note> *etc., Jonathan* <note>Targum Jonathan, the ancient Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Prophets.</note> *translates it thus:* And this captive people of the children of Israel shall possess the land of the Canaanites as far as <term>Zarpat</term> <note>Hebrew: צרפת (*Tsarfat*). In modern Hebrew, this is the word for France, but in biblical contexts, it refers to Sarepta near Sidon.</note> and the captives of Jerusalem who are in Spain <note>Latin: *Hispania*. The Hebrew text uses the word *Sepharad*, which medieval Jewish tradition identified with the Iberian Peninsula.</note> shall possess the inhabitants of the southern land. *That is, he treated the word* <term>hachèl</term> <note>Hebrew: החל</note>—*which does not have a <term>Yod</term> <note>The tenth letter of the Hebrew alphabet, often acting as a vowel marker.</note> between the letters <term>Chet</term> and <term>Lamed</term>—as having the same meaning as the other word* <term>hachayil</term> <note>Hebrew: החיל</note> *which does have a Yod, so that it signifies the 'host' or 'people' itself. Accordingly, according to his opinion, this text is to be explained thus: that the exiled children shall possess that land which had belonged to the Canaanites as far as Zarpat, which in this way would be that Sarepta which belongs to Sidon.*

*Furthermore, I have already indicated above the opinion of Rabbi Abraham Ibn Ezra, whom Rabbi David Kimchi* <note>Known as Radak (1160–1235), a famous grammarian and commentator.</note> *follows: namely, that the 'Canaanites' are the Germans. Moreover, Ibn Ezra relies on the testimony of certain people who convinced him that Germany is inhabited by Canaanites, but I have already said that this testimony is false. To me, however, it seems that the word* <term>hachèl</term> <note>Hebrew: החל</note> *here comes from the meaning of 'beginning,' as in Deuteronomy 2:24:* Begin <note>original Hebrew: *hachèl*</note> to possess. *The sense comes down to this: After the Prophet had said: 'And they shall possess the mountain of Esau toward the south,' implying that the Israelites would possess it, in the present text he explains which...*

<meta>This page continues the systematic examination of Isaac Abarbenel's commentary on the Book of Obadiah, specifically focusing on the geographical distribution of the Jewish diaspora and the future restoration described in Obadiah 1:19–20.</meta>

->## EXAMINATION OF ABARBENEL<-

For from the tribes individually there are those inhabitants of the south who are to possess the mountain of Esau; and he [Abarbenel] says these will be those who migrated as exiles into the Edomite land—namely, the land of the Christians. <note>In medieval Jewish commentary, "Edom" was almost universally used as a coded reference to Rome and, by extension, the Christian world.</note> For since they endured afflictions and losses there, they will be worthy to possess the primary part and the capital of their land, surely Mount Seir. And this is what he says: *And this host of the initial exile* <note>original: וגלת החלה הזה (*ve-galut ha-chel ha-zeh*), from Obadiah 1:20.</note>—that is, that first captive multitude of the Israelites, who, just as the Canaanites of old went as far as Zarephath (which is France), so too the multitude of exiles from Jerusalem who are in Sepharad (that is, Spain, according to the Targumist), <note>The Targum is an ancient Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible; it famously identifies Sepharad with Spain.</note> all these shall possess the southern cities.

And according to this exposition, the <term>Kaph of similarity</term> <note>The Hebrew letter כ (*kaph*), used as a prefix meaning 'like' or 'as'.</note>—or comparative particle—is missing in the text in the word *Kena’anim* [Canaanites], whose meaning is the same as if it had said *ki-Kena’anim* [as Canaanites]. Nor is this surprising. For thus Rabbi Ibn Ezra also writes that the "Kaph of similarity" is missing in that phrase: *shokhni* ("I dwell in the clefts of the rock") instead of *ke-shokhni* ("as if I were dwelling"). So also in this passage, the sense of the words "who [are] Canaanites" is "and as Canaanites." That is: the Israelites who, departing into the exile of the Second Temple—of which the Prophet says: *And that initial exile*—came into Greece and Italy as far as Zarephath (that is, France), just as the Canaanites once went when they fled, leaving their lands; thus also the multitude of exiles from Jerusalem, who were already in Spain, all of them shall possess the southern cities.

How pleasing, moreover, is what he says: *And the exile of Jerusalem, which was in Sepharad*; for behold, the Jews went into Spain from the destruction of the First Temple, to which place King Pyrrhus of Spain <note>A legendary figure in medieval Jewish history, often conflated with various historical rulers, said to have transported Jewish captives to the Iberian Peninsula.</note> led them and settled them in two districts. One was in Lusitania [Portugal], which at that time was a great city of Andalusia in the kingdom of Castile, and the other in the district of Toledo, as I also wrote toward the end of the Book of Kings. Let it not offend you that he says: *as far as Zarephath* (that is, France), and makes no mention of England; for this island is considered part of France and originally arose from it. Hence they call it in their ancient books the "Island of France," even though it later broke away from France and established its own peculiar kingdom.

And although even today there is no man in France or Spain who is called an "Israelite," <note>This refers to the period after the expulsions from England (1290), France (1306/1394), and Spain (1492), when Jews were officially banned unless they converted.</note> God is not a man that He should lie; for this promise can always be fulfilled in those who departed from there or in their descendants. Or perhaps he even points a finger toward those Israelites who <insert>fell away from their religion</insert> because of the severity of the afflictions and persecutions, <note>A reference to the *Conversos* or "New Christians"—Jews who converted to Christianity to avoid death or expulsion but often maintained Jewish identities in secret.</note> and remained in France and Spain by the thousands and tens of thousands in great gatherings, who will one day, having been converted, seek the Lord their God, as even today from...

<summary>Abarbenel interprets the "exiles" in Obadiah as the Jewish communities of France (Zarephath) and Spain (Sepharad), arguing that despite their current status as converts or exiles, they will eventually reclaim the lands of Edom.</summary>
<keywords>Isaac Abarbenel, Obadiah, Sepharad, Zarephath, Spain, France, Ibn Ezra, Conversos, Lusitania, Toledo, Diaspora</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the fulfillment of Obadiah’s prophecy, specifically addressing the final verse of the book regarding the "Saviors" who will judge Mount Esau, and the historical identification of Edom with Rome.</meta>

### ON OBADIAH.

...some do these things, and in them this prophecy can be fulfilled, and thus also the sixth question is resolved. Furthermore, concerning the words: *And Saviors shall ascend Mount Zion to judge Mount Esau, and the kingdom shall be the Lord's*, they say in *Bereshit Rabbah* <note>original: *Bereshit Rabbah*, a classic collection of ancient Jewish interpretations (Midrash) on the Book of Genesis.</note>: *Until I come to my Lord in Seir (Genesis 33:14).* We have turned through the whole of Sacred Scripture and we have not found that Jacob ever approached Esau at Mount Seir; but this is still to come in the future, whence it is also said: *Saviors shall ascend Mount Zion to judge Mount Esau.* Nor indeed can the seat of the future empire be safe and whole until the posterity of Esau is cut off, as is said in Psalm 9:6–7: *The enemy is consumed by the sword forever; you have overturned their cities, and their memory has perished with them.*

And a little later it is written: *But the Lord remains forever; He has prepared His throne for judgment.* And *Jonathan* <note>Referring to the Targum Jonathan, an ancient Aramaic translation and commentary on the prophetic books.</note>, wherever "Mount Esau" is named in the Prophets, translates it as "the great city of Esau." It is well-known <note>original: ἐξάκουστον (*exakouston*), a Greek term meaning "notorious" or "celebrated."</note> that this is Rome, the Princess of cities, which is in the power of those Edomites <note>In the medieval and early modern period, Jewish scholars frequently identified the biblical nation of Edom with the Roman Empire and, by extension, the Christian world.</note> who are sown from the seed of Esau.

But who are these "Saviors"? The interpreters write that they are the Messiah and the primary men of his time. And the prophet Micah, in chapter 5, verse 5, numbers these as: *Seven shepherds and eight leaders of men*, to whom our sages of blessed memory <note>original: *p. m.*, short for *piae memoriae*.</note> also refer here by name.

*Rabbi Aben Ezra* <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a highly influential Spanish-Jewish scholar and grammarian.</note> writes: *Saviors shall ascend: these are the judges who will be in the Land of Israel, who will judge the survivors on Mount Esau.* This is less correct, for the prophet threatens that no one from the Idumeans [Edomites] will be a survivor. But it seems to me that the prophet, in this final verse, is explaining how the vengeance to be taken upon the Idumeans will be managed. For since the Israelites alone could not defeat them, he says that "Saviors" will ascend Mount Zion; these, however, are the remaining peoples who will come to wage war against the Christians in the Holy Land. And when they liberate the land from those Christian Idumeans and take vengeance upon them in God’s name, he therefore calls them "Saviors." In that respect—because they expel them from the land and destroy them—they can certainly be called saviors on Mount Zion to judge Mount Esau and erase it from the world.

Do you not see how God calls Assyria the "rod of His anger" (Isaiah 10:5), and Nebuchadnezzar "His servant" (Jeremiah 25:9), and Cyrus "His anointed" (Isaiah 45:1)? This is not so much in respect to themselves, but in respect to the works which they performed by His supreme decree to take vengeance on enemies. In this way, those who were more truly "destroyers" in relation to the Idumeans, he here calls savior-

### EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

...saviors on Mount Zion: although, if those very nations are to fall and be uprooted—for the army is the vengeance of the Lord upon all—the destruction and slaughter of Edom will be much more grave and final, indeed so absolute that not one man shall be left of all those who come to that war; truly, the slaughter of the other nations will not be so great. However, the Prophet promises that after that heavy vengeance, the Lord shall be King over all the earth, so that all the inhabitants of the world may acknowledge His kingdom, just as Zechariah also says in chapter 14, verse 9: *The Lord shall be King over all the earth; in that day the Lord shall be one, and His name one.* Thus ends the explanation of the Prophecy and the Book of Obadiah. <note>original: L. ## EXAMINATION.

We advised from the beginning that the sermon of Obadiah is partly Legal <note>Refers to the "Old Law" or themes of judgment and justice.</note> and partly Evangelical <note>Refers to the "Gospel" or themes of salvation and the New Testament.</note>. And since we have explained the Legal parts thus far—and perhaps more extensively than was fitting—we shall now prove, with as much brevity as possible against *Our Man* <note>Referring to Isaac Abarbanel, the Jewish commentator whose views the author is refuting.</note>, that from verse 17 to the very end of his prophecy, *Obadiah* prophesied concerning the Kingdom of Christ and the propagation of the Gospel in the time of the New Testament. Truly, we have undertaken a task all the more difficult because Christian interpreters themselves disagree regarding this final part of the Prophecy. For some, explaining it primarily as the recovery of the *Israeli* land and the physical subjugation of the *Edomites*, contend that it was fulfilled from the return from Babylon until the coming of the Savior through the efforts of *Zerubbabel, Ezra, Nehemiah, and the Maccabees*. Such is the view of *Cyril, Remigius, Hugo of Saint Victor, Hugo Grotius*, and others. Others, distinguishing between the literal and mystical sense, explain everything literally as a physical liberation, and the same things mystically and allegorically as the preaching of the Gospel; thus *Jerome, Theodoret, Rupert, Cornelius à Lapide, Ribera, Tirinus, Menochius, Sa, Drusius, Pareus*, and others. Certain others take some parts literally as physical and some as spiritual liberation, such as *Castro, Junius, Calvin*, and others. Our own authors <note>Likely referring to the Lutheran or Reformed tradition to which the author belongs.</note>, almost to a man, understand everything literally as referring to the kingdom of Christ and the preaching of the Gospel. Concerning this matter, besides the *Blessed Luther*, a most venerable man has especially dealt—

### EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

The learned and magnificent Dr. Calov in his <term>unpublished</term> <note>original: ἀνεκδότοις (anekdotois)</note> notes on Grotius <note>Abraham Calov (1612–1686) was a prominent Lutheran theologian; Hugo Grotius was a famous Dutch jurist and scholar whose biblical commentaries were often seen as too rationalistic by more orthodox writers.</note> speaks thus: likewise among the Church Fathers, St. Augustine in *The City of God, book 18, chapter 31*; among the Roman Catholic writers, almost all including Lyra, Paul of Burgos, Arias Montanus, and Estius; and among the Calvinists, Mercer, Gwalther, the Dutch, and others.

Others, having progressed beyond the times of the revealed Savior, wish it to be understood that this passage deals with a certain universal conversion of the Jews; such is the view of Cocceius and the <term>Chiliasts</term> <note>Chiliasts, or Millenarians, believe in a literal 1,000-year earthly reign of Christ.</note>, who—while they prefer to live with Christ on earth rather than in heaven—dream up I know not what preludes to eternal life here. For these people, we pray for a sounder mind at this time. Or they extend the greater part of the fulfillment to the Last Judgment or the next life, as Oecolampadius does. 

However, the nature of our project does not allow us to reconcile or examine the differing opinions of Christian interpreters. Nevertheless, before we dissect our context, we will briefly defend the opinion of our own theologians against Abarbanel, who refers everything to the "Pseudo-Messiah" of his own time. First, Abarbanel relies on the most false and frequently refuted hypothesis that Christians are <term>Idumaeans</term> <note>Idumaeans are Edomites. In medieval Jewish polemic, "Edom" became a common code word for the Roman Empire and, by extension, the Christian Church.</note>: once this is overturned, all the Rabbi's delirious dreams collapse. Second, he likewise presupposes that the Messiah promised to the Patriarchs has not yet come; however, a great many authors cited by Hoornbeeck in his *Prolegomena* have <term>incontrovertibly</term> <note>original: ἀναντιῤῥήτως (anantirrhētōs)</note> proved this to be false. Therefore, once this foundation is removed, everything else must spontaneously fall with it. Third, if that opinion is adopted—that these things are to be fulfilled physically in the future—very many <term>difficulties</term> <note>original: ἄπορα (apora)</note> and even manifest <term>absurdities</term> <note>original: ἄτοπα (atopa)</note> will arise, which we shall demonstrate using the words of Blessed Luther rather than our own:

> *And the house of Jacob shall be a fire. Everything is absurd and conflicts with history if we understand a physical subjection. For what is less fitting than that the house of Jacob should be a fire and the house of Joseph a flame—which itself had already been reduced as if to ashes—and the house of Esau (that is, of Idumaea) should be stubble to be devoured by the flame? For the house of Joseph never returned after the Babylonian captivity, nor did the house of Judah ever possess the Idumaeans. Finally, this is the most absurd of all: that although he previously prophesied that all the Idumaeans would perish and be reduced to nothing, here he finally says they are also to be devoured by fire. And they shall possess those toward the South. There is a wonderful contradiction here if you understand the letter and some external kingdom. First he says, "They shall possess," then, "They shall be possessed." Likewise, they shall possess the plains of the Philistines. Furthermore, what is greatest of all: the kingdom of Israel was divided into twelve tribes, and the law of Moses carefully provided that the tribes should not be mixed among themselves, as can be seen in Moses. When, therefore, he clearly says here "those of the south," that is, those who are toward the*

...the south, that they would possess the mountain of *Esau*; likewise that *Benjamin* would possess *Gilead*—contrary to the way the land was divided by divine command through *Moses* and *Joshua*, etc. *Ephraim* [is also mentioned]. All the Prophets ordinarily fix their feet in the times of the *Messiah*; hence that *Talmudic* <note>The Talmud is a central text of Rabbinic Judaism containing legal and philosophical discussions</note> saying: <note>original Hebrew: כל הנביאים לא נתנבאו אלא לימות משיח (Kol ha-nevi'im lo nitnabu ela l'ymot Mashiach)</note> *No prophet prophesied except for the sake of the days of the Messiah.*

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Jewish commentator Isaac Abarbanel, moving from general arguments about the spiritual nature of prophecy to a verse-by-verse linguistic analysis of the Book of Obadiah, focusing on the "Day of the Lord" and the transition from divine judgment to Gospel promises.</meta>

# EXAMINATION OF ABARBANEL

...must not say so much what we think, but whom we follow. Thus the Prophet continues: Do not, O Edom, act as you are currently acting. For, behold, **IT IS NEAR** (Our opponent <note>Referring to Abarbanel</note> foolishly explains the word <term>קרוב</term> <note>Hebrew: "karov," meaning near</note>—which always implies proximity of either place or time—as referring to possibility and ease; even more foolishly, he derives its meaning from the related word <term>קרב</term> <note>Hebrew: "karav," which can mean to approach or to do battle</note>. For who does not know that words related in their material roots often acknowledge a different formal meaning? For example, from the root <term>דבר</term> <note>D-V-R</note> one identifies a "word," a "plague," a "sanctuary," a "desert," a "bee," and "buttocks." To confuse these meanings according to one’s whim would be more than childish—it would be ridiculous) **THE DAY**, that is, the time of punishment (as mentioned above) **OF THE LORD** (Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius, a Flemish Protestant Old Testament scholar</note> judges that just as "a human day" is used to mean human judgment, so this can be explained as the judgment of the Lord) **UPON ALL NATIONS**, specifically those which had then afflicted the Jews or were currently afflicting them, namely the Ammonites, Moabites, Idumaeans, and Babylonians, as seen in *Jeremiah 45–50*. **AS YOU HAVE DONE**, O Edom, **SO SHALL IT BE DONE TO YOU** according to the law of retaliation <note>Original Greek: κατ’ ἀντιπεπονθός (kat’ antipēponthos), meaning "quid pro quo" or reciprocal justice</note>, for you also shall be invaded by that same Nebuchadnezzar. **YOUR RETRIBUTION**, which you gave to others, **SHALL RETURN UPON YOUR OWN HEAD** (a phrase which occurs in *Psalm 7:17, Psalm 10:6, Judges 9:16, Psalm 137:8*, and elsewhere; it denotes a great and conspicuous punishment joined with ignominy, like when a stone falls back onto the head of the person who threw it, to the laughter of bystanders and the shame of the thrower). For **BECAUSE YOU HAVE DRUNK** the cup of my wrath (*Jeremiah 25:15, 18, 29; 49:12*) and the measure of tears (*Psalm 80:6, compare also Matthew 20:22*, etc.) **YOU** who dwell **UPON MY HOLY MOUNTAIN**. (This is an apostrophe <note>A rhetorical speech directed to a person or group</note> addressed to the Jews, and our author does not disagree; others, such as Jerome, Theodoret, Castrus, Tiringo, and Oecolampadius, want the speech to be directed to the Idumaeans, so that the sense is: "Just as you Idumaeans drank and feasted because my holy mountain was desolated, so all nations shall drink the cup of wrath." But since the word <term>שתה</term> <note>Hebrew: "shatah," to drink</note> does not carry the same sense on both sides in that reading, we prefer the former explanation, compare *Jeremiah 25:15 and 49:12*). **SO SHALL ALL** those **NATIONS DRINK CONTINUALLY**, but not in the same way as you; rather, they **SHALL DRINK AND SHALL SWALLOW DOWN** the dregs (*Psalm 75:9*). Thus far the legal matters; now follow the Gospel promises. Nevertheless, **ON MOUNT ZION THERE SHALL BE ESCAPE**, and true liberation is to be hoped for, for out of Zion (that is, Jerusalem) such preaching shall go forth which, when believed, delivers from death, sin, and hell; and this is to come first from Zion, just as Isaiah says in *chapter 2:3*, Joel in *2:32*, and *Psalm 110:2*. So says the Blessed Luther. **AND HOLINESS** shall be acquired in Jerusalem through the Messiah, the Holy of Holies (as noted by the Blessed Winckelmann <note>Johannes Winckelmann, a 17th-century Lutheran theologian</note>), and imputed to us. <meta>This page continues the verse-by-verse commentary on the Book of Obadiah, focusing on the restoration of the "House of Jacob" and the "House of Joseph" and the spiritual destruction or conversion of Edom (the "House of Esau"). It transitions from the judgment of the nations to the specific inheritance of the faithful.</meta>

### ON OBADIAH

Salomon Gesner, page 89: **AND THEY SHALL POSSESS** by hereditary right, as heirs of God and co-heirs of Christ <note>References: Romans 8:16; Galatians 3:26, 29, 4:7, 31</note>. **THE HOUSE OF JACOB**: the spiritual descendants of Jacob <note>References: Romans 9:7-8, etc.; 11:25-26; Galatians 4:26, 28, 31</note>. **THEIR POSSESSIONS**: Johannes Cocceius <note>A 17th-century Dutch theologian</note> well says here: *“The faithful of all tribes, walking in the footsteps of Jacob, shall each obtain some inheritance among the nations—namely, by God opening the door of preaching. And this shall happen as compensation for the lost earthly inheritance.”* <note>Compare Psalm 28; Isaiah 14:2; Deuteronomy 33:7, 13, 14, 16, 17</note>

Our author translates the word <term>מורשיהם</term> <note>Hebrew: *morasheihem*, "their possessions" or "those who dispossessed them"</note> poorly as *“their possessions,”* meaning those of the other nations, even though it is better explained reflexively, in regard to the name immediately preceding. The Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> translates it as: *“those who had inherited them.”* The Vulgate <note>The standard Latin Bible</note> reads: *“those who had possessed them.”* So also the Syriac version and Blessed Luther: *“his possessors,”* a sense that is quite convenient, unless the word <term>מורש</term> <note>Hebrew: *morash*</note> should rather signify something abstract <note>Compare Isaiah 14; Psalm 17</note>. **AND THE HOUSE OF JACOB**—the tribes of Judah and Benjamin—**SHALL BE** like **A FIRE, AND THE HOUSE OF JOSEPH**—the ten remaining tribes, which otherwise appear under the name "Israel" in the Prophets—**SHALL BE** like **A FLAME, BUT THE HOUSE OF ESAU** shall be to them like **STUBBLE** <note>Judges 9:15, 20; Isaiah 29:6</note>. **AND** those, **KINDLING** against the Edomites, **SHALL DEVOUR THEM**; that is to say, the disciples of Christ arising from the Jews, by the preaching of the word—which is called a flame and fire by metaphor <note>Psalm 119:140; Jeremiah 5:14, 23:19; Luke 12:49</note>—*“shall slay the Edomites and all the impious because of the unbelief and ingratitude with which they reject the Gospel of Christ,”* as Winkelmann says. Or, they shall devour them in such a way that they incorporate them into the kingdom of Christ spiritually. *“For that Arabia Petraea, in which the Edomites lived, was a notable place of holy men once the Gospel was revealed, which Isaiah also promises,”* says Luther. **AND NO ONE SHALL BE A SURVIVOR** (The Septuagint uses the term <term>πυρφόρος</term> <note>Greek: *pyrophoros*, meaning "fire-bearer"</note>, that is, a torch-bearer who, according to Greek custom, carried a flame before the army and was regarded like an ambassador; by this, they intended to suggest that everyone would be so utterly cut off that not even a messenger would survive the slaughter. <note>Compare the commentaries of Cornelius à Lapide, Caspar de Castro, Francisco de Ribera, Jacobus Tirinus, and Johannes Drusius on this passage</note>) **FOR THE HOUSE OF ESAU**; for that fire shall consume the impious, but in the godly it shall consume whatever remains of the "Edomite" disposition. Thus the Church shall be superior to all its enemies, and nothing shall be able to prevail against the kingdom of Christ, as Luther notes. *“And this shall not happen by human power, but because THE LORD HAS SPOKEN, whose command is as good as done,”* according to Gesner. **AND THEY SHALL POSSESS THE SOUTH** (that is, the <term>הנגב</term> <note>Hebrew: *Ha-Negev*, the Southland</note>; namely the Southerners, the tribe of Judah being further south than the others, specifically the more southern part of it <note>Joshua 15:21</note>). (Others, such as Our Author, Kimchi, Tarnovius, Calvin, Gvaltherus, etc., take the subject from the preceding
