<meta>This page continues the philological and theological examination of what it means to "walk with God," moving from a refutation of monastic interpretations to a study of the Hebrew grammar and classical commentaries.</meta>

...to be transferred to a person’s character. As Augustine says in his commentary on Psalm 103, Volume 8, column 1214: "Every man, in his own actions, has his feet in that which he does." <note>original Latin: "Omnis homo... in actu suo pedes habet in eo, quod agit." This means that one's "walk" or movement is defined by their moral conduct.</note> On this subject, one should consult Glassius’s *Sacred Rhetoric*, Treatise 1, chapter 12, page 1316. <note>Salomon Glassius (1593–1656) was a German divine and biblical critic.</note> The same is observed by Maimonides—who was the first among the Rabbis to cease speaking nonsense—in *The Guide for the Perplexed*, Part 1, chapter 24, folio 17, 2. <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204) was a preeminent Sephardic Jewish philosopher. His *More Nevochim* (Guide for the Perplexed) sought to harmonize Aristotelian philosophy with Hebrew Bible theology.</note> There, he shows the various meanings of the word *halakh* <note>the Hebrew verb for "to walk"</note>:

<note>original Hebrew: ושאל לשון הלך להנהגה במנהגים החשובים מבלי התנועה הגופנית כל אמר הלכת בדרכיו אחר יי׳ תלכו לכו ונלכה באור יי׳</note>

That is: The verb *halakh* (to walk) is adapted to refer to morals and customs which are carried out without any movement of the body, as in these passages: *And you shall walk in His ways* (Deuteronomy 28:9); *After the LORD your God shall you walk* (Deuteronomy 13:4); and *Let us walk in the light of the LORD* (Isaiah 2:5). On these passages, his commentators Shem Tov and Efodi <note>Jewish scholars who wrote standard commentaries on Maimonides' *Guide*.</note> remind us that although man is a physical being, God—toward whom man is said to tend—is entirely incorporeal; therefore, that "walking" is said to be incorporeal. However, such a technical distinction was not strictly necessary. Maimonides implies that such walking is performed primarily by the soul, not the body.

§. 6. Furthermore, because the verb is used in this passage in the <term>Hitpael</term> <note>A Hebrew verb conjugation that often expresses reflexive, intensive, or frequentative action—doing something to oneself or doing it repeatedly.</note> form, *hithallekh* (התהלך), it is not without its own emphasis. Junius <note>Francis Junius (1545–1602), a Huguenot theologian.</note> rightly judged its meaning to be frequentative (meaning "to go repeatedly" or "to walk continually"). One should also note what Rivet says in *Exercise 49*, page 241. <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot theologian.</note> Rabbi David Kimchi <note>A famous medieval Jewish grammarian and commentator (c. 1160–1235).</note> correctly says that this conjugation implies intensity, multiplication, duration, and sometimes the frequency of an action. Regarding our word *hithallekh*, the matter seems well-known; compare Job 1:7 and 2:2; 1 Samuel 30:31; Esther 2:11; Zechariah 1:10; 2 Chronicles 21:4, etc.

§. 7. Since, therefore, *hithallekh* is accustomed to being transferred to both morals and duties, the entire phrase can denote either a holy life or a sacred office, depending on the context. Forsterus, in his *Hebrew Lexicon*, page 194, chooses only the latter; most others choose the former. <note>Johann Forster (1496–1556), a Lutheran theologian and Hebraist.</note> However, because they do not translate or explain the phrase in the same way due to the little word *et* (את) <note>A Hebrew particle usually marking the direct object, but sometimes used as a preposition meaning "with."</note>, it is worthwhile to examine it. Thus, the Blessed Luther, in his discussion of the last words of David, takes it as a marker of the fourth case (the accusative): "He walked God," that is, he followed and imitated Him. It is like when we say: "He lives [like] Sardanapalus," referring to those who live a life of revelry. <note>Sardanapalus was a legendary Assyrian king, often used in literature as a symbol of extreme luxury and decadence. Luther's point is that the grammar suggests Enoch "lived out" God's character.</note> Similarly, the Blessed Solomon Gesner, on this passage, page 142, retains the reflexive meaning—as is familiar for the <term>Hitpael</term>—and explains it: "Enoch made himself walk," that is, to follow, pursue, and imitate *et ha-Elohim* (the LORD Himself). Just as in Isaiah 33:15, a pious man is said to "walk righteousnesses." It is as if the Lord...

<summary>This page analyzes the Hebrew phrase "walking with God" through the lens of grammar and historical commentary, arguing it refers to a continuous moral and spiritual imitation of God rather than a physical movement or monastic retreat.</summary>
<keywords>Enoch, Maimonides, Hitpael, walking with God, Solomon Glassius, David Kimchi, Martin Luther, Biblical Hebrew grammar, metaphors of conduct, Sardanapalus</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the Hebrew verb for "to walk" (halakh) and the particle "with" (eth), exploring how "walking with God" signifies companionship, obedience, and living in the divine presence.</meta>

...expressed by His word, He had made for Himself a path and a journey, from which He would depart neither to the right nor to the left. We certainly do not deny that it can be translated this way; however, since the verb *hithhallekh* <note>original Hebrew: התהלך; the intensive form of the verb "to walk," often used to describe a habitual way of life</note> is otherwise taken intransitively, we think it simpler that the particle *eth* <note>original Hebrew: את; a preposition that can mean "with" or act as a marker for a direct object</note> be expressed by the particle *with*. (Compare Malachi 2:6 and Micah 6:8).

### §. 8.
Furthermore, the particle *with* (*eth*), when used in this grammatical construction, introduces various modes of meaning to the phrase. First, it implies **companionship**. A person is said to walk with another when, agreeing upon a specific path, he joins himself in a friendly way as a *traveling companion* <note>original Greek: συνοδίτης (synodites)</note>. The phrase is then transferred to **familiar and friendly conversation** <note>In this context, "conversation" refers to one's general conduct or way of living with others</note>, as in 1 Samuel 25:13: "In all the time of our *walking with them*" <note>original Hebrew: אתנו התהלכנו (itnu hithhallakhnu)</note>, that is, of our fellowship. So in Psalm 35:14: "*I walked as if for a brother*" <note>original Hebrew: כאת לי התהלכתי (ke-ach li hithhallakhti)</note>, that is, I behaved with such familiarity. Thus, one is said to walk with God who is in good agreement with God; the metaphor is drawn from two friends who agree well with one another and gladly set out on a journey together, being of one mind and heart. As it is said in Amos 3:3: *Can two walk together, unless they be agreed?* Thus say Rivetus in his 49th Exercise on Genesis, and Glassius in his *Mosaic Christology*, page 183. <note>André Rivet (1572–1651) and Salomon Glassius (1593–1656) were influential Protestant theologians and philologists.</note> To this you may fittingly refer the passage in Malachi 2:6: *In peace and uprightness* "he walked with me" <note>original Hebrew: הלך אתי (halakh itti)</note>. On the contrary, the wicked are said "to walk contrary to me" <note>original Hebrew: לכת עמי בקרי (lekhet immi be-qeri)</note> in Leviticus 26:24, 40. 

Next, *hithhallekh* followed by *eth* imports **following**. Thus, "to walk with God" is to follow God as a Leader or *guide* <note>original Greek: ὁδηγὸν (hodēgon)</note>; that is, to cling to Him, to separate oneself from others, to do all things solely according to His prescription, and "to keep His word constantly before one's eyes as the rule of life," as the Blessed Gerhard says in his commentary on Genesis, page 162. <note>Johann Gerhard (1582–1637) was a key figure in Lutheran Orthodoxy.</note> In this sense, there is another very well-known phrase: to walk *after God* <note>original Hebrew: אחרי אלהים (acharei Elohim)</note>, that is, to follow Him. 

Furthermore, the little word *eth* (*with*) sometimes implies a **reverence for His presence**. Hence, for others, "to walk with God" is to walk in His sight; that is, to recognize and revere God's presence in all one's actions, just as a son reveres the presence of a father or a servant the presence of a master. This is expressed elsewhere as: "to walk before the Lord" <note>original Hebrew: לפני יהוה (lifnei Yehovah)</note>. Here you may quite aptly refer the passage in Micah 6:8: "*to walk modestly or reverently with your God*" <note>original Hebrew: הצנע לכת עם אלהיך (hatzne'a lekhet im Eloheikha)</note>. 

Finally, Glassius, in his *Sacred Philology*, page 785, says that *eth* in our phrase suggests **approval**: He walked with God, that is, "he so ordered his life that he might prove himself to God through true and living faith." (Hebrews 11:5).

<summary>This page analyzes the Hebrew phrase "to walk with God," breaking it down into four theological meanings: friendly companionship, faithful following of God's commands, living reverently in the divine presence, and acting in a way that earns God's approval.</summary>
<keywords>walking with God, Hebrew philology, companionship, obedience, divine presence, Salomon Glassius, Johann Gerhard, André Rivet, biblical metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the Hebrew verb for "to walk" (halakh) and the particle "with" (eth), exploring how "walking with God" signifies companionship, obedience, and living in the divine presence.</meta>

### §. 9.
All these opinions harmoniously agree on this point: that **walking with God** expresses a holiness of life. However, if one were to argue for a single interpretation, it is not easy to accept one in a way that excludes the others. For Enoch so ordered his life that he loved God as a friend, followed Him as a guide, and revered Him as a Father and Lord. In figurative expressions, an analogy can be drawn from several different concepts at once. 

Nevertheless, without wishing to prejudice anyone else's view, we believe that the analogy of "following a leader" carries the most weight in the histories of Enoch and Noah. While many people in their day were turning away toward the ways of the Cainites <note>The descendants of Cain, traditionally associated with a worldly and godless lifestyle</note>, they followed God as their guide <note>original Greek: ὁδηγὸν (hodegon)</note> and leader with constant devotion, pressing forward along the path of piety. This is perhaps why in the history of Noah there is an explicit mention of his "contemporaries" <note>original Greek: συγχρόνων (synchronon)</note> who went astray: Genesis 6:9, "*in his generations*" <note>original Hebrew: ודֹרוֹ (be-dorothav)</note>, and chapter 7:1, "*in this generation*" <note>original Hebrew: בדור הזה (ba-dor hazzeh)</note>.

Furthermore, that our phrase suggests piety and holiness of life is evident not only from what has been said, but can be further confirmed by other passages where similar phrases are placed alongside it, almost by way of explanation <note>original Greek: ἐπεξηγητικῶς (epexēgētikōs)</note>. These are formulas that signify or result in piety, such as in Genesis 6:9, Micah 6:8, and Malachi 2:6. We also see this in related phrases, such as: 

*   **Walking after God** <note>original Hebrew: אחרי אלהים (acharei Elohim)</note> — Deuteronomy 8:19, 11:28, 13:4, 26:14, 1 Kings 14:8, etc.
*   **Walking before** or **in the sight of the Lord** <note>original Hebrew: לפני יהוה (lifnei Yehovah)</note> — Genesis 17:1, 24:40, 48:15, 1 Kings 2:4, 3:6, 8:23, 2 Chronicles 6:16, etc.
*   **Walking in the ways of the Lord** <note>original Hebrew: בדרכי יהוה (be-darkhei Yehovah)</note> — Deuteronomy 8:6, 10:12, 11:22, 19:9, 26:17, 28:9, 30:16, Joshua 22:5, 1 Kings 2:3, 8:58, 11:38, Psalm 81:13, Psalm 119:3, Psalm 128:1. 
*   **In a good way** — 1 Kings 8:36, Jeremiah 6:16. 
*   **In the path of justice** — Proverbs 8:20.
*   **Walking in the law of God** — 2 Kings 10:31, Nehemiah 10:30, Psalm 119:1, Jeremiah 26:4, 32:23, 44:10, etc.
*   **In the fear of God** — Nehemiah 5:9, etc.
*   **In the commandments of God** — 2 Chronicles 17:4, etc.
*   **In the statutes of God** — Jeremiah 20:13, Ezekiel 5:6, 11:12, 18:9, etc.
*   **In truth** — 2 Kings 20:3, Isaiah 38:3.
*   **In the truth of God** — Psalm 26:3, Psalm 86:11.
*   **In the name of God** — Micah 4:5, Zechariah 10:12.
*   **In integrity** — Proverbs 10:9, 19:1, 20:7, Psalm 26:1, Psalm 84:11, etc.
*   **Walking in righteousness** — Isaiah 33:15.
*   And from the New Testament: **Walking in good works** — Ephesians 2:10, etc.

To this heap of evidence, we may add the testimony of the ancient translators. Onkelos <note>The author of a famous 2nd-century Aramaic translation of the Torah</note> translates it as: "And Enoch walked in the fear of the Lord" <note>original Aramaic: ומהלך חנוך בדחלתא דיי (wa-mehalekh Henokh bi-dekhaltha de-Adonai)</note>. The Jerusalem and Pseudo-Jonathan Targums <note>Ancient Aramaic paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible</note> say: "And Enoch served in truth before the Lord" <note>original Aramaic: והוה חנוך בקשוטא קדם יי (wa-havah Henokh be-qushta qodam Adonai)</note>. The Arabic translation by Saadia <note>Saadia Gaon, a 10th-century scholar and translator</note> and the Mauritanian version is: "Enoch walked in obedience to God" <note>original Arabic: באעתא אללה (bi-ta'at Allah)</note>.

<summary>This page analyzes the phrase "walking with God" by comparing it to various biblical idioms for piety and checking it against ancient Aramaic and Arabic translations. The author concludes that for Enoch and Noah, it specifically meant following God's guidance while others went astray.</summary>
<keywords>Enoch, Noah, Cainites, Onkelos, Pseudo-Jonathan, Saadia Gaon, walking with God, piety, holiness, biblical idioms</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of "walking with God," transitioning from the definition of the phrase to a defense of Martin Luther’s translation and a discussion on why the phrase is repeated in the biblical text.</meta>

...in obedience to **GOD**. The *Septuagint* <note>The earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> translators rendered it: "Enoch pleased God" <note>original Greek: εὐηρέστησεν Ἐνὼχ τῷ Θεῷ</note>. So too the Syriac <note>The Peshitta, an ancient Syriac translation</note>: "and he was pleasing to God" <note>original Syriac: וְשַׁפַּר לַאֲלָהָא</note>, he pleased **GOD**. The phrase implies this not formally—as Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a Dutch legal scholar and theologian</note> thought—but as a consequence. Compare *Hebrews 11:5*, *Sirach 44:16*, and *Wisdom 4:10*. Furthermore, Solomon ben Melech <note>A 16th-century Jewish grammarian</note> among the Rabbis explains the phrase well: *He placed all his desire in the love of GOD* <note>original Hebrew: שם כל חשקו באהבת השם</note>. *Among the Arabs also, al-Kisa'i in his "History of the Prophets," as cited by Hottinger in "Oriental History," page 23: He served GOD nights and days, nor did he cease from His praise.* There is no need to heap up other testimonies or versions here. No one has expressed the phrase more significantly than the Blessed Luther: *Because he led a godly life* <note>original German: Weil er ein göttlich Leben führet</note>. 

While today we see the translation of this Divine Man being picked apart and slandered everywhere by the half-learned, it is rightly called "crafty" <note>original Greek: δαιδαλήτης, referring to someone who over-complicates or manipulates</note>. After Columbus, everyone could compete to discover new worlds; after Luther, any schoolmaster from the street corner can translate the Scripture—nay, pervert it. Even a ridiculous mouse insults a dead lion. Among us, his golden translation is held in such high esteem that we grant it first place among all others. He alone both wished and was able to express idioms with idioms (to render word for word is the work of a mediocre mind); and so here too, that saying of W. Severus fits him:

->*Of the race of Japheth <note>Traditional ancestor of Europeans</note>, none was earlier or better than Luther,*<-
->*Nor, I believe, will there ever be.*<-

Otherwise, one can judge from what has been said what those "Apellas" <note>A mocking reference to overly-critical or "superstitious" scholars, derived from the Roman poet Horace</note> deserve, who with wicked loose-tonguedness <note>original Greek: ἀθυροστομίᾳ</note> have dared to represent Enoch as a hypocrite and almost as someone utterly impious. Truly, for their slander <note>original Greek: Διαβολήν</note> directed against an irreproachable <note>original Greek: ἀδιαβλήτῷ</note> man, let them experience the Devil's whip.

### §. 10.
Perhaps we have lingered too long on these three words: but it remains to be considered why the same phrase is placed twice in such a short history of Enoch, in the exact same words in verses 22 and 24, which might lead someone to think it a tautology <note>original Greek: ταυτολογίαν, a redundant repetition</note>. To resolve this, what Mercerus <note>Jean Mercier, a French Hebraist</note> says is not sufficient: *It is a repetition, as if he were saying: "Enoch walked, I say, with GOD."* Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan, a Catholic cardinal and commentator</note> wants it to be suggested by the repetition that he was holy and dear to **GOD** through his whole life, from childhood onward. We readily admit the opinion of our great Calovius <note>Abraham Calovius, a leading 17th-century Lutheran theologian</note>, who in his *Commentary on Genesis* in the place cited, refers the second instance to the holiness of his life, since it is linked with the *translation of Enoch* <note>Enoch being taken up to heaven without dying</note> and must explain how he conducted himself—he who was favored by **GOD** with this grace above others: not that he deserved translation by the holiness of his life,

<summary>This page explores various ancient translations of the phrase "walking with God" and offers a passionate defense of Martin Luther's German translation against contemporary critics. It also begins a theological discussion on why the phrase is repeated in the Genesis account of Enoch.</summary>
<keywords>Enoch, Martin Luther, Septuagint, Hugo Grotius, Solomon ben Melech, Abraham Calovius, translation of Enoch, biblical philology, Lutheranism</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Enoch’s character, shifting from the general definition of his "walk with God" to his specific roles as a prophet and priest. It also begins to examine Enoch’s historical reputation as a pioneer of the sciences, specifically astronomy and writing.</meta>

...but because he was pleasing to **GOD** through faith, about which we read in *Hebrews 11:5*. First, however, we should consider the profession and office of Enoch, which he held for three hundred years. For this reason, he is called a Prophet in *Jude 14*. He also seems to have performed the duties of a Priest, an office which in ancient times belonged to the firstborn. As we have already noted regarding the phrase "To Walk," it can refer both to one's actions and to one's official function. Therefore, "Walking with **GOD**" can be explained both as a holy and divine life and as a holy and divine office. 

The latter interpretation seems to prevail in *Malachi 2:6*: "The law of truth was in his mouth" (referring to Levi, etc.). "In peace and rectitude *he walked with me*" <note>original Hebrew: אתי הלך, *it-ti ha-lakh*</note> (these are the words of **GOD**) "and he turned many away from sin. Compare verse 7." Likewise, the equivalent phrase "To walk *before the Lord*" <note>original Hebrew: לפני יהוה, *lif-nei Yah-weh*</note> denotes both holiness of life, as we proved above, and a sacred office, as in *1 Samuel 2:30, 35*. In this holy office, Enoch sternly rebuked the impiety of life and profanity of speech of his time (*Jude 14, 15*), and for this reason, he exposed himself to the hatred of the Cainites <note>The descendants of Cain, often depicted as the wicked inhabitants of the pre-flood world</note> and undoubtedly experienced their persecutions. 

As for what Cedrenus <note>George Cedrenus, an 11th-century Byzantine historian</note> reports—that two of Enoch's brothers were violently slaughtered by Lamech, and that Enoch, deeply shaken by this domestic calamity, prayed to God that he might no longer witness such carnage and had his request granted—this may be regarded as a pious reflection, rather than being sold as certain history.

### §. II. 

But now at last we depart from this topic, lest we offend the reader with our wordiness. In other matters, we shall be more brief. We have said that Enoch was approved and was a priest. Some people are also concerned with his other studies. They say he was a particularly diligent student of Mathematics <note>term: Mathesis; in the early modern period, this term encompassed geometry, arithmetic, and especially astronomy</note>. *Eusebius*, in Book 7 of his *Ecclesiastical History*, says: "That the month among the Hebrews occurs around the equinox is shown also by the studies found in Enoch" <note>original Greek: τὸ δὲ τὸν παρ᾽ Ἑβραίοις μῆνα περὶ ἰσημερίαν εἶναι, παραστατικὰ καὶ τὰ ἐν τῷ Ἑνὼχ μαθήματα</note>. 

And in Book 9 of his *Preparation for the Gospel*, citing Eupolemus <note>A 2nd-century BCE Jewish historian who wrote in Greek</note> regarding the Jews, he says that when Abraham was teaching Astrology <note>term: Astrologia; at this time, the word described the scientific study of the stars (astronomy) as well as their influence on human affairs</note> and other sciences in Heliopolis, he asserted that the Babylonians traced their discovery back to Enoch, and that Enoch was the first to discover astrology. And later: he says the Greeks claim Atlas discovered astrology, but that Atlas and Enoch are the same person (a matter we have already discussed above); and that a son was born to Enoch named Methuselah, who learned all things through the angels of God, and thus we came to know them. 

Similarly, the author of the *Book of Juchasin* <note>Sefer Ha-Yuchasin, a 15th-century Jewish historical work by Abraham Zacuto</note> on page 134 writes: "*Enoch, who is called Edris, began to compose books on astronomy*" <note>original Hebrew: אשר נקרא אדריס והתחיל לחבר ספרים בתכונה. Edris/Idris is the name given to Enoch in the Quran</note>. Furthermore, Al-Baydawi, the commentator on the Quran, according to the testimony of Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss scholar of Oriental languages</note>, says: "He is also reported to have been the first scribe <note>term: Calamographus; literally one who writes with a reed pen, from the Greek *kalamos*</note> and astronomer."

<summary>This page discusses Enoch’s dual identity as a holy priest who rebuked the wicked and a founding figure of the sciences. The author cites various Jewish, Christian, and Islamic traditions that credit Enoch (also known as Idris or Atlas) with the invention of writing and astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>Enoch, Idris, priesthood, prophecy, astronomy, astrology, scribe, Abraham, Methuselah, Eusebius, Eupolemus, Cainites</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Enoch’s reputation in historical and rabbinic tradition. Having previously established his role as a prophet and priest, the author now addresses Enoch’s traditional association with the origins of arithmetic, astronomy, and the art of writing, while maintaining a skeptical stance toward specific "Enochian" alphabets. The page concludes by transitioning from Enoch's public and intellectual life to his domestic life and genealogy.</meta>

...and a student of **Arithmetic**. The Jews primarily attribute the care of the *intercalation* <note>original: *embolismus*; the practice of adding a leap month to the calendar to align the lunar year with the solar year</note> to him, as is clear from the words of Rabbi Eliezer cited above. From this they derive great mysteries, which I do not have the time to pursue here. For more on the *Secret of Intercalation* <note>original Hebrew: סוד העבור, *Sod Ha-Ibur*</note>, as well as the general system of the year among the Hebrews, one may consult Maimonides <note>A preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher and jurist</note> in his *Laws of the Sanctification of the New Moon* <note>original Hebrew: הלכות קדוש חדש, *Hilchot Kiddush Ha-Chodesh*</note> (chapter 4); Rabbi Mordechai Jaffe in *The Royal Wardrobe* <note>original: *Levusch Malcut*; a famous code of Jewish law</note> (Part I, nos. 427, 428); Sebastian Münster’s *Hebrew Calendar*; Joseph Scaliger’s *On the Emendation of Time* (Book 7); Johannes Cocceius on the first chapter of the *Sanhedrin* (p. 6 and following); John Selden’s *On the Civil Year and Calendar of the Ancient Church and Jewish Commonwealth*; Jacob Christmann’s *Palestine Calendar*; Rabbi Ori; Nicolaus Müller’s *On the Jewish Luni-Solar Year*; Dr. Aegidius Strauch’s *Disputation on the Rabbinic Talmudic Computation*, and others.

Nor do some make Enoch merely an astronomer; they also identify him as the first grammarian. George Cedrenus <note>An 11th-century Byzantine historian</note> says of him expressly: "This man was the first to learn and teach letters, and was deemed worthy of the revelation of divine mysteries" <note>original Greek: οὗτος πρῶτος γράμματα μανθάνει καὶ διδάσκει καὶ θείων μυστηρίων ἀποκαλύψεως ἀξιοῦται</note>. Indeed, there are even those who depict "Enochian characters" as if they were well-known and understood. The first to make these public, as far as is known, was Giovanni Agostino Pantheo, a Venetian priest, in a book to which he gave the hideous title *Voarchadumia* <note>A 1530 work on alchemy and "theophanic" alphabets</note> (p. 15), from which the celebrated Thomas Bang described the same in his *The Celestial Orient* (Exercise 2, p. 104). Claude Duret, a Frenchman, critiqued Pantheo in his *Treasury of Languages* (p. 127), but James Bonaventure Hepburn, a Scotsman, presented a different "Alphabet of Enoch" in his work which he calls *The Golden Rod* <note>original: *Aurea Virga*</note>.

But why do we linger on such uncertainties? For from what source, I ask, is it established that Enoch discovered astronomy or intercalation? Yet we admit that he cultivated the sciences. For the Patriarchs surely did not look upon the *entire universe* <note>original Greek: τὸ πᾶν, *to pan*</note> for so long without using their minds. But from where did others learn his letters? It is hardly even certain whether he used any at all. The Antediluvian fathers, on account of the *long life* <note>original Greek: μακροβιότητα, *makrobiotēta*</note> granted by **GOD**, were able to faithfully pass down "face to face" <note>original Greek: στόμα πρὸς στόμα, *stoma pros stoma*, literally "mouth to mouth"</note> even to their late-born descendants those things which we propagate through writing. 

Nor, however, is it any less false what Juan Nieremberg asserts in his *Origins of Sacred Scripture* (Book 1, chapter 10): that "literary and the art of writing were first invented by **MOSES**, and neither books nor letters existed before him, but only hieroglyphic figures were in use." Since the celebrated Dr. Matthias Wasmuth has answered him in his *Vindication of Hebrew* (Part 1, p. 87), and Bang has shown the rashness of Pantheo, Duret, and Hepburn in his 11th Exercise, we proceed securely to other matters.

§. 12. Enoch did not lead a celibate life, but became both a husband and a father of children (*verse 22*). Their firstborn (*verse 21*) is called

<meta>This page continues the discussion of biblical figures and their lifespans, specifically focusing on Methuselah’s record-breaking age and the theological debates surrounding how it compares to Adam’s lifespan. It then pivots to correct historical errors regarding Enoch’s character and domestic life.</meta>

מתושלח <note>Hebrew: *Methuselah*</note> *Methuselah*, is memorable for this above all else: that of all mortals, he was the most long-lived. For he completed 969 years. Indeed, there is little to be gained from what is mentioned here by Pererius <note>Benedictus Pererius (1535–1610), a Jesuit theologian known for his commentaries on Genesis</note> and:

<margin>Cornelius à Lapide, p. 97, col. 1. <note>À Lapide was a hugely influential Jesuit biblical commentator; the author here is preparing to mock his logic.</note></margin> **"He was the longest-lived of all mortals: for Adam can be called more long-lived than him only by this reasoning: that Adam was created in a state of perfect age and stature—such as one who is already thirty, or even sixty years old at the minimum. Methuselah, however, was born an infant and grew for sixty years until he reached the state and stature in which Adam was created. Therefore, if you subtract sixty years from Methuselah, or add the same to Adam, Adam will surpass Methuselah by twenty-one years."** 

Behold, Jesuitical shrewdness! The question at hand concerns the duration and number of years they lived—not precisely as adults or men, but as human beings. Why did this Jewish enigma not also please the Jesuits: "One day of God is destined for Adam, i.e., a thousand years," according to **Psalm 90:4**. Adam, however, had seen by a prophetic Spirit that David would die in his mother’s womb. Therefore, out of his own millennium, he granted David 70 years. Hence Adam lived for only 930 years. 

But let these things pass. We cannot, however, overlook Henriquez <note>Henriquez was another Jesuit author</note> here, who, falling into a discussion of Enoch under an unlucky omen, writes the following in **Book 12, Chapter 5, Section 1**: *"Enoch, who began to call upon the name of the Lord, was translated as a Virgin into paradise, destined to return in the time of the Antichrist."* 

Henriquez! <note>The author uses a Greek quote from Homer's *Iliad* and *Odyssey* to express shock at a foolish statement.</note> "What a word has escaped the barrier of your teeth?" <note>original Greek: ποῖον σε ἔπος φύγεν ἕρκος ὀδόντων;</note> For it is not said of Enoch that he began to call upon the name of the Lord, but of Enos in **Genesis 4:26**. Splendid "virgins" indeed, who father sons and daughters, as is said of both in **Genesis 5**! Certainly, our common women read the Scriptures more diligently than Henriquez; it is manifest from this that he either never began to read them seriously or did not progress far from the beginning. Would Henriquez not have better consulted his own reputation if he had said the same thing here as Arriaga <note>Rodrigo de Arriaga (1592–1667), a Jesuit philosopher and theologian</note>, who, in **Volume 1 on Thomas, p. 19**, when happening upon the verse *"Who shall declare his generation?"* explained himself thus: *"It is a question for Scripturalists; therefore, I leave it aside."* It is a wonder that such dull-witted men are found today who, in preference to the fundamental scrutiny of the Original Scriptures, would wish to recommend the writings of men of this sort to students of Theology.

### §. 3. 

But we shall bring this chapter to a close once we have added a few things concerning the span and limit of the life—that is, the earthly journey—of Enoch. Concerning him, Moses in **verse 23** has this: *And all the days (the entire age) of Enoch were...* <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "trecen," which would continue as "three hundred [and sixty-five years]" on the next page.</note>

<summary>The author critiques the "Jesuitical" logic used to argue that Adam was effectively older than Methuselah and corrects a scholar named Henriquez for confusing Enoch with Enos and claiming Enoch was a "virgin" despite having children.</summary>
<keywords>Methuselah, longevity, Adam, Enoch, Enos, Jesuit scholarship, Cornelius à Lapide, Henriquez, Arriaga, biblical chronology, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the chronological calculation of the lives of the biblical Patriarchs, specifically focusing on Enoch's lifespan of 365 years. It transitions from comparing his life to Adam's to a broader discussion of his "translation"—his being taken up to heaven by God without dying.</meta>

three hundred sixty-five years. Enoch completed as many years as the solar year has days. He lived therefore on this earth with Adam for 308 years; with Seth, Enos, Kenan, Mahaleel, and Jared for the whole of his life; with his son Methuselah for 300 years; and with his grandson Lamech for 113 years. He was received into heaven 57 years after the death of Adam, 55 years before the departure of Seth, and 69 years before the birth of Noah. This occurred in the year of the world 987, at which time Seth was 857 years old, Enos 752, Cainan 662, Mahaleel 592, Jared 527, Methuselah 300, and Lamech 300—as calculated by Dr. Abraham Calovius <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686) was a prominent Lutheran theologian known for his massive Bible commentaries.</note> in his commentary on Genesis, page 611. His life was shorter than those of the other Patriarchs before the Flood. For Cornelius à Lapide <note>A famous Jesuit biblical scholar (1567–1637) whose work was widely read by both Catholics and Protestants.</note> relies on a false hypothesis when he notes on page 97: *"Enoch was taken up before the year of the world 987."* Therefore, since in this current year of Christ 1615 we are in the year of the world 5565, it follows that Enoch is now in the 4,578th year of his "translation" <note>translation: the act of being moved from earth to heaven without dying.</note>, and the 4,943rd year of his life (and thus now 4,998). Nevertheless, Scripture records exactly how many were the total days of Enoch on earth, to which nothing more can be added after he was translated into heaven and entered eternity. From this, let others judge how the paradox of <unclear>original: "τοῦ δεῖν" (tou dein) — of a certain person</unclear>, a man otherwise most religious, can be defended: *"Of all the sons of Adam, not Methuselah, but his father Enoch (Genesis 5:24) lived the longest, as his life still continues today."* 

Furthermore, some conclude the brevity of Enoch’s life from the use of the singular verb <note>Hebrew: *vayehi*</note> "and it was" instead of the plural "and they were" in verse 23, as if the days of his life, when compared to the days of others, were like a single day (see Paul Fagius <note>A 16th-century Hebraist and reformer.</note> on the *Aramaic Paraphrase of Onkelos* at this place). Others, however, derive from this a mystery concerning the constancy and perpetual tenor of his life (see Mercerus <note>Jean Mercier, a French Hebraist.</note> at this place, page 136, column 1). But this has no foundation in the word itself. For in the concluding sentence on the life of Lamech in verse 31, there is likewise the singular "and it was" instead of the plural "and they were," yet he completed 777 years. So too, based on the warning of the Masora <note>The Masoretes were Jewish scholars who standardized the Hebrew Bible text; they often noted grammatical irregularities.</note> in the concluding sentence for Noah in Genesis 9, last verse, who nevertheless lived 950 years. This should therefore be attributed to a habit of the language, and the word <note>Hebrew: *mispar*</note> "number" or something similar can be understood as implied. And these are the things concerning Enoch's journey.

# CONCERNING THE TRANSLATION OF ENOCH.

1. *What does "And he was not" mean? Was Enoch's translation achieved through the intervention of death? Many among the Jews affirm this: Rashi, Aben-Ezra.*

It addresses various Jewish and Christian interpretations of whether Enoch actually died or was taken to heaven alive.</meta>

*Solomon Ben Melech. Rabbi Saadia Gaon. The paraphrase of Onkelos <note>The Targum Onkelos, the ancient Aramaic translation of the Torah.</note> suffers from a different reading. 2. Certain Christians who are of the same opinion <note>original Greek: ὁμόψηφοι (homopsēphoi)</note> as the Jews. The words of Oecolampadius <note>Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), a leader of the Protestant Reformation in Basel.</note>. Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a Flemish Protestant scholar of Hebrew.</note> hesitates greatly here. André Rivet’s judgment of him is noted. 3. That Enoch was translated without death is proven from the change in the style of the Holy Spirit. Why is "and he died" <note>Hebrew: וימת (vayamot)</note> said of the others? 4. On the emphasis of the word "took" <note>Hebrew: לקח (lakach)</note> in this place. 5. It is clear from the New Testament that Enoch did not die. 6. Jewish minor objections are satisfied. 7. Many among the Jews concede that Enoch was translated alive. From where did they gather this? Drusius is noted again. 8. The process of Enoch's translation cannot be determined with certainty. Was he translated while the other Patriarchs were watching? 9. The destination of the translation, which the Jews and Roman Catholics <note>original: Pontificii (meaning those loyal to the Pope)</note> establish as the earthly paradise. 10. Concerning Enoch's state after the translation and at present; the same authors are ridiculous.*

->§. 1.<-

**W**e have seen the course of Enoch's life; now his departure must be examined, which **Moses** sets before our eyes with wondrous brevity <note>original Greek: συντομίᾳ (syntomia)</note>. **And he was not.** <note>Hebrew: ואיננו (v'einennu)</note> **Rivet** <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot theologian.</note> advises that we should supply either *He was [no more]* or *He was [not] found*. But the former is already present in the Hebrew idiom. For the word *not* <note>Hebrew: אין (ayin)</note> contains within its own meaning the substantive verb "to be," and therefore it cannot be combined with finite verbs. The meaning, therefore, is: And (since he had not yet fulfilled even half the years granted to others at that time, and so in the view of others was as if in the middle of his course) he was no more, **he was not found** <note>original Greek: οὐχ εὑρίσκετο (ouch heurisketo), citing the Septuagint and Hebrews 11:5</note>; he ceased to tarry and dwell, namely, among **mortals** <note>original Greek: θνητούς (thnētous)</note> or inhabitants of the earth, with whom he had been until now. For no one has ever said he was annihilated, nor could anyone say so. The reason: **because GOD took him.** <note>Hebrew: כי לקח אתו אלהים (ki lakach oto Elohim)</note> But as to whether this was done with or without the intervention of death, you may see authors—to use a **Homeric** phrase—
->**fighting with opposing words.**<-
<note>original Greek: ἀντιβίοις μαχέσασθαι ἐπέεσιν (antibiois machesthai epeesin)</note>

Among the **Jews** you may find those to whom the words of **Moses** seem to imply an ordinary death. They say, however, that the style of speech is changed here because, since **Enoch** was remarkably pious and his age was most corrupt and dangerous, God snatched him away prematurely through death lest he be corrupted by his **contemporaries** <note>original Greek: συγχρόνοις (synchronois)</note>—indeed, that his holiness had already begun to fade somewhat. **Menasseh ben Israel** <note>A famous Portuguese rabbi and author (1604–1657) who lived in Amsterdam.</note> openly confesses this to be the opinion of certain of his kinsmen in his work *On Human Frailty*, page 106 (although he is not of **the same opinion** <note>original Greek: ὁμόψηφος (homopsēphos)</note> as them here). *"As far as Enoch is concerned,"* he says, *"not o-"* <note>The text breaks off mid-word "contin-" or "omnis" due to the page ending.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the theological debate surrounding Enoch's "translation," examining the Hebrew and Greek phrasing to determine if he died an ordinary death or was taken alive to heaven. It highlights the tension between literal scriptural brevity and various Jewish and Christian interpretations of his sudden departure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the translation of Enoch. While the previous page introduced various Christian and Jewish perspectives on whether Enoch was taken to heaven alive, this page focuses on the specific arguments of Jewish commentators who believe "taking" refers to an early, natural death rather than a physical ascent to heaven.</meta>

<sig>C 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

...the common opinion holds among our people <note>The author likely refers to his fellow Christians or the broader scholarly consensus of his time.</note> that he was translated into heaven with both body and soul. However, many of the ancients in the *Bereshit Rabbah* <note>original: *Bereſit rabba*; a collection of ancient Jewish interpretations and legends concerning the Book of Genesis.</note> maintain that the phrase: *And Enoch walked with God*, and the phrase, *because God took him*: denotes a brief death. This is the opinion followed by Rabbi Solomon <note>Raſchi (Rashi), the famous 11th-century commentator.</note>, Abarbanel, and the learned Ibn Ezra, who says regarding this same text that "he died" <note>original Hebrew: מת (*met*)</note>, and proves that this way of speaking is very common in the Holy Scriptures. It is similar to when Elijah said, *Take, I pray, my soul:* and when God said to Ezekiel <note>original: *Jebazkel*</note>, to signify to him the death of his wife: *Behold, I will take away the desire of your eyes*, and a little later it is said that she died. It does not follow from this, however, that she was translated into heaven with body and soul. The same can be established regarding Enoch, as is clear from the *Tosefta* <note>A supplement to the Mishnah, the first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions.</note> at the end of the first chapter of *Yevamot*; and from a manuscript copy of the *Targum* <note>An ancient Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible.</note> that existed at that time, specifically the Aramaic Paraphrase of Onkelos, which said concerning this verse: *And he was not, for the Lord caused him to die* <note>original Aramaic: ולאיתוהי ארי אמית יתיה יי (*Velaithohi ari amit yathiah Ya*)</note>. Among those cited, let us hear Rashi and Ibn Ezra. These are the words of the former:

*And Enoch walked. He was a righteous man but light-minded <note>original: *levi animo*; suggesting a character that was easily influenced or unstable in faith.</note> and easily turned back to wickedness; therefore the Holy One, blessed be He, hurried and took him by death before his time. Hence the Holy Scripture changes its style in the description of his death and says: And he was not—that is, in the world to complete his years. Because he took him—meaning, before his time, as it is said there: Behold I take from you the desire of your eyes. Ezekiel 24:16.* <note>The author provides a Latin translation of Rashi's Hebrew: ויתהלך חנוך . צדיק היה וקל בדעתו לשוב להרשיע וכו׳</note>

Indeed, Ibn Ezra notes these things on this passage: *God took him. He died. As in: Take I pray my soul. Jonah 4:3. Behold I take the desire of your eyes, in Ezekiel 24:16. For afterwards it is explained what happened, and his wife died, verse 18. Furthermore, the "taking" of Enoch is not attributed to a plague <note>original Hebrew: מגפה (*magephah*)</note> as it is in Ezekiel; nor to the formula: "And he died."* <note>The author translates Ibn Ezra's Hebrew: כי לקח אתו אלהים / מת וכן קח נא את נפשי וכו׳</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological debate regarding Genesis 5:24 ("And Enoch was not, for God took him"). Having previously established that some Jewish commentators interpret "taking" as a metaphor for death, the author now examines specific scriptural proofs and the conflicting witnesses of ancient Aramaic and Arabic translations.</meta>

But the reason for this interpretation must be sought from the words of *Asaph* <note>Asaph is the traditional author of several Psalms; the author is drawing a parallel between the "taking" of Enoch and the language of the Psalms.</note>. And afterward *you will receive me* <note>original Hebrew: ותקחני (*ve-tiqqacheni*)</note> with glory (*Psalm 73:24*). And likewise in the *Psalm of the sons of Korah*: Truly God will redeem my soul from the underworld, for *he will receive me* <note>original Hebrew: יקחני (*yiqqacheni*)</note> (Selah) (*Psalm 49:15*). To the wise, this is enough <note>A Latin idiom, *sapienti sat*, suggesting the scriptural parallel is self-evident.</note>. Thus Rabbi Solomon ben Melech, regarding the words *because God took him*, warns that it signifies *the matter of the death of the righteous* <note>original Hebrew: ענין מיתת הצדיק (*inyan mitat ha-tzaddiq*)</note>. To these may be added the Arabic translation of Rabbi Saadia <note>Saadia Gaon (882–942), a prominent Jewish philosopher and exegete.</note>, which for the phrase *and he was not* expressly has *tawaffa* <note>original Arabic: תוהפא</note>, meaning "he has passed away" or "he returned his spirit to God." However, regarding the Paraphrase of Onkelos <note>The *Targum Onkelos*, the standard Aramaic translation of the Torah.</note>, which they say records the same in the *Bereshit Rabbah* and the *Tosefta* on *Yevamot* (folio 16, page 2), the matter is not quite so certain. This is what Menasseh ben Israel <note>A leading 17th-century Portuguese rabbi based in Amsterdam.</note> shows (in the place cited, page 107) with these words: *But Rabbi Bahya and Hezekiah affirm that the best copy of Onkelos is that in which it is written in this manner:* "And he exists, for the LORD did not kill him" <note>original Aramaic: ואיתוהי ארי לא אמית יתיה יי (*v'itohi ari la amit yateih YY*)</note>. And it stands as (IN LIFE), for God did not put him to death: *deriving that word* "and he was not" <note>original Hebrew: ואיננו (*ve-einennu*)</note> *from the adverb* "Is" <note>original Hebrew: אין (*en* or *hin*), which the author here treats as a variant of *hen* (behold) or a positive existence, though it usually means "not."</note>, which is diametrically opposed to the first version. Nevertheless, in the editions of Venice and Bomberg (to which I add from my own collection the Royal English, Basel, etc.), it is read differently: namely, *and he is not (that is, in the world), for the LORD did not kill him.*

§ 2. Among Christians, Hieronymus ab Oleastro <note>A 16th-century Portuguese Dominican scholar.</note> was the first on this passage—following the consensus of all antiquity regarding Enoch being translated alive (a consensus so great that it is not permitted to doubt it, according to Augustine in the second book of *On the Grace of God against Pelagius and Caelestius*, chapter 22)—to judge it a probable opinion if someone were to maintain that Enoch was translated through death, even though he meanwhile believed the other view (translation while alive) to be more probable. Following Oleastro were Agostino Steuco of Gubbio and François Vatable on this passage; Marcus Marinus in his *Noah's Ark*; Thomas Brightman in his work on the *Apocalypse*; to whom they also add Calvin, for whom André Rivet wishes to make an excuse (page 242). Certainly, Calvin did not explicitly assert that Enoch was dead. But Johannes Oecolampadius <note>A prominent German Protestant Reformer (1482–1531).</note> most clearly stated that Enoch had died. His words on *Romans 5:14* are as follows: *That is, death reigned even over those (namely, the saints) who did not sin against a given law, like Adam; yet they sinned nevertheless, whence they were not exempted from death. Or, they did not sin in this specific point like Adam, because in Adam there was an integrity of nature which was lacking in his posterity. One may gather, however, that Enoch and Elijah were not exempted from death, although common opinion believes they still* [live]...

So says **Oecolampadius** <note>Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), a prominent Protestant Reformer.</note>, whom **Wolfgang Franz** therefore rebuked in his *On the Interpretation of the Oracles of Scripture*, page 134. Furthermore, it is difficult to say what **DRUSIUS** <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a renowned Flemish Protestant Old Testament scholar and Hebraist.</note> thought here, since he either did not know or—as seems more likely to us—did not wish to pronounce his view boldly. It was otherwise his custom to propose many possibilities but to conclude very few. In his *Annotations* on this passage, he says: *"And he was not," evidently among the living. Thus even one who has died is said "not to be." Rachel, weeping for her children, refused to be comforted, because they are not* (Jeremiah 31:15); *that is, they do not live. Therefore, one who lives is said "to be," and one who is not in life is said "not to be."* He adds, *Took—that is, God took him away, snatched him away before his proper time.* Many times, he says, I have wondered, and I still wonder, what moved the Jews (for I am not speaking of Christians now) to gather from this passage that Enoch still lives. For even if we grant this to be true, it cannot be gathered from these words as they wish. Indeed, one who is dead is said "not to be," and one who is carried off by an untimely death is said to be "snatched" or "taken." Whoever denies this shows himself to be a child in these matters of literature. Having thus overturned the literal foundation of the text, although **Drusius** adds that he does not wish to oppose the Apostle <note>Referring to the author of the Book of Hebrews in the New Testament, specifically Hebrews 11:5, which states Enoch did not see death.</note>, he nevertheless attempted to weaken the truth of the history from which the Apostle took it. *Far be it from me,* (he says) *to contradict the Apostle asserting that he was translated so that he might not see death. He who dies sees death. But there is a certain death of sinners, about which, if the Apostle did not speak, the author of the Book of Wisdom nevertheless spoke for him: "lest malice change his understanding."* <note>A reference to Wisdom of Solomon 4:11.</note> *The words are clear enough: he snatched him away, lest he be corrupted by the vices of his age.* At last, he adds that he is rather inclined to believe he was changed. He says Enoch can be said not to have died, because either he was translated together with his body, or he met death in righteousness (which is not properly death), or because Scripture does not use the word "death" for him as it does for Jacob, or because he was changed. On the other hand, he says he could be called dead, either because "change" takes the place of death, or because Moses' words can conveniently be understood as death,

<meta>This page continues a philological and theological debate regarding Genesis 5:24. The previous page discussed Johannes Drusius’s hesitant view on whether Enoch’s "taking" refers to a natural, albeit premature, death or a miraculous removal from the world. The author is now critiquing Drusius's reliance on certain Rabbinic interpretations.</meta>

...they are, or because there are those among the Rabbis who think he was snatched away by death. This is what Drusius says. Behold indeed the critic’s judgment! Enoch can also be called "dead" because there are those among the Rabbis who think he was snatched away by death. Certainly, if whatever some Rabbi thought can be stated as fact immediately, you will soon say that everything can be said which I used to deny could be said. For indeed, no *invention* so feverish can be produced that has not, at some point, entered the mind of some Rabbi. Let those who think we are unfairly biased against Drusius know that he did not even please his own associates. This is the judgment of **Rivet** <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a prominent French Protestant theologian.</note> concerning him: *In him, I was never pleased by his opinion, often delivered as if from an oracle <note>original: "tanquam ex tripode," literally "as if from a tripod," referring to the seat of the Pythia at Delphi</note>, and his contempt for others. This is the view of Drusius from a member of his own sect.*

§. 3. Truly, however, it can be established in many ways that Enoch was not taken away by the intervening separation of soul and body—that is, that he did not die. While we attempt to show this, we are sometimes forced to use the words of theologians—not because we are taking their role upon ourselves (we know our limits), but lest we, while **acting as philologists** <note>original Greek: Φιλολογοῦντες</note>, change **the pattern of healthy words** <note>original Greek: τρόπον τῶν ὑγιαινόντων λόγων; a reference to the biblical instruction in 2 Timothy 1:13 to hold to "sound teaching."</note>. For all those critics seem to us to be foolish here who are "wise" without Holy Theology. If any of our words—which we hope is not the case—do not agree with Theology, let them be not only unsaid, but let them be struck out by that very fact.

But before all else, we want it to be carefully weighed why the Holy Spirit, when sealing the lives of the other **antediluvian** <note>Existing before the Biblical Flood.</note> Patriarchs, says so consistently **"and he died"** <note>Hebrew: וַיָּמֹת (*Vajamot*)</note>—as concerning Adam in verse 5, Seth in verse 8, Enos in verse 11, Kenan in verse 14, Mahalel in verse 17, Jared in verse 20, Methuselah in verse 27, and Lamech in verse 31—yet does not use that funeral dirge and mournful conclusion in the closing of Enoch’s course. Instead, He forms His style far differently: *And he was no more, because God took him.* Therefore, it must be that something singular and plainly extraordinary happened to this man, distinct from the others, when he departed from the earth. It is well known that the others died. But what would we be attributing to Enoch above the others in his departure if we said that he also died? If that had indeed happened, the Holy Spirit would no more have remained silent about it than about the others. And to what purpose did He lead in **by way of explanation** <note>original Greek: αἰτιολογικῶς (*aitiologikōs*)</note>: *And he walked with God* (clinging to Him through true faith; *For holiness of life is not the cause of translation to the other life, but faith, by which the Apostle testifies in Hebrews 11:5 that Enoch pleased God and was translated.* So says the Great **Dr. Calovius** <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a leading figure in Lutheran Orthodoxy</note>, page 617), unless the phrase that follows contains the reason for some remarkable divine grace...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the translation of Genesis 5:24 regarding the fate of Enoch. The previous page established a critique of Johannes Drusius, who argued that Enoch's "taking" might simply mean a premature natural death. The author is now demonstrating why that interpretation is logically and scripturally inconsistent.</meta>

...of grace? But what is the connection <note>original Greek: συνάφεια (synapheia), meaning the logical link or coherence</note>: *He walked with God, and therefore he died*, and indeed sooner than the other *Patriarchs*? It is as if sudden death were the ordinary consequence of walking with God, even though it is well known that Noah—of whom it is similarly said: *Noah walked with God* <note>original Hebrew: את-האלהים התהלך-נח (*eth-ha-Elohim hith-halek Noah*)</note> (*Genesis 6:9*)—completed 950 years (*Genesis 9:29*). Therefore, one must be impudently argumentative to say that Enoch departed from the earth in the same manner as it is established the others departed. 

Indeed, it is worth investigating in passing why this clause—*And he died*—is so carefully added to the history of the others, since it would be easily understood that they had died unless they were expressly exempted by some special privilege. Rabbi Moses Albilda <note>A 16th-century Jewish philosopher and commentator known for his work *Olat Tamid*.</note> raises a doubt about this on page 45, column 3, near the end: *The second point is that Moses says of each of them: "and he died," which likewise seems superfluous. For since he indicates that there were so many years of life, it could be established that he died afterward; hence, among the descendants of Noah, nothing of this sort is mentioned, neither the first nor the second.*

He then responds on page 46, section 2, in the middle, that the wise observe: *The wicked are called dead even while they still live* <note>A common Rabbinic adage suggesting that those without spiritual merit do not truly "live" in the theological sense.</note>, *whence they may not rightly be said to die, since they were already dead while living. But these (the righteous) die,* he continues, *when they have fulfilled their days; for while they live, they are truly said to have lived because of the virtues in which they excelled. Because, therefore, neither situation applied to the descendants of Noah, there is no mention made there of the sum of the life spent nor that they died, since this does not apply to the wicked like them.* 

Others wish it to be implied that they were extinguished not by the flood, but by a natural death. Thus Abendana <note>Jacob Abendana (1630–1695), a Sephardic Rabbi and scholar in London.</note> says on this passage: *It is said of each of these generations: "And he died," because they all passed away in peace and did not see the evil of the flood.* So too says the author of the *Tzenah Urenah* <note>original: "Zennorenna," a popular Yiddish homiletic commentary on the Torah, typically intended for a female readership.</note> on folio 9, column 3. For us, the illustrious Rivet has summarized everything in a few [words]—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Rabbi Moses Albilda's commentary regarding the deaths of the Patriarchs and transitions into a philological analysis of the Hebrew word "laqach" (took) to prove that Enoch was physically translated by God.</meta>

...the argument seems intertwined, and therefore we deem his words worthy to be added here:

> *To the long review of the Patriarchs' lives, the final line is eventually added: **and he died**. This is repeated for all of them, undoubtedly for a serious reason. The primary reason is to show that the divine threat against sinful man was not in vain; conversely, that the serpent's promise was empty and deceptive, etc. [A bit further on]. To this reason, other less primary ones can be added. 1. That he wanted to designate that all of those men died by their own natural death, not swallowed up by the flood or taken by some other violent death. 2. To indicate a clear distinction between the present life, which ends in death, and the future life, which has no end. 3. That nothing is long-lasting if it has an end, because whatever passes away, no matter how long it was, is considered as nothing. 4. So that no one at this time, or henceforth, however long-lived, should promise himself immortality in this life, but should spend his life in the meditation of death, always prepared for that end when God might wish to call him from his post.* These are his words from *Exercise 48 on Genesis, near the end.*

§. 4. Furthermore, the phrase <note>original Hebrew: לקח (*laqach*)</note> **He took him**, which is God’s act, serves to significantly confirm our opinion. Immersed in these circumstances, it denotes the *translation, transportation, or transfer* of a person from place to place, or from a former dwelling to another, as in *Genesis 24:17, 27:45, Exodus 14:11, etc.* For this reason, it is explained through the term **translation** <note>original Greek: μετάθεσιν (*metathesin*), meaning a change of position or state</note> by the *Septuagint interpreters in this place* <note>The Septuagint is the influential Greek translation of the Old Testament from the 3rd century BCE.</note> and by *the son of Sirach in chapter 44:16*, and also by the *Apostle himself in Hebrews 11:5*. We wish to weigh this phrase more carefully. Until then, *Enoch* had been on earth and lived among men in a similar fashion <note>original Greek: ὁμοειδῶς (*homoeidōs*), meaning he lived a visible, human life like others</note>. Suddenly it happens that he is no longer seen: <note>original Hebrew: ואיננו (*ve-en-en-nu*)</note> **and he was not**. Now, it is certain he did not simply vanish. What then? Did he perhaps hide himself, or plunge into the earth or sea? Or did he rush out beyond the earth? But to where? To whom? In what way? If he was snatched away by another, by whom? *Moses* answers: *God* <note>Hebrew: לקח (*laqach*)</note> **took him**—meaning He received him after he was translated from the earth (and not just his soul alone). The same word occurs regarding the translation of *Elijah in 2 Kings 2:3, 5, 9, 10*, who ascended into heaven in *verse 11*. Because of this, *Elisha* <note>original Hebrew: לא ראהו עוד (*lo ra’ahu od*)</note> **saw him no more** in *verse 12*, just as *Enoch*, when he had been translated, **no longer appeared among** his contemporaries <note>original Greek: συγχρόνους (*synchronous*)</note>. Moreover, a similar word is used regarding the *Savior*, who **was taken up into heaven** <note>original Greek: ἀνελήφθη εἰς τὸν οὐρανὸν (*anelephthē eis ton ouranon*)</note> in *Mark 16:19*. Therefore, the translation commonly attributed to *Jonathan ben Uziel* <note>Refers to the Targum Jonathan, an Aramaic paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible.</note> elegantly expressed the sense of the *Scripture*:

<summary>The author argues that the biblical phrase "God took him" refers to a physical translation to heaven rather than death, drawing parallels between Enoch, Elijah, and the Ascension of Christ. He contrasts this with the explicit mention of death for other Patriarchs, which served as a reminder of human mortality and the falsity of the serpent's promise.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological proof that Enoch did not die a natural death but was physically taken by God. It moves from analyzing the Hebrew word "laqach" (took) to examining ancient Aramaic paraphrases and New Testament evidence, specifically from the Epistles of Jude and Hebrews.</meta>

...provided: <note>original Aramaic: והא ליתוהי עם דיירי ארעא ארום אתנגיד וסליק לרקיעא במימר קדם יי</note> **"And behold! he was no longer among the inhabitants of the earth. For he was taken away and he ascended into heaven by the Word that is before the Lord."** <note>This is a translation of the Aramaic Targum, an ancient Jewish paraphrase of the Bible.</note> This undoubtedly hints at the **prophetic** <note>original Greek: προφητικόν (*prophētikon*)</note> word about which Enoch had prophesied, according to the testimony of the Apostle Jude in verse 14.

### §. 5.
But the best Arbiter and Interpreter of this matter is the Holy Spirit himself in the New Testament. For thus says Saint Paul in **Hebrews 11:5**: <note>original Greek: Πίσει Ενωχ μετετέθη τοῦ μὴ ἰδεῖν θάνατον. καὶ οὐχ εὑρίσκετο, διότι μετέθηκεν αὐτὸν ὁ θεός.</note> **"By faith Enoch was translated so that he should not see death; and he was not found, because God had translated him."** Certainly, no room for evasion is left here. Indeed, Christian men should be ashamed to cast doubt on such clear writing by arguing **conjecturally** <note>original Greek: στοχασικῶς (*stochastikōs*), meaning by guesswork or speculation</note>. What could have been said more clearly? The phrase **"to see death"** is denied in the case of Enoch. In both the Old and New Testaments, this phrase signifies **natural death** <term>death of nature (mors naturae): the separation of soul and body that all humans eventually undergo</term>. Thus, **Psalm 89:48**: <note>original Hebrew: ולא יראה מות</note> **"What man lives and shall not see death?"** Likewise, in **Luke 2:26**, it is said to have been revealed to Simeon by the Holy Spirit that **"he would not see death"** before he had seen the Christ. It is also worthy of consideration that when the Apostle said Abel still speaks through faith **after having died** <note>original Greek: ἀποθανόντα (*apothanonta*)</note>, he immediately turns the course of his speech to Enoch and says he was translated **"so as not to see death."** Certainly, there is no doubt that he is speaking of natural death.

### §. 6.
It remains for us to call the Jewish "little exceptions" to examination. At the outset, we do not think it worth the effort to linger on their quibble that Enoch was a man of **uncertain character** <note>original Greek: ἀμφίδοξον (*amphidoxon*)</note>, and was therefore snatched away by death early lest he eventually degenerate completely. For from the phrase <note>original Hebrew: התהלך את האלהים (*hithallek eth ha-Elohim*)</note> **"he walked with God,"** these detractors most foolishly infer Enoch’s inconstancy or a BOARDSIZE inclination toward vice, as we have proved above. If that phrase implies such a thing, how is it that Noah—of whom the same phrase is used—completed nine and a half centuries? The Blessed Martin Luther rightly says regarding this passage <note>page 79, column 2</note>: 

> *The Rabbis,* he says, *are worthy of strong criticism, for whatever the Scripture holds as most remarkable, they most foully corrupt. Thus they chatter about Enoch in this place, saying that he was indeed good and just, but greatly inclined to carnal desires; therefore God had mercy on him and prevented him by death, lest he should sin and be damned. I ask you, is this not a remarkable corruption of the text? For why is it necessary to say of Enoch alone that he had evil inclinations, as if the rest of the Patriarchs did not also have and feel them? Furthermore, why do they not observe that Moses says twice: "He walked with God"? This is surely a testimony—*

<summary>The author uses New Testament evidence (Hebrews 11) and linguistic analysis of the phrase "to see death" to argue that Enoch was physically translated to heaven without dying. He also refutes Rabbinic interpretations—supported by Martin Luther's commentary—which suggested Enoch died early due to a moral weakness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological defense of Enoch’s bodily translation into heaven. The previous page established that the New Testament supports a literal reading of Enoch "not seeing death." This page addresses counter-arguments regarding specific Hebrew vocabulary used in the Old Testament accounts of his departure.</meta>

### §. 7.
...testimony is that he did not yield to temptations, but strongly overcame them by faith, etc. Moreover, Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616) was a prominent Flemish Protestant Old Testament scholar and Hebraist.</note> notes along with the Jews that the word **איננו** <note>Hebrew: *eīnennū*, meaning "he was not."</note> is also said of the dead (*Jeremiah 31:15; Matthew 2:18*); we knew this before they did. For we would not contend so uniquely on this single word alone that Enoch did not die, unless other circumstances urged it or other reasons were available. However, it is false that **איננו** formally and precisely implies death. In general, it signifies "not being present" or "not being found" in one's usual place or state. By consequence, it is used for the dead. Meanwhile, it is also used for the living. Thus the Patriarch Jacob says of his son Simeon, who was left behind in Egypt (*Genesis 42:36*): **שמעון איננו** <note>Hebrew: *Shim'ōn eīnennū*</note>, **"Simeon is not present."** Indeed, it is said of God, **ואיננו** <note>Hebrew: *v'eīnennū*, "and he is not"</note> (*Job 23:8*), when he does not show himself to be seen. Finally, if **איננו** formally implies death, there is a danger that death itself might die, for Job says of death (*Job 3:20*) **ואיננו**. In passing, we note from the *Masora* <term>Masora: the tradition and body of notes concerning the correct spelling and statistics of the Hebrew Bible text.</term> on Job 27 that **איננו** occurs twelve times in Holy Scripture, namely: *Genesis 5:24; Isaiah 19:7; Jeremiah 49:10 and 50:20; Psalm 37:10; Psalm 103:16; Job 3:20, 23:8, 24:24, and 27:19; Proverbs 23:5; Ecclesiastes 6:2.* Let anyone apply the meaning of death to all these if they can.

Nor are we moved by those who bring forward passages to show that God "took" those whom He killed. For these are quite irrelevant <note>original Greek: ἀλλότρια (*allotria*), meaning foreign or unrelated to the matter at hand.</note>. Granted, "to take the soul" certainly means to kill in the case of Jonah or Elijah. But God is not said here to have *taken the soul of Enoch*, but Enoch himself, as he was. Nor is the passage found in Ezekiel successfully dragged into this. When God causes the Prophet's wife to die, He certainly deprives him of the pleasure he took from her sight and company. However, "to kill the wife" and "to take the desire of the eyes" are not formally equivalent <note>original Greek: ἰσοδυναμοῦντες (*isodynamountes*), meaning having equal power or meaning.</note> and reciprocal phrases. For if, by God's will, the wife had been led away alive by enemies, the "desire of the eyes" would have been taken from the Prophet, and yet the wife could live elsewhere. Next, one must attend to the circumstances of the text everywhere, rather than looking at individual words with dull eyes <note>original Latin: *oculis vaccinis*, literally "cow eyes," meaning a staring, uncomprehending gaze.</note>. Ezekiel says in clear words that the wife died. But from our text, no such thing can be drawn out; rather, the opposite. Perhaps more plausibly, foolish men would have called Job to their side, who says among other things about his children crushed in the ruins: *The Lord gave and the Lord* **לקח** <note>Hebrew: *laqach*, "took"</note> *took away* (*Job 1:21*); if indeed it were right to care nothing for what is in the text, or what precedes and follows it. But let us leave those "Arcadian cattle" <term>Arcadian cattle (Arcadica pecora): a proverbial insult for people who are slow-witted or stubborn, originating from the idea of rural Arcadians being simple-minded.</term>, whom it is most difficult to correct and inglorious to defeat.

<meta>catchword: Sun-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Hebrew phrase "he was not" does not necessarily mean death, citing several biblical examples where it refers to absence or invisibility rather than mortality. It also clarifies that when God "took" Enoch, it referred to the whole person, unlike other passages where "taking" specifically refers to the soul at death.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological argument that Enoch did not die but was bodily translated to heaven. Having previously discussed the Hebrew word "eīnennū" (he was not), the author now turns to Jewish tradition and various scholarly interpretations to support the claim that Enoch entered Paradise alive.</meta>

### §. 7. 

There are, however, those among the Jews for whom, as the saying goes, *the Titan has fashioned their hearts from a better clay* <note>Latin: *queis, quod dicitur, de meliore luto finxit præcordia Titan*. This is a classical allusion to Juvenal, meaning some people possess a superior or more refined nature/intellect.</note>. We have already seen the interpretations from the Targums <note>Aramaic translations or paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible.</note> that strongly favor our position. From the Talmudic appendix, it is worth citing here what is said in the *Tractate Derech Eretz Zuta, chapter 1, near the end*: **תשעה נכנסו בחייהם בגן ערן ואלו הן** <note>Hebrew: "Nine entered into the Garden of Eden [Paradise] during their lives, and they are these:"</note> **Nine enter Paradise alive; namely, Enoch son of Jared, Elijah, the Messiah, Eliezer the servant of Abraham, Hiram King of Tyre, Ebed-melech the Ethiopian, Jabez the son of Rabbi Judah the Prince (otherwise known as the Holy Rabbi), Bithiah the daughter of Pharaoh, and Serah the daughter of Asher**, **ויש אומרים אף יהושע בן לוי** <note>Hebrew: "and some say also Joshua son of Levi."</note> **to whom others add Joshua the son of Levi.** Isaac Abravanel <note>A famous Portuguese Jewish statesman and theologian (1437–1508).</note> denies that these should be taken literally, though others affirm it. For our purpose, it is sufficient that Enoch is said to have entered Paradise alive (which is cited as being <term>καδδιαμετρον</term> <note>Greek: *katadiametron*, meaning "diametrically opposite."</note> to death in the *Tosephtot Yevamot, folio 16, 2*), even if this claim might be false regarding most of the others listed, or certainly can never be proven.

Furthermore, regarding the translation of Enoch without death, several authorities agree: *Josephus in Book 1 of Antiquities, chapter 8; Rabbi Akiba in Rakanati; Haskuni on this passage; Rabbi Moses Albelda in Olat Tamid, folio 46, 3; the author of Tsenah Urenah, folio 9, 2; Rabbi Moses Cordovero (also called Corduarius) in Pardes; Rabbi David Kimchi; Menasseh ben Israel in "On Human Frailty," page 108; and others.* The Cabbalists <note>Followers of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah).</note> observe that Enoch was snatched away in the same manner as Elijah in a whirlwind; they note that the letters of the phrase **כי לקח אותו אלהים** <note>Hebrew: *kī lāqach ōtō Elōhīm*, "for God took him."</note> produce the same numerical value (namely, 667) as the words **ברוח סערה העלהו** <note>Hebrew: *b'rūach s'ārāh he'elāhū*, "He caused him to ascend in a whirlwind."</note> **"He caused him to ascend in a wind of the whirlwind."** 

However, Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius, a prominent Hebraist mentioned on the previous page.</note> wonders how the Jews could have reached such a conclusion—believing Enoch was translated without death—since it can by no means be gathered from the words of the text itself. But it is clear from what has been said that it can be very well concluded. Nevertheless, I wish to add for good measure the words of the *Illustrious Rivetus* <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot theologian.</note> *on page 242: It cannot be denied that those phrases are very often used for those who are taken from life by death; nevertheless, those Hebrews judged correctly, not based on one or two phrases, but from the sequence of the entire history. They considered the preface regarding Enoch’s piety and justice. They knew that the longevity of the godly pertains to the blessing of GOD. They heard that Enoch did not reach the age of his fathers, nor even of his sons, on this earth. They saw it expressly said of all the others that they had died, while for Enoch, the Scripture used different words, which sufficiently indicated that he was no longer seen on earth, nor was he buried by men. They considered that not only was it said that "he was not," but it was added that God had "taken him": which they saw applied not only to the soul, but to the transition of the body...*

<summary>The author cites various Jewish traditions (Talmud, Cabbala) and Christian scholars (Rivet) to argue that Enoch's "taking" was a unique, bodily translation to Paradise without experiencing death.</summary>

§. 8. We have thus far proved that Enoch did not die, but was translated by God alive in body and soul, and thus we think we have fulfilled our duty. Accordingly, let no one demand from us the specific method and process of this translation. For it is not our part to explain it, nor if it were, could we bring forward anything certain. Without doubt, therefore, his father and grandfather were dismayed by the very heavy loss of such a great man. For they knew with what zeal he had taught religion, and how much he had suffered. When they suddenly lose this man, therefore, who held the testimony of piety before God and men, what do you think was in their minds? Here, then, give me either a poet or a copious rhetorician who may treat this passage with the dignity and emotion it deserves. Enos, Seth, and all the other Patriarchs do not know where Enoch has been snatched. They seek him, therefore; his son Methuselah seeks him; other children and grandchildren seek him. They were suspicious of the malice of the Cainites <note>The descendants of Cain, often characterized in theology as the wicked enemies of the godly line of Seth.</note>. And so, perhaps they thought he had been killed and secretly buried like Abel. Until finally, through God revealing it by an angel, they learned he had been snatched by God himself and placed in Paradise. However, they did not learn that on the first or second day, but perhaps with many months or years intervening. Meanwhile, the holy men mourned his miserable condition, as if he had been killed by the hypocritical Cainites.* To others it seems probable...
