<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Enoch's departure from Earth. While the previous page focused on philological arguments against the idea that Enoch died, this page explores the circumstances of his translation—who witnessed it, the physical transformation he underwent, and his final destination.</meta>

That Enoch was translated while many of the patriarchs of the antediluvian world were looking on—namely Seth, Enos, Cainan, Mahalalel, his father Jared, his son Methuselah, and his grandson Lamech—is conjectured by *Lyferus, Walther*, and others. They suggest this occurred on the Sabbath itself, following the established exhortation during the performance of sacrifice, when Enoch was carried up in a flame; this is based on a comparison of *Jude 13*. The reasoning for this conjecture, in the judgment of that most eminent man *Doctor Calovius*, our venerable Gamaliel <note>A reference to Gamaliel the Elder, a celebrated teacher of the Law in the New Testament; here used as a title of high respect for Calovius's wisdom.</note>, is described by *the Blessed Lyferus* as *not ungraceful*. *More probable* (he says here) *seems the opinion of those who establish that this was done in the sight of the Church. For how would Enoch, by his translation, be an example of repentance to the world—by which the impious might be terrified and those who had fallen away might be stirred to regret—if these things had happened secretly, and no one had known where he had gone? Unless this had happened publicly with many watching, and especially in the presence of his Father and Son, there would have been a suspicion that he had been killed by the Lamechites or Cainites, just as Abel was. Therefore, God translated him in such a way that the remaining Fathers could understand that he was in peace.* Furthermore, concerning the "change" <note>original Greek: *metallagē*</note>, by which they believe that in his translation Enoch’s "corruptible part" <note>original Greek: *to phtharton*</note> put on "incorruption" <note>original Greek: *aphtharsian*</note> and his "mortal part" <note>original Greek: *to thnēton*</note> put on "immortality" <note>original Greek: *athanasian*</note> (*1 Corinthians 15:50 to 54*), we add nothing, since these matters must be referred to the schools of orthodox theologians. Meanwhile, "in passing" <note>original Greek: *hōs en parodō*</note>, mention should be made of the Chaldean-Samaritan Paraphrase, which expressed the words of the text "God took him" <note>original Hebrew: *laqachathū Elohīm*</note> as: *The angels took him.* <note>original Aramaic: *nasabthē malachayyā*</note> The distinguished *Edmund Castell* testifies that this is also the sentiment of the Arabic-Samaritan translation. We do not dispute the matter itself. We admit that Enoch was surrounded by angels when he was translated. However, because this Interpreter substitutes an angel for GOD, both here and in many other places, he rightly incurs the censure of the distinguished *Hottinger* in his *Dissertation on the Paris Heptapla.*

§. 9. Furthermore, our text does not expressly mention the *destination* <note>Latin: *terminum ad quem*, literally "the boundary to which."</note> of the translation. However, that it is the *heaven of the blessed* is established from the analogy of the translation of Elijah. Meanwhile, the Jews and the Papists <note>Latin: *Pontificii*, referring to Roman Catholics.</note> imagine that Enoch was snatched away into an *earthly Paradise*. Among the former, *Manasseh ben Israel* (in book 2 of *On the Resurrection of the Dead*, chapter 3, page 136) writes explicitly concerning Elijah (to whom he joins Enoch as a peer near the end of the chapter): *He was translated whole, in body and soul, into the earthly paradise. He then proves it from a letter written and sent (as he imagines) after the transportation. From which, he adds, it is to be gathered that he is in a physical place and manages human and earthly affairs...*

### §. 8.
Thus, we have proved so far that Enoch did not die, but was translated by God while alive in both body and soul, and so we believe we have fulfilled our duty. Therefore, let no one demand from us the specific manner and process of this translation. For it is not our part to explain it, nor if it were, could we bring forth anything certain. Yet, lest this treatise have a gap, it is pleasing to provide the pious meditations of distinguished and serious theologians for the fair reader to consider. The Blessed Luther <note>Martin Luther, the primary figure of the Protestant Reformation.</note> (page 79, column 2) says: 

> *Perhaps Enoch, lying in a grassy place and praying, fell asleep; and while sleeping he was snatched up by God, without pain and without death.* 

Truly, Luther judges that this happened secretly from others. To that end look these words of his from page 78, column 1, on the word <term>Veenennu</term> <note>Hebrew: "And he was not."</note>: 

> *“And he was not” signifies that, beyond the hope and expectation of the remaining Patriarchs, Enoch was lost or disappeared, and ceased to be among men. Without doubt, therefore, his father and grandfather were dismayed by the most heavy loss of such a great man. For they knew with what great zeal he had taught religion, and how much he had suffered. When they suddenly lose this man, who held the testimony of piety before God and men, what do you think was in their hearts? Here, then, give me either a poet or a skilled orator to describe this scene with the dignity and emotion it deserves. Enos, Seth, and all the other Patriarchs do not know where Enoch has been snatched. They search for him; his son Methuselah searches for him; other children and grandchildren search for him. They were suspicious of the malice of the Cainites <note>The descendants of Cain, often depicted as the "wicked" branch of humanity before the Great Flood.</note>. And so perhaps they thought that he, like Abel, had been murdered and secretly buried. This continued until finally, through God revealing it via an angel, they learned that he had been snatched by God himself and placed in Paradise. However, they did not know this on the first or second day, but perhaps after many intervening months or years. Meanwhile, those holy men lamented his miserable condition, as if he had been killed by the hypocritical Cainites.*

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion regarding the "translation" (taking up into heaven) of Enoch. The previous page ended with Martin Luther's reflections on the event, and this page moves into the specific circumstances and witnesses of the event, as well as the nature of Enoch's transformed body.</meta>

That Enoch was translated while most of the patriarchs of the pre-flood world were watching—namely Seth, Enos, Cainan, Mahalaleel, his father Jared, his son Methuselah, and his grandson Lamech—is a conjecture based on a comparison of *Jude 14* <note>The text says "Jud. XIII," but this likely refers to the Epistle of Jude, verse 14, which mentions Enoch's prophecy.</note> by the Blessed Leyser, Walther, and others. They suggest this happened on the Sabbath itself, following the established assembly, during the act of sacrifice, when Enoch was carried up in a flame. The Blessed Leyser <note>Polycarp Leyser (1552–1610), a prominent Lutheran theologian.</note> offers a reasoning for this conjecture that is quite fitting, according to the judgment of that most eminent man Doctor Calovius, our venerable Gamaliel <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a leading defender of Lutheran orthodoxy; the author compares him to the famous biblical teacher Gamaliel.</note>:

> *The opinion of those who establish that this was done while the Church was watching seems more probable,* (he says). *For how would Enoch, by his translation, be an example of repentance to the world—by which the wicked might be terrified and those who had fallen away might be stirred to return to their senses—if these things had happened in secret, and no one knew where he had gone? For unless this had happened publicly with many watching—especially his father and son—there would have been a suspicion that he had been killed by the Lamechites or Cainites, just as Abel was. Therefore, God translated him in such a way that the remaining Patriarchs could understand that he was in peace.*

Furthermore, concerning the transformation <note>original Greek: μεταλλαγῇ (*metallagē*)</note> through which they believe that, in this translation, Enoch’s corruptible <note>original Greek: τὸ φθαρτὸν (*to phtharton*)</note> nature put on incorruption <note>original Greek: ἀφθαρσίαν (*aphtharsian*)</note> and his mortal <note>original Greek: τὸ θνητὸν (*to thnēton*)</note> nature put on immortality <note>original Greek: ἀθανασίαν (*athanasian*)</note> (referencing 1 Corinthians 15:50–54), we add nothing further, since these matters must be left to the schools of orthodox <note>original Greek: ὀρθοδοξόντων (*orthodoxontōn*)</note> theologians. Meanwhile, as if in passing <note>original Greek: ὡς ἐν παρόδῳ (*hōs en parodō*)</note>, mention should be made of the Chaldean-Samaritan Paraphrase <note>A translation or "Targum" of the Bible used by the Samaritans.</note>, which expressed the words of the text "God took him" <note>Hebrew: לקחתו אלהים (*lekachto Elohim*)</note> in this way: "The angels took him" <note>Aramaic/Samaritan: נסבתה מלאכיא (*nesabta malachia*)</note>. The illustrious Edmund Castell <note>An English orientalist and lexicographer (1606–1685).</note> testifies that this is also the sentiment of the Arabic-Samaritan translation. We do not dispute the matter itself; we admit that Enoch was surrounded by angels when he was translated. However, because this interpreter substitutes an "angel" for "GOD" here, as in many other places, he rightly incurs the criticism of the illustrious Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian.</note> in his *Dissertation on the Paris Heptapla* <note>The "Heptapla" refers to a polyglot Bible featuring seven versions or languages.</note>.

§. 9. Furthermore, our text does not expressly mention the *destination* <term>terminus ad quem</term> <note>Latin: "the end point to which" something moves.</note> of the translation. However, it is clear from the analogy of Elijah’s translation that it is the "heaven of the blessed." Meanwhile, the Jews and the Papists <note>A period-typical, often polemical term for Roman Catholics.</note> imagine that Enoch was snatched away into an *earthly paradise*. Among the former, Menasse ben Israel <note>A famous Portuguese rabbi and author (1604–1657) based in Amsterdam.</note> writes explicitly in his second book *On the Resurrection of the Dead*, chapter 3, page 136, concerning Elijah (to whom he joins Enoch as an equal at the end of the chapter): *He was translated whole, with body and soul, into the earthly paradise.* He then proves this from a letter written and sent (as he imagines, at least) after the transportation. From this, he adds, it can be gathered that *he is in a physical place and human affairs—* <note>The text cuts off here, leading to the next page.</note>

furthermore, that these matters were his concern. And he saw Adam's place in Paradise, in which, if Adam had observed that precept, he would have lived perpetually and remained for eternity. But because he did not obey the command of his Lord, he was therefore cast out to his own loss and punished.</gloss> <note>original Hebrew: "אשכחנא בספרא דחנוך..."</note>

The Papists <note>A common early modern Protestant term for Roman Catholics.</note>, however, citing Sirach 44, say that Enoch was translated into "paradise" (though this word does not appear in the Greek text), and they similarly do not understand this to mean the heavenly paradise. Thus Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a highly influential Jesuit Cardinal and a central figure in the Counter-Reformation.</note> in *On the Grace of the First Man*, Book 1, chapter 12, page 17, writes: "It is certain that Enoch did not ascend into the heavenly Paradise; for no one descends from there to offer repentance to the nations." Whom, then, do they want him to be? Suarez <note>Francisco Suárez (1548–1617), a prominent Spanish Jesuit priest and philosopher.</note>, along with others, places Enoch in some hidden region of the earth in his commentary on Thomas [Aquinas], Part 3, question 59. Caspar Sanctius, commenting on 2 Kings 11, says: "If paradise exists now"—and Bellarmine in the cited work, chapter 14, contends that it does; Lorinus, in his *Commentary on the Epistle of Jude*, says this is the more common opinion in the Jesuit schools; while Eugubinus, Jansenius, Hieronymus ab Oleastro, Vatablus, Jacobus Naclantus, Maldonatus, Catharinus, Genebrardus, Bl. Viegas, Pererius, Cornelius à Lapide, and others deny that it exists—"which some believe to be true, and perhaps not falsely, then I would not doubt that Enoch stays there now, nor would anyone else, unless they were quite bold in their opinions." But what happened to Enoch during the Great Flood? The evasions of the Papists are clearly ridiculous, betraying a bad cause by their very variety; some...

<meta>This page continues the theological debate regarding the physical location of Enoch and Elijah. ...saying that the waters of the flood, which surpassed the whole world and the highest mountains under heaven, nevertheless did not reach Paradise. Others imagine that same Paradise to be twenty cubits <note>An ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches.</note> higher than all the highest mountains, so that it would stand out at least five cubits above the floodwaters. Others labor to keep Enoch safe during the flood by holding him suspended in the air above the waters for the entire duration of the deluge; or even covering him with the waters in a vaulted chamber, like a series of arches; or casting him into some corner of the Ark where he might hide during the whole time of the flood. Finally, others lift Paradise up into the heights as far as the sphere of the Moon. 

When Cornelius à Lapide <note>A renowned Jesuit priest and biblical exegete (1567–1637) whose commentaries were widely used.</note> asked *where Enoch and Elijah are now and what kind of life they lead? He responds that the Church Fathers say they live in paradise.* "But I say," he adds, *"that Enoch was translated <note>In this context, 'translation' refers to being taken up to God without dying.</note> into Paradise before the flood; but after the flood, by which Paradise seems to have been inundated and removed, he lives in some pleasant place which God prepared for them either in the air or on the earth. Elijah was snatched away to this same place after the flood; there, they lead a life together that is, as it were, blessed,"* etc. 

But where are we drifting? We are pleased by the critique of that most sharp-witted theologian, Blessed Johann Georg Dorsche <note>A 17th-century Lutheran theologian known for his rigorous academic works.</note> in his *Fifteen Dissertations, Dissertation I: On the Prophecy of Enoch, paragraph 18, page 570.* "That opinion," he says, "concerning the removal of Enoch into an earthly Paradise rests on an uncertain foundation regarding the existence of an earthly paradise, has no testimony in the Holy Scriptures, is burdened with contradictions, and fosters the error concerning the return and killing of Enoch expected in Revelation 11:7 <note>Referring to the 'Two Witnesses' in the Book of Revelation, whom many historical commentators identified as Enoch and Elijah returning to earth to be martyred.</note>—which is contrary to the text of Paul in Hebrews 11:5." For the *translation* <note>original: μετάθεσις (metathesis)</note> of Enoch was performed there *so as not to see death* <note>original: τοῦ μὴ ἰδεῖν θάνατον (tou mē idein thanaton)</note>. The Fathers explode that idea, etc. And on page 569, paragraph 17, at the end: "Certainly, if Enoch is to be detained in some earthly paradise until the end of the world, that singular privilege of a miraculous translation, by which God wished to honor him above the lot of other mortals, is not a privilege at all, but a most troublesome detention and a seclusion from those good things which he would have already entered into possession of, had he not been translated."

§. 10. Just as elsewhere many absurdities follow once one is granted, so among the Jews as well as the Roman Catholics <note>original: 'Pontificios'</note>, many vain little questions arise from these flimsy hypotheses (concerning the mortal state of Enoch and his placement in an earthly location) regarding the actions of Enoch, which I might as well review...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical state and survival of Enoch and Elijah. Following the previous page's examination of where these figures might be hidden—whether in a high mountain paradise, a vaulted chamber, or even the lunar sphere—the author now turns to the mundane details of their existence according to Jesuit theologians, before transitioning to a new chapter on the books attributed to Enoch.</meta>

...or to examine these matters now is of no concern. What the Jews imagine about Elijah visiting Jewish schools <note>original: "Scholas Judaicas"</note> from time to time is commonly known among the people. Take, if you please, reader, at least one or two specimens of Jesuit chatter concerning the food and clothing of Enoch. *Sanchez <note>Gaspar Sánchez (1554–1628), a Spanish Jesuit commentator.</note> says in the place cited that it is not improbable that food was brought by the ministry of angels or by some other miraculous way, by which he sustained his life and strength for so long.* But, he adds, *it seems much more likely to me that in that state Enoch uses no human food, but being unfed, he miraculously preserves a vigorous and robust life for so long.* So also *Suarez* <note>Francisco Suárez (1548–1617), a highly influential Jesuit philosopher and theologian.</note> in Volume 2 on the third part of Thomas Aquinas, discussion 55, section 1. *Concerning clothing,* says Sanchez, *and other necessary supports and the comforts of nature, all things are uncertain, such as whether they cut their hair, trim their nails, and similar things. On this matter, Tostado <note>Alonso Tostado (c. 1400–1455), also known as Abulensis, a prolific Spanish scholar and bishop.</note> writes at length in question 24; indeed, unless he goes naked in Paradise like Adam, who was not ashamed of his nakedness in that better state, one might not foolishly think that those garments he took with him when he was pulled away from the company of men into Paradise are now preserved entirely whole, and that his nails and hair have not grown, nor has anything else occurred that could be a nuisance to them.* To recount such stories, or their like, is to refute them.

## CHAPTER FOUR.
# CONCERNING THE WRITINGS OF ENOCH.

> *1. Various writings are attributed to Enoch. First, Mathematical. The Arabs themselves attribute thirty books to him. They record that the first was Sardor: that the Book of Enoch was found in the chest of Adam. 2. Do the Abyssinians <note>The people of Ethiopia; the Ethiopian Orthodox Church is one of the few that includes the Book of Enoch in its biblical canon.</note> possess the Prophecy of Enoch? 3. Or the Jews? This is certain to Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a Flemish Protestant scholar of the Old Testament.</note>. 4. The Church Fathers cite an Enochian writing. Boulduc <note>Jacques Boulduc (c. 1580–1644), a Capuchin monk who wrote on ancient history and theology.</note> says it was preserved in the Ark. 5. A fragment of the Book of Enoch concerning the Watchers <note>original: "Egregoris". These are the angelic beings mentioned in the apocryphal Book of Enoch who descended to Earth.</note>. Its argument. 6. The worthlessness of that same work is shown. 7. It proceeded from a trifling Jew. And perhaps from a Hellenist <note>A Jew living in a Greek-speaking culture, influenced by Greek philosophy.</note>? Disagreement with Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss orientalist and theologian.</note>. Agreement with Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a French religious scholar famous for his work on ancient chronology.</note>. 8. From where the Prophecy of Enoch became known to Jude <note>Referring to the Epistle of Jude in the New Testament, which quotes the Book of Enoch.</note>. He did not cite a fragment? Nor did he have it from ancestral tradition. But from revelation.*

### §. I.

**E**NOCH, as Claudianus Marius Victor <note>A 5th-century Gaulish rhetorician and poet who wrote a hexameter paraphrase of Genesis.</note> sings in the second book of his *Poem on Genesis*,

> *He lives still, and already possesses the kingdoms of the life to come.* <note>original: "Vivit adhuc & habet vitæ jam regna futuræ."</note>

There have been those, however, who perhaps did not think it enough for him to simply live, and so they strove to secure a life for him through writings as well. Naturally, in order to grant credibility to their own fables, they preferred to attribute them to one whom they knew was least suspected of fabulizing. Thus, men of no account are accustomed to seek noble parents for the monstrous offspring of their own intellect; nor, even if the true parents are known, is the effect immediately traced back to the cause. Furthermore, such various things are reported concerning the scholarly labors of Enoch that we are hardly able to recount them all. First, they attribute *Philosophical* writings to him, especially those concerning *Mathematics*, a matter on which we brought forward several points from Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), the "Father of Church History."</note> in the preceding chapter. Likewise, Origen <note>A prolific early Christian scholar and theologian (c. 184–253 AD).</note> in his *28th Homily on Numbers* cites the books of Enoch, in which he says were contained *secrets regarding the names not only of the regions of heaven, but also of all the stars and constellations.* <note>original: "secreta de nominibus non solùm plagarum cœli, sed etiam omnium stellarum atq; siderum."</note> From the Hebrews, Rabbi Shem Tov <note>Shem Tov ibn Shem Tov (c. 1380–1441), a Spanish Jewish philosopher and critic of Maimonides.</note> in the *Book of Beliefs* <note>original: "ספר אמונות" (Sefer Emunot), Port 3, chapter 4.</note> produces a book whose author he identifies as Enoch, which discusses the movements of the heavens and stars and the science of magic. Similarly, the author of the *Book of Genealogies* <note>original: "Juchasin" (Sefer ha-Yuhasin), a 15th-century historical work by Abraham Zacuto.</note> on page 143 says that *he began to compose books on astronomy.* <note>original: "התחיל לחבר ספרים בתכונה" (hitchil le-chaber sefarim bi-tkhunah).</note> It is also pleasing to survey the comments of certain Arabs, with Athanasius Kircher <note>A 17th-century Jesuit polymath and "master of a hundred arts" who attempted to decode Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> leading the way in his *Egyptian Oedipus*, Volume 1, Treatise 1, chapter 8. From *Abenephius* <note>Likely Ibn Wahshiyya, an Arabic author whose works on "Nabatean Agriculture" and ancient scripts were used by Kircher.</note> he cites these words on page 67:

> *And Idris himself <note>The Quranic name for Enoch.</note>—who is Enoch to the Hebrews, and Osiris and Hermes to the Egyptians—was the first before the deluge to profess Astrology and Geometry; he was the first who discoursed on sciences of this kind, and who brought them from potentiality into action. He also wrote a book in which he marked down their doctrine. Then he set out for Ethiopia and Nubia and other provinces, and having called the people together, he taught them various things, and was the first who diverted the Nile into Egypt.* There is another tradition of the Arabs that thirty books were sent down to Enoch. So says Al-Baydawi <note>A 13th-century Persian scholar and Quranic commentator.</note> in his Commentary on the Sura of Mary. Kircher brings forward Ka'b al-Ahbar <note>A 7th-century Yemenite Jew who converted to Islam and is a major source of Judeo-Christian lore in Islamic tradition.</note> to the same effect, who even establishes Enoch as the leader of the tailors. The words of the author, as translated by Kircher, are these: *But Idris, peace be upon him, was similar in form to Seth, and he was the first who after*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Enoch’s literary legacy, moving from his reputation for astronomical knowledge mentioned on the previous page to his identification with the Islamic prophet Idris (Edris) and the search for his lost prophecies in Ethiopic and Jewish traditions.</meta>

Seth taught the art of writing to the Prophet, and he was Edris <note>The Islamic name for the prophet Enoch, often identified in the early modern period with the figure of Hermes Trismegistus.</note> who, accustomed to participating in religious worship until he reached a more mature age, at which point he surpassed and went far beyond all men of his age in religious devotion. God therefore appointed him a Prophet and sent down thirty books to him; he also received the books of Seth by inheritance, as well as the ark containing the corpse of Adam—peace be upon him. He lived by the labor of his hands, for Edris was a tailor, and the first who sewed garments; with every stitch of the needle, he praised God and sanctified Him. Furthermore, Kessæus <note>Al-Kisa'i, an 11th-century author of a "Lives of the Prophets" (Qiṣaṣ al-Anbiyāʾ).</note> in his *Lives of the Fathers* (as reported by Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss orientalist and theologian.</note>) testifies that the Sabaeans <note>A religious group mentioned in the Quran, often associated by scholars of this era with ancient star-worship.</note> boast that they are the heirs and possessors of the books of Edris. He also tells the tale that Abraham, when he opened the chest of Adam, found the codex of Edris among other books. But we leave these fables to their authors.

**§. 2.** Because the Apostle Jude called Enoch a prophet, most people believed that a prophecy had been written by him; many also attempted to produce it. Gassendi <note>Pierre Gassendi (1592–1655), a French philosopher and scientist.</note>, in his *Life of Peiresc* <note>Nicolas-Claude Fabri de Peiresc (1580–1637), a famous French scholar and book collector.</note> (p. 395), mentions a noteworthy sight seen by Giles of Loches <note>Aegidius Lochiensis, a Capuchin friar and traveler who studied in Egypt.</note>, a Capuchin who spent seven years in Egypt studying Oriental literature. He saw a library of eight thousand volumes, many of which bore signs of the Antonine age <note>The period of the Roman "Five Good Emperors" in the 2nd century AD.</note>. Among other things, he said he noticed the *Mashapha Einok* <note>original: "Mashapha Einok"; a transcription of the Ethiopic *Metsahaf Henok*, or the Book of Enoch.</note> or the *Prophecy of Enoch*, which declares those things that will happen until the end of the age. This book had long been unseen in Europe, but was written in the Ethiopic character and idiom (of the Abyssinians) among whom it had been preserved. Peiresc was so inflamed with the desire to acquire it at any price that, sparing no expense, he finally made it his own property. However, the most illustrious Hottinger, in his *Heidelberg Dissertations* (Ennead 1, p. 3), demonstrated that this writing belongs to a more recent age.

**§. 3.** There is another widespread belief that a *Prophecy of Enoch* existed in the past among the Jews, or that it still lies hidden among them—or at least a book sold under that name. Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a Flemish Protestant scholar of Hebrew and the Bible.</note> was more certain of this book of Enoch than of Enoch himself. Thus he says in his *Enoch* (chapter 22, page 27): "Menachem of Recanati <note>Menachem ben Benjamin Recanati (c. 1250–1310), an influential Italian Kabbalist.</note> does not allow me to doubt that this book still exists today among the Jews, and..."

It focuses on the efforts of scholars like Peiresc and Hottinger to locate and authenticate these ancient prophetic texts.</summary>

...as he is praised several times, such as on folio 30, columns 1 and 2. This is partly due to the Kabbalah <note>The Jewish mystical tradition.</note> of the later sages, who wrote that they had found the book of Enoch, son of Jared, etc. We previously gave a passage from the *Zohar* <note>The foundational text of Jewish mysticism.</note> taken from Menasseh ben Israel’s <note>A prominent Portuguese rabbi and diplomat based in Amsterdam (1604–1657).</note> *On the Resurrection*, book 3, page 335. Perhaps that same workshop produced this Book of Enoch, which Georg Rostius <note>A 17th-century theologian and commentator.</note> (in his notes on the *Epistle of Jude*, page 133) records was found by Guillaume Postel <note>A French Renaissance polymath and linguist (1510–1581) known for his interest in universal religion.</note>, and which "Capnion" <note>The Greek-derived pseudonym of Johann Reuchlin (1455–1522), a pioneering German Hebraist.</note> is said to have purchased from Pico della Mirandola <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), the famous Italian Renaissance philosopher who integrated Kabbalah into Christian thought.</note> for a large sum of money.

**§. 4.** Whatever the truth of these matters may be, Tertullian <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD).</note> leaves us in no doubt that a book once circulated under the name of Enoch, especially in his *Apology* (chapter 22), *On Idolatry* (chapter 4), *On Female Dress* (chapter 3), *On the Ornaments of Women*, etc. He did not hesitate to use the authority of this "Enochian" book several times, just as other Church Fathers were accustomed to call upon the testimonies of the same book. A "cloud of witnesses" <note>A biblical idiom for a large group of people testifying to a truth.</note> is provided by Jacob Pamelius <note>A Flemish theologian (1536–1587).</note> in his notes on Tertullian’s *On Female Dress*, and by Jacques Bolduc <note>A French Capuchin scholar (died 1644).</note> in his *On the Church Before Moses*, book 1, chapter 12, page 126 and following. This last author boldly declares on page 134 that Noah diligently preserved and placed the writings of Enoch in the ark at the time of the flood with no less care than he gave to the bones of Father Adam and certain other Patriarchs. He argues that since Noah could not have the bones of Enoch (whom God had snatched away) to keep, he instead guarded the writings as a most sacred pledge and deposit for himself and his descendants. Clearly, the survival of Enoch’s writings is considered as certain a matter as the survival of the others’ bones! For this occurred to the mind of the illustrious Petrus Cunaeus <note>A Dutch scholar (1586–1638) famous for his work on the Hebrew state.</note>, who says of the book of Enoch in his *On the Hebrew Republic*, book 3, chapter 1:

> Truly, this book either emerged intact from the waves of the flood when almost everything else perished, or it later fell from heaven—which is an old wives' tale.

**§. 5.** From that celebrated book of Enoch, there is even today a "Snippet" <note>original Greek: Ἀποσπασμάτιον (*apospasmation*)</note> or Fragment circulating "from the first book of Enoch concerning the Watchers (that is, the Angels)" <note>original Greek: ἐκ τοῦ πρώτου βιβλίου Ἐνὼχ περὶ τῶν Ἐγρηγόρων (*ek tou prōtou bibliou Enōch peri tōn egrēgorōn*)</note>. Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a foundational figure in modern historical chronology.</note> inserted this in Greek in his *Observations on the Chronicle of Eusebius*, page 244. After him, Athanasius Kircher <note>A Jesuit polymath (1602–1680) known for his vast, if sometimes eccentric, scholarship on Egypt and science.</note> reviewed it in Greek and Latin in his *Egyptian Oedipus*, volume 2, part 1, class 2, chapter 2, from page 69 onwards. Thomas Bang <note>A Danish theologian and Hebraist (1600–1661).</note> transcribed the Latin translation of it in his *Heaven of the Orient*, exercise 2, page 25 and following. The gist of this fragment is: that "Semixas" <note>Semyaza, a leader of the fallen angels in Enochic literature.</note> was the prince of the Watchers (by whom angels are implied, as Scaliger and Kircher show, not the descen[dants of Cain]...

*Seth taught the art of writing to the Prophet* <note>The text here identifies Seth (son of Adam) as the teacher of Enoch/Idris.</note>, *and Edris* <note>Idris is the Islamic name for the prophet Enoch.</note> *was also accustomed to participate in religious worship until he reached a more mature age, at which point he surpassed and far preceded all men of his age in the practice of religion; therefore God appointed him as a Prophet and sent down thirty books upon him. He also received the books of Seth as an inheritance, as well as the ark* <note>Or coffin.</note> *containing the body of Adam (peace be upon him). He lived by the labor of his hands, for Edris was a tailor and the first who sewed garments; with every stitch of the needle, he praised God and sanctified Him. Furthermore, Al-Kisa'i* <note>Muhammad al-Kisa'i, an 11th-century author of the *Stories of the Prophets*.</note> *testifies in his "Lives of the Fathers" (as reported by Hottinger* <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss scholar of Oriental languages.</note>*) that the Sabaeans boast of being the heirs and possessors of the books of Edris. He even tells the story that Abraham, when he opened the chest of Adam, found among other books the volume of Edris. But we leave these fables to their own authors.*

**§. 2.** Because the Apostle *Jude* stated that *Enoch* had prophesied <note>Referencing Jude 1:14-15 in the New Testament.</note>, most people believed that a prophecy had been written by him, and many have attempted to bring it forward. *Gassendi* <note>Pierre Gassendi (1592–1655), a French philosopher and scientist.</note>, in his "Life of Peiresc" <note>Nicolas-Claude Fabri de Peiresc (1580–1637), a famous French collector and scholar.</note> (page 395), cites the *Abyssinians* <note>Ethiopians.</note>: It is a memorable thing, he says, that *Giles of Loches* (a *Capuchin* friar who spent seven whole years in *Egypt* studying Oriental letters) saw a library of eight thousand volumes, a significant part of which bore the marks of the *Antonine* age <note>The 2nd century AD.</note>. Since he claimed to have noticed among other things the *Mashapha Einok* <note>original: "Mashapha Einok"; the Ge'ez (Ethiopic) term for the "Book of Enoch".</note> or *Prophecy of Enoch*, which explains those things that are to happen until the end of the world—a book long unseen in *Europe*—and since it was written in the *Ethiopic* or *Abyssinian* character and language among whom it had been preserved, *Peiresc* was so inflamed with a desire to acquire it at any price that, sparing no expense, he finally made it his own property. However, the most distinguished *Hottinger* demonstrated that this writing belongs to a more recent age in his *Heidelberg Dissertations* (part 1, pages 3–4).

**§. 3.** Otherwise, there is a widespread belief that the *Prophecy of Enoch* once existed among the *Jews*, or that it still lies hidden among them, or certainly that a book has been sold under that name. *Drusius* <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a Flemish Protestant Hebraist.</note> was certainly more sure of this book of *Enoch* than of *Enoch* himself. He says in his "Enoch" (chapter 22, page 27): *Menachem of Recanati* <note>Menachem Recanati (1250–1310), an influential Italian Kabbalist.</note> does not allow me to doubt that this book still exists today among the *Jews*, who...

<summary>This page discusses the Islamic traditions identifying Enoch as the inventor of tailoring and writing, and recounts 17th-century efforts by European scholars like Peiresc to recover the lost "Book of Enoch" from Ethiopia.</summary>
<keywords>Seth, Idris, Al-Kisa'i, Hottinger, Jude, Gassendi, Peiresc, Ethiopia, Mashapha Einok, Drusius, Menachem of Recanati</keywords>

...when he praises it several times, as on folio 30, columns 1 and 2. This is partly from the later sages of the <term>Kabbalah<note>A Jewish mystical tradition. The author is referring to later medieval or early modern Jewish scholars who cited "Enochian" texts.</note></term> who wrote that they had found it in the book of Enoch, son of Jared, etc. We have already provided a passage from the *Zohar* <note>original: "E Sohar locum suprà dedimus"</note> above, taken from Menasseh ben Israel's *On the Resurrection*, Book 3, page 335. Perhaps that same workshop produced this book of Enoch, which Georg Rostius (in his commentary on the *Epistle of Jude*, page 133) records was discovered by Guillaume Postel <note>Guillaume Postel (1510–1581) was a French linguist and mystic who claimed to have found the Book of Enoch in Ethiopia.</note>, and which Capnio <note>The scholar Johann Reuchlin (1455–1522), often called Capnio, was a pioneer of Hebrew studies in the Renaissance.</note> is said to have bought from Pico della Mirandola for a large sum of money.

**§. 4.** Whatever the truth of these matters may be, <term>Tertullian<note>An early Christian author (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) who is famous for regarding the Book of Enoch as scripture.</note></term> leaves us in no doubt that a book once circulated under the name of Enoch, especially in his *Apology* (chapter 22), *On Idolatry* (chapter 4), *On the Apparel of Women* (chapter 3), and *On the Cult of Women*, etc. He did not hesitate to use the authority of this Enochian book several times, just as other Church Fathers were accustomed to call upon the testimonies of the same book to their side. A "cloud of witnesses" <note>A biblical idiom for a large group of people testifying to something.</note> is provided by Jacques Pamelius in his *Notes on Tertullian’s 'On the Apparel of Women'* and by Jacques Bolduc in Book 1 of *The Church before Moses*, chapter 12, page 126. This latter author, on page 134, boldly pronounces: *that Noah diligently preserved and stored the writings of Enoch in the ark during the flood with no less concern than he did the bones of Father Adam and certain other Patriarchs; since he could not have the bones of the one whom God had taken up* <note>Referring to the biblical tradition that Enoch did not die but was taken by God.</note> *to be preserved, he guarded his writings for himself and posterity as a most sacred pledge and deposit.* It seems the matter of Enoch’s preserved writings is considered as certain as that of the bones of the others! For this came to the mind of the illustrious Peter Cunaeus, who says regarding the book of Enoch in Book 3 of *The Hebrew Republic*, chapter 1: *Truly, either that book escaped intact from the waves of the flood when almost everything else perished, or it later fell down from heaven—which is an old wives' tale.*

**§. 5.** From that celebrated book of Enoch, a certain *Excerpt* or *Fragment* <note>original: "Ἀποσπασμάτιον s. Joseph Scaliger <note>A premier Renaissance scholar of history and chronology.</note> inserted this in Greek in his *Observations on the Chronicle of Eusebius*, page 244; after him, Athanasius Kircher <note>A famous Jesuit polymath known for his studies on Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> reviewed it in Greek and Latin in his *Egyptian Oedipus*, Volume 2, Part 1, Class 2, chapter 2, from page 69; and subsequently, Thomas Bangius described a Latin translation of it in *The Heavens of the East*, Exercise 2, page 25. The essence of that fragment is: *Semyaza, the leader of the Watchers (by whom angels are implied, as Scaliger and Kircher show, not the Ca-*

In this context, it refers to the knowledge of potions, herbalism, and sorcery rather than modern medicine.</note></term>, the art of forging all kinds of war instruments, mining metals, polishing gold, embroidery, the art of adorning women's finery, and the cutting of precious stones, by which means men went forth and were deceived; another taught the art of singing, star-watching, astrology, sky-watching, divination by earth, and moon-watching, and thus revealed the mysteries of heaven to women. The *giants* began to devour the flesh of men, while men became proud and acted savagely upon the earth; the remaining wretched mortals groaned and cried out to heaven. The four Archangels *Gabriel*, *Raphael*, *Michael*, and *Uriel* saw this, and complained before <term>God</term> regarding *Exael* <note>A variant of Azazel, the demon of the wilderness.</note>, because of the mysteries of heaven he had revealed. God immediately sent *Uriel* to *Noah* and predicted the flood; *Raphael* against *Exael*, to cast him bound into the desert of *Dodoël*; *Gabriel* against the giants, to arm them against each other’s own vitals <note>That is, to incite them to civil war and mutual destruction.</note>; and *Michael* against *Semixa*, so that he might be hurled down, bound, into the depths of the earth. To these accounts of the giants, the "human-demons" <note>original: "Anthropodæmonibus"</note>, certain absurdities are added.

§. 6. The fragility and futility of this fragment has been most extensively shown by *Thomas Bang* <note>Thomas Bang (1600–1661) was a Danish theologian and Hebraist who wrote "Caelum Orientis" to debunk magical and pseudepigraphal claims.</note> in the work cited, from pages 30 to 93, so that it seems unnecessary to re-weave the same web. In just a few words—since the present material grows under our hands—we will indicate why that fragment is worthy neither of *Enoch* nor of any Divine Man, because it clearly contains many things contrary to Scripture and reason.
1. It attributes "intercourse with women" <note>original Greek: "γυναικομιξίαν"</note> to angels, which it claims was the cause of the flood in Genesis 6. But this must be understood as referring either to good angels or to evil ones. The former are strangers to such a shared life (Matthew 22:30). The latter are nowhere called "sons of God" <note>original Hebrew: "בני אלהים" (B'nai Elohim)</note> but are rather distinguished from them in Job 1.

<meta>This page continues the critique of the "Book of Enoch" fragment, focusing on theological and linguistic inconsistencies between the apocryphal text and the Holy Scriptures.</meta>

In the Book of Enoch, these are the angels who descended to earth to father children with humans.</note></term> mortal; he imagines the Watchers themselves bound in hidden places of the earth, and he supposes that Exael <note>A variant of the name Azazel.</note> was cast into the desert to be tortured with sharp and jagged rocks placed beneath him, and so forth.
2. He makes the Giants into <term>man-demons<note>original Greek: "Ανθρωποδαίμονας" (Anthropodaimonas), suggesting a hybrid nature part-human and part-spirit.</note></term>, born indeed partly from men and partly from the Watchers. He calls these same beings "evil spirits" who plague men with phantoms; he imagines that they eat nothing, yet they still suffer thirst, and so forth.
3. He attributes the invention of the arts to angels, whereas Sacred Scripture assigns this to men in *Genesis 4:20 and following*. And yet the Archangels complain before God: *Exael taught mysteries and revealed to the world the things that are in heaven.* For Exael was imagined to have taught men <note>original Greek: "ποιεῖν μαχαίρας καὶ θώρακας καὶ πᾶν σκεῦος πολεμικόν, καὶ τὰ μέταλλα τῆς γῆς, καὶ τὸ χρυσίον πῶς ἐργάσωνται, καὶ ποιήσωσιν αὐτὰ κόσμια ταῖς γυναιξὶν, καὶ τὸν ἄργυρον"</note> **to make swords and breastplates and every instrument of war, and the metals of the earth, and how they should work gold and make ornaments for women, and silver.** These, evidently, are the exercises of heaven!
4. Concerning Mount Hermon, the place of their conspiracy, he imagines that *cold, snow, and frost shall never depart from there, and dew shall never descend upon it*; even though the Holy Psalmist himself speaks of the dew of Mount Hermon in *Psalm 133, verse 3*. We shall add no more; it will be easy for anyone <note>original Greek: "ἐκ τοῦ κράσπέδου τὸ ὕφασμα γινώσκειν"</note> **to judge the whole cloth by its hem.** <note>A Greek proverb meaning one can judge the quality of the whole work by a small sample.</note>

§ 7. Furthermore, Bangius <note>Thomas Bang (1600–1661), a Danish theologian and scholar of Hebrew.</note> proved that the writing itself is the offspring of a certain "superstitious Apella" <note>A literary reference to Horace’s "Credat Judaeus Apella," used here to suggest the author was a credulous or superstitious person.</note> from the fact that the fables crowded into it agree exactly with Jewish legends—such as those concerning Shemihazai and Azazel <note>original Hebrew: "שמחזי & עזאל"</note>, which the author of the *Tze'enah u-Re'enah* <note>original: "Zennorenna." A popular Yiddish commentary on the Torah for laypeople, first published in the early 17th century.</note> narrates at length on page 102—and thus its weak hypotheses agree with the established opinions of those words. However, Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist.</note> concludes in his *Ennead*, page 9, among other points, that this person was not an accurate Hebraist, but a **Hellenist** <note>A Greek-speaking Jew or a person influenced by Greek culture.</note> ignorant of Hebrew. He bases this on a wordplay: "Pharmaros taught sorceries" <note>original Greek: "Φαρμαρὸς ἐδίδαξε Φαρμακείας" (Pharmaros edidaxe pharmakeias). The pun relies on the phonetic similarity between the name and the art.</note>, just as in the history of *Susanna* there is the pun <note>original Greek: "ἀπὸ τοῦ σχίνου σχίσαι καὶ ἀπὸ τῆς πρίνου πρίσαι"</note> **"to split from the mastic tree"** and **"to saw from the holm-oak."** <note>This refers to a famous Greek pun in the story of Susanna and the Elders, which many scholars used to argue the text was originally written in Greek rather than Hebrew.</note> But we concede to Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a foundational figure in modern chronology and philology.</note> in the place cited, page 245b, that the writing was translated from Hebrew, whether the original author was a Hebraist or a semi-Hebraist. For anyone moderately skilled in both idioms, Hebrew and Greek, will see that the style is *interpretive*. Nor does that wordplay stand in the way: for although *Pharmakos* <note>original Hebrew: "פרמקוס." The Greek word for "sorcerer" or "poisoner" adopted into Hebrew script.</note> is a word of Greek origin, it was nevertheless granted citizenship by the Rabbis and occurs in the *Genesis Rabbah, section 77* <note>original: "Bereschit rabba." A collection of ancient Jewish homilies on the Book of Genesis.</note>. From this, some Hebrew author could have produced the same wordplay, though he was foolish in this: that he attributed a word of Greek origin to Enoch, in whose age the Greek language did not exist.

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of the "Book of Enoch" by examining the specific citation in the Epistle of Jude. It transitions from critiquing the fragment's content to debating the source of the Apostle's knowledge.</meta>

§. 8. But what, finally, shall we say regarding the *Prophecy of Enoch*, of which Jude makes explicit mention? Three possibilities can be considered. 1. That the Apostle had seen a certain book then in existence, either originating from Enoch or published by someone else under his name. 2. That Enoch's prophecy was handed down through <term>tradition<note>original Greek: "παράδοσιν" (paradosin). This refers to the passing down of oral history or teachings through successive generations.</note></term> until the time of the Apostle. 3. That the Apostle received Enoch's words through a singular divine revelation. The first view is adopted by Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a foundational figure in modern chronology and historical criticism.</note>, among others, who believes the Apostle cited the "Fragment concerning the Watchers" (page 244. a.). Gregorius Primaticcius holds the same in his *Commentary on the Epistle of Jude, page 167.* Truly, without summoning more arguments here, the words cited by the Apostle do not actually appear in the "Fragment concerning the Watchers." The second view is favored by Michael Medina in *On True Faith in God, book 6, chapter 24*, by Johannes Maria Scribonius in *Sermon 17 on the Epistle of Jude, page 259*, and others. These authors argue that this prophecy was presented as something well-known and famous among those to whom Jude wrote—though such a conclusion cannot actually be gathered from the Apostle's words. We simply rest on the final view. Rightly does the <term>distinguished Bangius<note>Thomas Bang (1600–1661), a Danish theologian known for his works on the origins of language and sacred texts.</note></term> state on page 94: *"Jude, a man led by the Spirit of GOD, had no need to resort to <term>antediluvian<note>Meaning "before the Flood."</note></term> writings or traditions, since he enjoyed an extraordinary and spontaneous readiness in writing and speaking, as well as the <term>remembrance and teaching<note>original Greek: "ὑπομνήσει καὶ διδάξει" (hypomnēsei kai didaxei). This refers to the internal guidance of the Holy Spirit bringing truths to mind.</note></term> of the Holy Spirit Himself."* Furthermore, from the Apostolic citation of Enoch's prophecy, no one could easily conclude that Enoch committed that prophecy of his to writing. The Apostle does not say: "<term>he wrote<note>original Greek: "ἔγραψε" (egrapse).</note></term>," but "<term>he prophesied<note>original Greek: "προεφήτευσε" (proephēteuse).</note></term>;" nor does he introduce Enoch as "<term>writing<note>original Greek: "γράφονta" (graphonta).</note></term>," but as "<term>speaking<note>original Greek: "λέγοντα" (legonta).</note></term>." In the same way, Peter relates that Noah was a <term>herald of righteousness<note>original Greek: "κήρυκα τῆς δικαιοσύνης" (kēryka tēs dikaiosynēs); see 2 Peter 2:5.</note></term>. Yet it does not immediately follow from this that Noah published volumes of sermons. Therefore, from the fact that Enoch is read to have prophesied, it cannot be inferred that he wrote down the prophecy. Let learned and wise re-
